PDP64 DECISIONS AND STATUS: FEEDBACK FORM

This file is generated by tools/feedback/mkfeedback.py from the open items (Appendix J), the
proposals (Appendix H), the confirmation list and the status ledger. Re-run the generator
after any chapter edit; answer by typing on the DECISION and NOTE lines only.

HOW TO ANSWER (read in the pager, edit, write the file)

  Open items (J.*): read the issue and the lettered options; on the line 'DECISION:' type a
    letter, several letters ('A, C'), 'other: your text', or 'defer'. Put reasons or your
    own wording on the 'NOTE:' line (it may continue on indented lines below it).
  Proposals (H.*): each is a statement the assistant proposed. Type A (accept), B (reject)
    or C (accept with changes; write the changes in NOTE), or 'defer'.
  Confirmations (V.*): 'Is this right?' Type A (yes) or B (no, with the change in NOTE).
  Ledger subjects (S.*) and standing decisions (W.*): type a letter, 'other: ...' or
    'defer'; details of a decision go in NOTE. Lines 'Leaning (assistant)' are not decisions.
  New questions and decisions of your own: the last section, free text.
  Entries are in ORDER: those with 'AFFECTS OPCODES: yes' come first, then 'maybe', then 'no',
  because a decision there can change opcodes, mnemonics or the encoding. Cited rows 'R<n>'
  are rows of the decision register (docs/decision-register.md). Notes made with the pager's
  annotation keys are read back as well, by line.

================================================================================================
INDEX BY AFFECTS OPCODES
================================================================================================

ENTRY J.9.13
ORDER: 1   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: Range checks on a register value: trap, sticky flag, clamp, mask or nothing
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (range-check instructions (compare-and-trap, two-bound check, sticky
   range flag, clamp))
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A range check (or bounds check) tests that a value lies in an interval: an array index
   i in 0 <= i < n, a length that fits a buffer, a result that fits a field. Pascal, Ada, Java,
   Rust, Go and Swift insert one on every checked subscript; the Linux kernel has
   array_index_nospec and the bounds sanitizer of its own tests. A check is used in one of three
   ways, and they want different machinery. A guard stops the program before a bad value is used
   as an address, a length or a divisor: the memory-safe use. [...]  (Full text: Appendix J,
   J.9.13.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Memory safety by bounds check is the commonest security check in compiled
   code, and each language that makes it the default puts it in front of every access. Its price
   in instructions, bytes and speed is paid at every subscript, so a design that saves one
   instruction per check is felt in the whole program. [...]
   FURTHER: What the full design already has. Each of these was read in the grammar and in
   chapter 9. - CBCC (9.10.4), compare and branch, writes no flags: one parcel against zero for
   CBEQ and CBNE; two parcels against an immediate or a second register. The unsigned test i >=
   n is CBHIS i, n, fail, and because a negative index is a huge unsigned number, one unsigned
   compare checks 0 <= i < n. - TRAPCC.cc (9.14): [...]
   FURTHER: The analysis (the assistant's reasoning, not a decision). - Guard. The bad index
   must not reach the load, so execution must stop or divert before it. A trap or a branch does
   that. A sticky bit does not: execution continues with the bad index, and the load that
   follows it has already happened. The floating-point analogy holds for pure computation, where
   a not-a-number (NaN) never becomes an address; [...]
   FURTHER: Counts from the current grammar (sizes from the encoding of the grammar, registers
   among the hot ones unless said; the access movq (r3)(r1*8),r4 is one instruction of 4 bytes
   and is not counted). Added per checked access: - cbhis r1, r2, fail: 1 instruction, 4 bytes;
   flags untouched; 4 bytes for r17, r18; with an immediate bound of 100, 4 bytes for r1 and 6
   bytes for r17. [...]
   FURTHER: Opcode cost. A leaf with prefix length L takes 2^(16-L) of the 65,536 first-parcel
   patterns (docs/encoding-work/optimizer.md; the lengths are in spec/pdp64/tables.md). Existing
   leaves of the same shape give the prices. CBCC register-register, the leaf CBR8/c: prefix 12,
   16 patterns for its four conditions, 4 each (CBR8H/c, prefix 14: 1 each). The
   register-register group R2 (prefix 12: [...]
   FURTHER: The answer to the author's last question (the assistant's reasoning, with the counts
   above). As a check, a guard (CBHIS or CMP; TRAPCC) is more efficient than a clamp: it is 1 or
   2 instructions and 4 bytes against 4 to 10 instructions and 12 to 32 bytes, and it is off the
   critical path. As a computed repair, MIN and MAX stay, with the cost J.9.1 sets.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Nothing new: CBCC and TRAPCC, with the aliases. 0 patterns. For: the guard is already one
      instruction (cbhis, 4 bytes, flags untouched) or two (cmp; trapcc, 4 bytes); nothing to
      specify or verify; errata 85 recorded no compare-and-trap form. Against: CBHIS needs a
      stub; CMP; TRAPCC writes the flags and is two instructions; there is no sticky form, so an
      accumulation costs a branch per step; the clamp is 10 instructions.
   B) A compare-and-trap CHK rs, rb (unsigned, register or 12-bit immediate bound), with its own
      vector. 0 patterns in the R2 shape (4 bytes), 256 for a one-parcel hot form (2 bytes), 2
      for the immediate form. For: one instruction in place of two; flags untouched, so it can
      sit between a compare and its branch; the compare runs in the branch unit's comparator
      (inference). Against: errata 85 decided the other way, and in the usual case it saves an
      instruction and no bytes (4 against 4); only the one-parcel form saves bytes, and that
      spends 0.39% of the code space on one word; one more vector. Precedents: 68000 CHK (an
      emulator's decoder has it, target/m68k/translate.c, verified), PowerPC tw, twi, td, tdi
      (target/ppc/insn32.decode of the same source, verified), MIPS TLT, TGEU and the other
      trap-compares (target/mips/tcg/micromips_translate.c.inc, verified), SPARC Tcc
      (target/sparc/insns.decode, verified), x86 BOUND, which is illegal in 64-bit mode (the
      decoder marks it so, target/i386/tcg/decode-new.c.inc, verified); the VAX INDEX
      (unverified).
   C) A two-bound CHK2 rs, rlo, rhi. 0 patterns in the R3 shape (4 bytes, 1 instruction against
      8 bytes and 2); 32 for a form with two immediates. For: the Pascal and Ada subscript with
      a lower bound other than zero is one instruction; replaces the INDEX of the proposals.
      Against: in C, Rust and Go the lower bound is zero and the unsigned compare of option A
      already does it; three register fields; the same reversal of errata 85. Precedents: 68020
      CHK2 and CMP2 (the emulator source gates chk2 on the 68020 feature,
      target/m68k/translate.c, verified), x86 BOUND (verified, above), VAX INDEX (unverified).
   D) A sticky range flag: RANGE rs, rlo, rhi sets a bit in a status register that is read and
      cleared like FSFLAGS, with the rule that it guards computation only. 0 patterns (R3 and R2
      words), plus a system register or a bit in FPSR (details open). For: no branch in an inner
      loop; one test after the block; the model the floating-point flags already use. Against:
      execution goes on with the bad value, so a use as an address is not stopped (the rule must
      be enforced by the compiler); a new piece of saved state; the compiler must place the
      test, and a lost test is a silent error. Precedents: the ARM Q flag (QF in
      target/arm/cpu.h, PSR_Q_BIT in arch/arm/include/uapi/asm/ptrace.h, verified), the PowerPC
      summary-overflow bit XER[SO] (XER_SO, target/ppc/cpu.h, verified), the MIPS DSP control
      register (DSPControl in target/mips/cpu.h, verified to exist; the flag field unverified),
      the x86 MXCSR sticky flags (mxcsr in target/i386/cpu.h, verified to exist; the flag
      meanings unverified) and the IEEE 754 flags (unverified).
   E) Clamp and mask forms: a CLAMP rd, lo, hi or the aliases. 0 patterns in the R3 shape. For:
      the repair is wanted where a value must be forced into range (a colour component, a
      volume); constant time (J.9.2). Against: hides the bug when used as a check; one or two
      dependent operations on every use; the select instructions already serve (J.9.1, which
      decides how MIN and MAX expand, and the cost of the alias above: 10 instructions and 32
      bytes against 4 and 12 with a flag-reading select). The mask needs nothing new (3
      instructions, 10 bytes, or 4 and 8). Precedent: array_index_nospec (verified, above).
   F) One instruction with a mode in its sub-opcode: trap, sticky, mask, clamp. 0 patterns (four
      words in R3, or one name in the assembler over four words). For: one mnemonic to learn;
      the compiler picks the use. Against: four behaviours under one name; a mode that lives in
      a register instead of the sub-opcode would be state that changes what the instruction
      does, which is the strange side effect the author has refused (J.9.12); the same flag and
      trap questions as options B and 4.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Options A and 4 together: keep CBHIS and TRAPCC for
   guards, and add the sticky RANGE only for accumulation, if a compiler wants it; do not add
   the compare-and-trap of options B and 3 without a measurement of sanitiser-built code, since
   they save an instruction and no bytes. This is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "new design features from another linux kernel developer: make some
   instructions or easy way to do range checks on a register value, a trap on out of range,
   similar to overflow, or set a bit in a sticky register, exception register, etc. same thing
   as on floating point math when you have nan/inf etc, you don't trap you set bits in a status
   register. figure out what is most efficient from a coding point of view. would this be more
   efficient than using the cse select min/max functions?" (errata 138); errata 84 item 9
   (overflow-trapping arithmetic, "leaning yes") and errata 85 (TRAPCC a trap, no
   compare-and-trap form); errata 98 (MIN and MAX are aliases).
   BODY: Section 9.7.7 and section 9.21.1.

ENTRY J.9.14
ORDER: 2   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: Provenance and taint tags: input taint of the Perl kind, tag bits in RAM
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (taint lane, tag instructions and a tag bit per word of memory)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: Perl's taint mode marks data that comes from outside the program (the command line,
   the environment, files, the network) as tainted. Operations propagate the mark: a string
   built from a tainted string is tainted. Using a tainted value in a dangerous place (exec,
   open for writing, system, eval, a database query) is an error, unless the program launders
   the value, which in Perl means extracting it with a pattern the programmer has checked. [...]
   (Full text: Appendix J, J.9.14.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Injection (a command, a path, a format string, a query built from input) is
   the commonest class of software vulnerability, and an application cannot easily check it
   everywhere. [...]
   FURTHER: What is decided already, and what is not. Decided, read in docs/study-proposals.md,
   errata 84, 85 and 86, docs/retire-stage-candidates.md and the manual: - Poison (study item
   10; errata 84 and 86): a bit per register, set by a failed deferred load, propagated by OR
   through register operations, a fault when a poisoned register writes the flags, is a base, an
   index or the data of a store; saved in EPSW. [...]
   FURTHER: How hard is this with the current opcodes (the assistant's analysis, labelled;
   counts from the encoding of the grammar).
   FURTHER: (a) In an interpreter or a language runtime, as Perl does it: no architecture is
   needed. Perl keeps the mark in the value: a scalar has taint magic (PERL_MAGIC_taint,
   SvTAINTED, sv.h, mg_vtable.h) and a global PL_tainted says that the operation in progress
   touched tainted data; the sinks call TAINT_PROPER, which croaks (perl.h; all read in the Perl
   headers on this machine). The pdp64 runs this as any machine does.
   FURTHER: (b) A compiler-inserted shadow memory scheme (LLVM DataFlowSanitizer style) on the
   current opcodes. A shadow byte for each byte of memory (or for each 8 bytes) lives at a fixed
   offset from the data. Per memory access: with a shadow byte for each byte, one extra
   instruction, movzb (r9)(r1*1), r5 for a load and movb r5, (r9)(r1*1) for a store, 4 bytes
   each (the scaled-index form with a dedicated base register r9); [...]
   FURTHER: (c) A kernel-enforced coarse scheme with the page bits that exist. A page class for
   input buffers, and PROBER and PROBEW to test the pages of the arguments of a system call. 0
   opcodes; page granularity, so a tainted page taints everything on it (false alarms); a copy
   from a tainted page to a clean one escapes unless laundered; cheap, weak.
   FURTHER: (d) Colours as provenance. A colour for "from outside". The colour check compares
   the pointer's colour with the memory's and does not propagate; and no instruction reads a
   colour yet (items 1 and 2 are to be specified), so it needs that first. It tells where a
   buffer came from, not what a value computed from it is.
   FURTHER: (e) The secret-taint lane for integrity. The same propagation rule with different
   sinks (system call arguments, exec paths), by a second set of masks or a mode flag. Possible;
   but the secrecy sinks are timing sinks, and the integrity sinks are system calls and
   addresses of the kernel, so the enforcement logic differs and the lane is not reusable as it
   is.
   FURTHER: (f) A full hardware scheme. A fourth lane per register beside poison, secret and
   valid-pointer (32 bits more in a saved word at 32 registers), a tag bit per word of memory
   shared with the pointer tag (2 bits per 8 bytes: [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) None in hardware: runtime taint as in Perl, and a compiler shadow scheme on the current
      opcodes. 0 patterns. For: nothing to specify, verify or save; works now; the 40 to 80
      agent-hour estimate above. Against: the 2 to 4 times slowdown and the memory cost make it
      a testing tool and not an always-on defence; no protection against code outside the
      compiled program.
   B) Page-level taint with the page bits and the probe instruction. 0 patterns, kernel work
      only. For: cheap; the kernel can refuse a tainted page as a system call argument. Against:
      page granularity; false alarms; copies need laundering; weak.
   C) Provenance by colour. 0 patterns beyond what items 1 and 2 already take, plus one
      instruction to read a colour. For: reuses a decided mechanism. Against: it does not
      propagate, so it marks buffers and not values; needs the colour specification first.
   D) A fourth lane (untrusted) beside poison, secret and valid-pointer, with per-word memory
      tags shared with the pointer tag. 2 to 4 words in R2 (0 patterns); the saved word grows by
      a lane; the tag side band doubles to 2 bits per 8 bytes. For: Perl's model for compiled
      code at the cost of one OR per ALU result; deterministic like the secret lane; shared tag
      storage. Against: more state in the context switch and the exception frame; DMA and device
      writes must set tags; a policy at the sinks needs the kernel and compiler to agree; the
      over-tainting problems of every dynamic taint system; it widens the memory path.
   E) The full programmable metadata unit. Words: a handful; gates: large. For: any policy,
      including integrity, secrecy and colour, in one mechanism. Against: heaviest in gates and
      verification; the research hardware that has it is not in a product (unverified).
   F) Taint only for secrecy, as item 11: no integrity taint. 0 patterns. For: nothing more to
      specify; matches the decisions; the kernel and the language runtimes keep the integrity
      checks they have. Against: no hardware help for injection.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A now, because the machine can run it today and
   the estimate is 40 to 80 agent-hours; keep options D and 6 open until the poison and secret
   lanes (J.9.12) are specified, since a fourth lane shares their design and the tag side band;
   do not take option E. This item asks whether to add an input-taint lane and per-word tags;
   the mechanisms of poison, secret taint, the valid-pointer tag, colours and the page bits
   stand as decided. This is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: Perl's own implementation (verified in perl.h, sv.h and mg_vtable.h of the
   installed headers). OpenBSD pledge and unveil as the policy alternative: they restrict what a
   process may do, not where a value came from (sys/kern/kern_pledge.c, kern_unveil.c exist in
   the OpenBSD tree on this machine; verified). The Linux kernel's "tainted" flags (TAINT_* in
   include/linux/panic.h, read) are a different thing: they record that the kernel as a whole
   has run in an unsupported state (a proprietary module, a forced module, an out-of-spec CPU),
   not the origin of data. Unverified, from general knowledge: SPARC M7 Application Data
   Integrity, ARM Memory Tagging Extension, the CHERI tag bit, the Intel iAPX 432, Burroughs and
   Lisp-machine tagged memory, the dynamic information-flow tracking papers of Suh and others
   (2004), RIFLE, Raksha, FlexiTaint, and the libdft, DFSan and TaintCheck software tools.
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "also perl taint mode, bits for each word in ram, to mark where the bytes
   came from, tagging.... do we have something like that already?" and "so right now, tell me
   how hard this would be to do with current opcodes (as per the full design), the taint thing
   as in perl" (errata 138); errata 84 items 3, 10 and 11, errata 85 and 86 (the poison, secret
   and valid-pointer lanes decided in outline).
   BODY: Section 6.4.4 and section 9.21.1.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.5.5
ORDER: 3   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 5 Drivers and devices
   TITLE: The fence in C
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (FENCE spelling in C: memory ordering instruction)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A driver must execute FENCE between the last write that the device will read and the
   write that notifies the device (5.3.5, 5.4.3). The instruction exists. Nothing in the C
   language names it, and the compiler has no intrinsic (a built-in function that the compiler
   turns into an instruction) for it. A driver can call a function written in assembly, but then
   the fence is a call, with the call's cost and the compiler's assumption that registers may be
   changed.
   WHY IT MATTERS: Every driver of the chapter, and every lock and queue of the kernel that
   shares memory with a device, executes this fence on its fast path. The answer is part of the
   compiler and of the ABI (appendix D), and the OpenBSD port needs a spelling for its own
   memory-barrier macros.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) An intrinsic. A built-in such as __builtin_pdp64_fence() that the compiler turns into the
      instruction and treats as a barrier against reordering. For: one instruction, no call, the
      compiler knows it is a barrier. Against: a new name that only this compiler knows; the
      LLVM back end needs it as well.
   B) Inline assembly. The driver writes asm volatile("fence" ::: "memory"). For: needs nothing
      new in the compiler. Against: every driver repeats it; the compiler that has no inline
      assembly cannot build it.
   C) A function in assembly. A leaf function in the runtime library. For: works with any
      compiler. Against: a call on every doorbell; the compiler must assume everything changed.
   D) The C11 fences. atomic_thread_fence and the release and acquire orderings, mapped by the
      compiler to FENCE as needed. For: standard C; portable drivers. Against: the C11 orderings
      describe threads and not a device, and a mapping that is too weak or too strong is a bug
      that no test shows.
   E) The platform's barrier macros. The operating system defines membar_producer-style macros
      in a header, built on one of the above. For: what OpenBSD code expects. Against: it
      answers only the spelling, and not how the compiler treats the fence.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A, with option E on top of it for the operating
   system: it is the only spelling that costs nothing at run time and that the compiler can
   reason about.
   PRECEDENTS: Linux defines mb(), wmb() and rmb() per architecture, and OpenBSD
   membar_producer() and membar_consumer() (unverified). The compiler's name table
   (arch/pdp64/pdp64-names.h of the pcc tree) knows the mnemonic and nothing else (verified:
   read).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: None named; the one fence rule of the bus chapter (2.8).
   BODY: Section 5.13.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.6.4
ORDER: 4   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: The register number and the layout of the kernel's root table (KTBR)
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (system register number)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author accepted the split of the address space by its top bit (errata 71): the
   lower half is walked from PTBR, one table for each address space, and the upper half from a
   second register, KTBR, one table for the whole system. The system-register table (6.5.1) has
   no number for KTBR: numbers 0 to 12 are taken and 13 and up are reserved. Nothing says how
   the top-level tables of the two roots are laid out. [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.6.4.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel writes KTBR once and the walker reads it on every upper-half miss,
   so the number is fixed in the instruction stream of every kernel (MTSR takes an 8-bit
   constant), and the layout decides how a kernel builds its tables and whether a page can hold
   both roots. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Number 13, the first free register. For: no gap, and the numbers 0 to 12 stay as they are.
      Against: it takes the number that J.6.1 would use for a feature register, so one of them
      moves.
   B) A number in a block kept for translation (for example 16 and up), leaving 13 to 15 for
      identification. For: translation registers stay together, and identification keeps its own
      run. Against: a gap in the numbering, and the decision about the block is itself new.
   C) Each root is a full page; KTBR holds the address of a 4 KB table whose upper 256 entries
      are used. For: the same table shape as every level, so one walker routine serves both
      roots. Against: half a page of each root is unused; with one kernel root for the whole
      system the loss is small, with a root for each address space it is not.
   D) PTBR and KTBR each point at a half-page of 256 entries. For: no unused entries. Against: a
      table that is not page-sized is an exception to the rule that every table is one page
      (6.6), and a page cannot be given back whole.
   E) One shared table with two roots indexed by the top bit (what x86 kernels do when they
      share a table and split it by position in the top level). For: a single register, so the
      number question disappears. Against: the user half would see the kernel entries in the
      same table, which is what the split was made to avoid (the speculative read of kernel
      translations, 6.9.1); this is the arrangement the author's decision replaced.
   F) The upper half is selected by a field instead of the top bit (a base and a size register).
      For: the kernel could choose where the halves meet. Against: it defeats the canonical test
      that decides the half by one bit and puts a comparator in the walker.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A with option C: KTBR as number 13 if J.6.1 puts
   its feature register elsewhere, otherwise the first number after the feature block, and each
   root a full page. It costs the least new rule; the unused half is small because there is one
   kernel root.
   PRECEDENTS: AArch64 has two translation base registers, TTBR0 and TTBR1, and selects between
   them by the top bits of the address (unverified: not in the QEMU source checked here). RISC-V
   has one satp and no second root (verified in the QEMU source). x86 kernels shared the user's
   tables until page-table isolation of January 2018 and have since switched the table on every
   entry (repository text: docs/kernel-pain-points.md, row 1). The VAX had a base register for
   the process space and another for the system space (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: His acceptance of "all 8" of the edits of research round 7 (errata 71),
   of which the split is one.
   BODY: Section 6.9.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.6.5
ORDER: 5   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: The system-call register convention
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (system-call register convention)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A system call is TRAP #n through vector 256 + n (6.10.2), and the call number and
   arguments "travel in registers by the ABI". Appendix D, the ABI, does not say which
   registers. In V7 the call number was fetched back from the user's sys n instruction and the
   arguments were read from the user's stack with fuword.
   WHY IT MATTERS: The first operating system (OpenBSD, 6.12.2) and its C library each hard-code
   the convention, and so does a system-call stub. The kernel's entry code reads the registers
   before it knows anything else about the process. A convention that overlaps the registers the
   callee-saved rule preserves costs a move on every call.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The number in the TRAP vector alone, and arguments in the first argument registers of the
      ordinary calling convention. The vector number is the system-call number, so the kernel
      dispatches in hardware. For: no register carries the number, and the dispatch is a table
      lookup. Against: 256 numbers (TRAPL reaches them), which is a limit, and an operating
      system with more calls than that needs a second level.
   B) The number in one register (the first argument register or a dedicated one), arguments in
      the following ones, and one vector (TRAP #0). For: the form of Linux and of the BSDs on
      every architecture (unverified); any number of calls; one entry point. Against: one
      register is spent on the number; the dispatcher reads it before using the table.
   C) The number in the TRAP operand and arguments in the argument registers, results in the
      first result register, an error in the carry flag. For: PDP-11 Unix reported an error in
      the carry bit after sys (unverified) and the BSDs on x86 kept it; the result and error
      need no second register. Against: ties the convention to the flags, which the entry clears
      (6.3.2) and the exit must set.
   D) The number in a register and the arguments in memory at an address held in a register.
      For: any number of arguments without a limit from the register file. Against: the kernel
      must read user memory with the guard open (6.10) before it can dispatch, which is the V7
      cost the register form was meant to remove.
   E) Follow the platform's ordinary calling convention exactly, so the stub is a function with
      one TRAP. For: the compiler needs no special case; a wrapper of the C library is trivial.
      Against: the callee-saved registers and the argument registers are the ABI's to choose
      first (appendix D), so this option only moves the question.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B with the error and result in registers,
   following the ABI's argument registers, because it matches what the first operating system's
   other ports do. The author or the ABI work decides it, and the decision belongs in appendix
   D.
   PRECEDENTS: V7 read the number from the user's instruction and the arguments from the user's
   stack (verified: trap.c on this machine). Linux and the BSDs on x86-64 and AArch64 put the
   number in a register and the arguments in the first registers (unverified); RISC-V Linux uses
   a7 for the number and a0 to a5 for arguments (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: None recorded for the convention; the decisions are that TRAP #n has its
   own vector (6.10.2) and that the ABI names the registers.
   BODY: Section 6.10.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.6.6
ORDER: 6   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: Enabling translation safely
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (translation-enable instructions)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: Translation is off at reset and MMUCTL bit 0 turns it on (6.8.2); the write takes
   effect after the next ISYNC (6.5.2). The instruction after the ISYNC is fetched through the
   new tables. The running code was fetched at a physical address, so it continues only if the
   new tables map it at the same address, or if something else (a window, a trampoline, a second
   mapping) covers it. The architecture does not say which.
   WHY IT MATTERS: Every kernel's first page of boot code has to do this. If the architecture
   does not say how it is safe, each port invents a trick, and a trick that depends on the
   implementation (the length of an instruction queue, say) is a bug.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The kernel maps its own boot code at the address it is running at. The kernel builds the
      tables so that the virtual address of the next instruction equals its physical address (an
      identity mapping of the boot code), enables translation, and jumps to the real address.
      For: no hardware help; it is what the first OS ports to RISC-V do. Against: the kernel
      must build an identity mapping before it can enable anything, and tear it down after.
   B) A direct-mapping window covers physical memory at reset or at the write. For: the kernel
      need not build anything first. Against: the format of the windows is deferred (appendix I,
      6.9.2), and the ISA text's old rule that DMW0 covers physical memory at reset was
      superseded by errata 44, so this option needs the deferred decision made.
   C) Translation enables in two steps: the write sets a bit that takes effect at the next RTI.
      The kernel arranges EPC and EPSW so that the RTI jumps to the mapped address. For: one
      clean transition, in the same way the kernel already leaves for user mode. Against: a new
      rule for one bit, and RTI now has a second job.
   D) The write takes effect immediately and the instruction after it is fetched physical, once.
      For: no mapping needed for that single instruction, and the kernel puts a jump there.
      Against: it breaks the rule that ISYNC is the point at which a change takes effect
      (6.5.2), and an out-of-order core cannot promise it.
   E) A bare mode: when translation is on, an address in the first page range is still taken as
      physical. For: the early kernel keeps running where it is. Against: it recreates a fixed
      window the architecture otherwise refuses (errata 97: no address bit means "device"), and
      the range is another number to choose (J.4.1).
   F) The boot ROM enables translation with a ready-made identity table and hands over with
      translation on. For: the kernel starts in a mapped world. Against: the reset state is then
      different from the decided one (translation off, 6.8.2), and the ROM carries a page table.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A, since it needs no new rule and is what a
   kernel already does. If a window is later specified, option B makes the early kernel simpler
   and can be added without changing option A.
   PRECEDENTS: RISC-V's satp has a bare mode and kernels turn paging on after an identity
   mapping or a jump to a mapped trampoline (satp modes verified in QEMU; the kernel practice
   unverified). AArch64 has SCTLR.M, and a kernel that turns the MMU on runs from an
   identity-mapped page (unverified). The PDP-11 enabled the memory manager by a write to SSR0
   and the V7 boot code ran with the map loaded to identity (mov $1,SSR0 in mch_i.s, verified;
   the identity map unverified). The VAX turned on mapping with MAPEN and a jump to a mapped
   address (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "good thing we already had dmw in the design!" (chosen; deferred by
   errata 44).
   BODY: Section 6.12.2.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.6.7
ORDER: 7   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: Saving and restoring a thread's user DP
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (DP register save and restore)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: DP is the direct-page register, banked by mode (6.2.4). A user program's copy points
   to its thread's private data. At a thread switch the kernel must save the old thread's user
   DP and restore the new one's. MTDP and MFDP name only the copy of the current mode, and the
   system-register table (6.5.1) has no number for the user's DP, whereas USP and SSP have
   (numbers 6 and 7).
   WHY IT MATTERS: Every thread switch and every signal frame needs the thread pointer. Without
   a way to read and write the user copy from kernel mode, the kernel cannot give threads their
   own thread-local storage, which every C library with threads needs.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A system register for the user's DP (for example UDP, beside USP). For: the same idiom as
      USP; one MFSR and one MTSR. Against: one more register number, and the supervisor copy
      would want its own for symmetry.
   B) An instruction that reads or writes the previous mode's DP (as MFPI and MTPI reach the
      previous mode's space on the PDP-11). For: no number; the previous mode field already says
      which. Against: a new opcode, and it works only on the way in from user mode, not for a
      thread other than the one that trapped.
   C) The kernel keeps the thread pointer in its own thread structure and writes it back into
      the trap frame. The user's DP is saved on entry like any other register. For: no new
      hardware; DP becomes an ordinary register for exception handling. Against: the entry code
      must save a banked register that the hardware did not save for it, so the claim that entry
      costs one PUSHX and a few stores no longer holds for it.
   D) The thread pointer is an ordinary general register by the ABI, as RISC-V's tp is, and DP
      stays the per-CPU pointer in kernel mode only. For: no banked user copy; the register is
      saved in the trap frame with the rest. Against: the user DP is a decided feature (6.2.4,
      the thread pointer is one MOVQ d(DP), Rd), and removing the user copy is a change of the
      decided design.
   E) MTDP and MFDP in kernel mode with a mode operand. For: one pair of instructions. Against:
      it changes decided instructions.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A, in the same register table as USP, because it
   repeats a pattern that is already decided.
   PRECEDENTS: RISC-V keeps the thread pointer in the general register tp and the kernel saves
   it in the trap frame (unverified). x86-64 reads the user's FS base with rdfsbase or writes it
   with wrfsbase, and the kernel swaps GS with swapgs (unverified). AArch64 has TPIDR_EL0 for
   the user thread pointer, reachable by MRS and MSR from the kernel (unverified). The PDP-11
   reached the other space by MFPI and MTPI (verified: V7 source).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: None recorded; the banked DP is the author's decision (6.2.4).
   BODY: Section 6.11.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.6.8
ORDER: 8   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: What a write to the translation registers flushes, and invalidating by address-space
   number
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (TLB flush semantics, TLBIASID)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: Two texts of the specification disagree. Section 6.8 (and the purpose of the
   address-space tags) says that a context switch is a write to PTBR and to ASID and an ISYNC,
   with the old process's entries staying in the buffer. The entry of MFSR in chapter 9 says
   that a write to PTBR, ASID or MMUCTL flushes the whole translation buffer. The reference
   executor does the latter (docs/implementation-status.md). A related gap: the architecture has
   TLBI (by address) and TLBIA (everything) but no invalidate by ASID, so a kernel that reuses a
   number, or that wraps the 16-bit counter, can only flush everything; [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.6.8.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: If a write flushes, tags save nothing and every context switch pays a refill
   of the buffer, which is what the tags were added to avoid. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A write to PTBR, ASID or MMUCTL flushes nothing; the kernel invalidates when it reuses a
      number. For: the tags work, and a switch costs two register writes. Against: a reused ASID
      needs a whole-buffer flush (TLBIA) or an invalidate-by-ASID instruction, and a mistake in
      the kernel's allocator shows up as a wrong translation.
   B) A write to MMUCTL flushes everything, and a write to PTBR or ASID flushes nothing. For:
      turning translation on or off is rare, so a flush there is cheap, and it removes stale
      state at the one moment when it matters. Against: one more rule to state, and an extra
      flush on a write that sets only a bit.
   C) Every write flushes the entries of the old tag only. For: the old process's entries cannot
      answer under a reused number. Against: it is a hardware search for the tag on a write, as
      costly as an invalidate by ASID; the entries that survive are the ones of other tags.
   D) Add TLBIASID rN, an invalidate by address-space number, to option A. For: the cheap way to
      reuse one number without flushing the others; a wrap is then a loop over numbers or a
      TLBIA. Against: a new broadcast instruction (each core must search the tag), and the
      kernel needs the bookkeeping either way.
   E) Flush all on every write to PTBR or ASID (the text of chapter 9 today). For: the simplest
      hardware, with no tag matching in the invalidate path; the buffer is always consistent
      with the registers. Against: the tags are then dead weight (16 bits in every buffer
      entry), and every switch is a full refill.
   F) The tag has the generation in it (the high bits count the wraps). For: a wrap needs no
      flush because an old number never equals a new one. Against: a wider tag, and the
      generation is the kernel's policy, not the hardware's, so this is option A with the policy
      written in.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A, and the entry of MFSR in chapter 9 corrected
   to match; the invalidate by number of option D only if the first kernel finds that TLBIA at
   each wrap is too expensive.
   PRECEDENTS: RISC-V's satp carries a 16-bit address-space number and the sfence.vma
   instruction can invalidate by it (SATP64_ASID verified in the QEMU source; the instruction
   form unverified). x86 has a process-context tag (CR4.PCIDE, verified in the QEMU source) and
   an instruction invpcid that invalidates by it (unverified). AArch64 has TLBI ASIDE1
   (unverified). The PDP-11 loaded all page registers at each switch and had no buffer to flush.
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The ISA text takes the ASID and the G bit from the Commodore 128's
   memory-management unit (docs/pdp64-isa.md section 8.1); the purpose of the tag, a switch
   without a flush, is stated in 6.8.
   BODY: Section 6.8.3. chapter 9 (the entry of MFSR).

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.6.9
ORDER: 9   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: Whether supervisor mode has a use
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (status word mode field)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The status word has a mode field of two bits with kernel 0, supervisor 1 and user 3,
   and the value 2 reserved (6.2.2). The stack pointer and DP are banked for supervisor mode as
   for the other two (6.2.4), and the system registers include SSP. No code uses the mode. The
   mode is decided; whether it has a use is not. 2.11BSD runs its network code in supervisor
   mode on the PDP-11 (trap() tests SUPVMODE(ps), verified).
   WHY IT MATTERS: A mode that nothing uses costs a third copy of two registers and a third set
   of mode checks (docs/pdp64-system-design.md, "an unresolved question, not a decision"). [...]
   FURTHER: What the earlier text said (restored at the author's request, errata 117). "Three
   modes cost a third copy of two registers and no logic the kernel uses; supervisor mode has no
   user, and is included because adding a mode would change the status word and every saved copy
   of it." That is the assistant's inference, not the author's statement, and no document gives
   a reason; [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Keep it unused. For: nothing to build or specify; a later use needs no change of the
      status word. Against: a third bank of two registers and a mode comparison that nothing
      uses.
   B) Use it for a library with its own privilege (a user-level subsystem protected from the
      program that calls it). For: finer protection between the kernel and a program. Against:
      needs a page permission that supervisor but not user may use, a new U-like bit, and a way
      to enter and leave the mode cheaply.
   C) Use it for a hypervisor's guest kernel (the guest runs in supervisor mode and the host in
      kernel mode). For: a guest kernel needs a privilege between its host and its user
      programs. Against: a hypervisor needs more than a mode (a second translation,
      virtual-machine control), which is not specified.
   D) Use it for the network stack, as 2.11BSD did. For: the one historical use; a bug in the
      stack cannot reach the kernel's own data. Against: 2.11BSD gave it up in later systems
      (unverified); the cost of the mode change on each packet.
   E) Remove the mode and reserve the value 3 for user and 2 and 1 as illegal. For: three copies
      become two and the decode is simpler. Against: the status-word layout does not change, so
      no bit is saved (see above); loses the PDP-11 encoding's meaning; reverses a decided
      statement (6.2.1).
   F) Make the mode field a width that holds four modes, reserve the fourth for a hypervisor.
      For: room to grow. Against: it already holds four values; the value 2 is free now.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A: keep the mode, since removing it saves no bit,
   and decide a use when the hypervisor question (option C) is asked.
   PRECEDENTS: x86 has four rings of which two are used (unverified); AArch64 has exception
   levels 0 to 3, of which a hypervisor uses level 2 (unverified); RISC-V has user, supervisor
   and machine modes, and the hypervisor extension adds virtual versions (unverified). 2.11BSD
   uses the PDP-11's supervisor mode for the network (verified: SUPVMODE in trap.c); V7 used
   kernel and user only (verified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The PDP-11 encoding (kernel 0, supervisor 1, user 3), which the author
   kept (6.2.1).
   BODY: Section 6.2.6.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.6.10
ORDER: 10   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: The vector of TRAPCC
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (TRAPCC vector)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: TRAPCC.cc (the word that enters the kernel when a condition holds) is a decided trap:
   it completes and EPC is the next instruction (6.13.8), and the body gives it the check
   vector, 16, which the study the author approved (errata 85, 86) names. What is not worked out
   is the number itself, which the author did not discuss (errata 88: "the vector number is the
   proposal's"), and what EVA holds (the study says the address of the TRAPCC). The options
   below are the alternatives to the study's choice.
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel's vector table needs the entry, a debugger and a signal layer need
   to know what the trap means, and a compiler that emits TRAPCC for a runtime check (an array
   bound, an arithmetic overflow) must name what the program sees.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Vector 16, a vector of its own, with EVA the address of the TRAPCC. For: the kernel tells
      a check from an illegal instruction at once; the vectors 0 to 31 are the processor's, and
      16 is free. Against: a vector of the processor's block used up by an optional word of two
      members.
   B) The vector is 256 + n of a TRAP number named by the instruction. For: no processor vector
      is spent, and the operating system names the check. Against: TRAPCC has no operand (one
      parcel), so it cannot carry n.
   C) The illegal-instruction vector (2). For: nothing to add. Against: a check is not an error
      in the instruction; the kernel must decode to tell them apart.
   D) The breakpoint vector (15). For: the same shape (a deliberate trap by a program). Against:
      the debugger and a check share a vector.
   E) One vector for each condition (fourteen vectors). For: the handler learns the condition
      from the vector. Against: it uses fourteen of the thirty-two entries of the processor
      block, which leaves no room.
   F) The vector chosen by a system register (a TRAPCC vector register). For: the operating
      system picks. Against: one more register and a hidden dependence between an instruction
      and the register.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A as the study had it, and as the body states it;
   a decision of the author fixes the number.
   PRECEDENTS: MIPS has TEQ, TNE and the other trap-on-condition instructions, which raise the
   trap exception with the condition in a code field (unverified). PowerPC has TW and TD (trap
   word and doubleword on a condition) (unverified). x86 has INTO (trap on overflow, its own
   vector 4) (unverified); AArch64 has BRK with an immediate (unverified); RISC-V has no
   trap-on-condition instruction (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: Errata 85 (item 9: TRAPCC with the fused ADDV, SUBV and MULV forms) and
   errata 86 (TRAPCC is a trap and not an abort); none recorded for the vector number or for
   EVA.
   BODY: Sections 6.3 and 6.13.8.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.6.12
ORDER: 11   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: Recovering from a failed user copy
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (user-copy fault: instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A kernel copy routine opens the guard, copies and closes it (6.10.3). Before the copy
   PROBER checks that the page is readable, but the copy may still fault (a file mapped into the
   process may be truncated under it, or a page may have to be brought in). The page-fault
   handler can bring a missing page in and restart the instruction (6.4), so a fault that the
   kernel can repair is not a problem. A fault that it cannot repair (an unmapped address) has
   to turn into an EFAULT return from the copy routine. The author accepted the fixup table with
   the .U qualifier (errata 86, item 18; [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.6.12.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Every system call that takes a user pointer depends on it, and a kernel that
   cannot recover panics on a bad pointer from user mode, which is the attack that the guard
   exists to stop.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A global nofault return address, as V6 and V7 had. The routine stores a recovery address,
      the handler on a fault in kernel mode at a user address jumps there. For: already
      described in 6.4 and tested in V7; no new instruction. Against: a global (or per-thread)
      variable that the routine must reset on every path out; a fault in other kernel code at
      the same time would be taken for it.
   B) Address ranges of the copy routines. The handler compares EPC with the start and end of
      the copy routines and jumps to one recovery address. For: no variable to set, no
      instruction change. Against: every copy routine must lie in the range, and any new copy
      loop must be added to it.
   C) A fixup table: the qualifier .U marks the instructions that touch user memory and the
      handler looks up EPC in a table of recovery addresses. This is the decided form of 6.10.4.
      For: exact for each instruction, no variable, no range, and the move accesses user memory
      with user privilege so the guard is not needed. Against: a new qualifier on seven words, a
      table to build, and the specification is incomplete (the atomics).
   D) Check every page first with PROBER and PROBEW, never fault. For: no recovery path.
      Against: a page can disappear between the check and the copy (a file truncated by another
      thread), so it is not safe for a mapped file.
   E) Run the copy with a fault handler stack: setjmp-style context saved at the system-call
      entry. For: any depth of nesting returns to the system-call entry with an error. Against:
      the context save is memory traffic on every system call; it needs a new rule that unwinds
      kernel state.
   F) Exception tables in the manner of Linux, in a section of the kernel image. For: the same
      as option C without a new qualifier: any instruction can be listed in the table. Against:
      the table is a section that the assembler and linker must support, and each use is a
      macro.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option C is decided for the .U moves; option A for the
   first kernel's other copies, which is what the OpenBSD port will expect (pcb_onfault).
   PRECEDENTS: V6 and V7 set a global nofault and the trap handler jumped to it (verified:
   trap.c and mch_i.s on this machine). Linux keeps an exception table of (instruction,
   recovery) pairs for user access (unverified); OpenBSD on x86 and ARM keeps a pcb_onfault
   address in the process control block, which the fault handler reads (unverified). x86 reads
   user memory with SMAP open by STAC and CLAC and Linux uses the exception table (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "pdp64 specifically designs the instructions for minimal uops, so we have
   minimal restart/recovery code needed, no?" (2026-11-09, on restart); the five leanings of the
   study that the author accepted (the fixup table, the .U spelling, keeping UA, STUA and CLUA
   until the OpenBSD port confirms them, bit 15 of ECAUSE, the atomics taking the user form).
   BODY: Sections 6.10 and 6.10.4.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.7.1
ORDER: 12   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 7 Multiple processors
   TITLE: The wake rule of WFE and the idle loop
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (WFE semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: WFE (wait for event) puts a core to sleep until another core executes SEV (send event)
   or an interrupt arrives (7.10.1). The idle loop of a kernel checks the run queue and then
   sleeps; if an interrupt arrives between the check and the sleep, it queues work, returns, and
   the core sleeps with work waiting (a lost wake-up, an event that arrives in the gap between
   the test and the sleep and is never seen). The PDP-11's V7 kernel had the same gap and a
   clock tick repaired it within one tick. A core that takes no periodic tick has no repair. The
   question is what ends a WFE sleep. The author decided (2026-10-22: [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.7.1.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The idle loop of every kernel, and the port's cpu_idle_cycle, depend on it. A
   lock waiter that could sleep instead of spinning would save memory bandwidth on 16 cores, and
   the answer decides whether an unlock must execute SEV. A wrong rule is a hang that occurs
   once a week.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The decided rule alone, with SEV sticky for one wait. The loop is SPL #7, test, WFE, SPL
      #0. For: closes the gap with no new instruction; two rules in the core. Against: none
      beyond the decided rule: a core wakes for an interrupt it will not take until the level
      drops (it then takes it, as intended).
   B) Option A, and WFE also ends when the reservation of a preceding LL is lost. A spin wait
      becomes LL r, lock; test; WFE; the holder's ordinary release store wakes the waiter, and
      the unlocker executes no SEV. For: unlock stays one store; a waiter costs no bus
      bandwidth. Against: the reservation must stay armed while the core sleeps; a spurious wake
      is allowed, so the loop re-reads; the reservation rules (7.4.1) must say that the
      reservation survives the sleep.
   C) WFE takes a priority operand. One instruction lowers the level and sleeps. For: no SPL
      around it. Against: a new operand form to encode; no gain where the loop is already two
      instructions.
   D) A timeout form with a deadline. For: a waiter cannot sleep forever if the wake source
      breaks; the secure core's bounded waits. Against: an operand; a deadline needs the timer.
   E) No WFE; poll with the HINT pause. For: nothing to specify. Against: burns power and
      bandwidth, and on an FPGA a spinning core takes the memory bandwidth the other cores want.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B, which contains option A: a lock waiter sleeps
   without an SEV.
   PRECEDENTS: ARM's WFE ends on SEV, on an interrupt and on the clearing of the exclusive
   monitor, which is how a lock waiter sleeps until the holder's store (unverified: general
   knowledge). RISC-V's wfi ends when an interrupt is pending and enabled in its own register
   whatever the global enable says, and may be a no-op; Zawrs adds a wait on a reservation
   (verified in the repository's notes). x86 has HLT after STI (the instruction after STI cannot
   be interrupted, which makes the pair safe) and MONITOR/MWAIT on a cache line (unverified).
   V7's idle routine lowered the priority and executed wait in a loop (verified in the
   repository's notes). OpenBSD's riscv64 idle is intr_enable(); wfi with a comment that doubts
   it (verified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "main cores tickless"; no system named for the idle loop.
   BODY: Sections 6.11 and 7.10.2.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.7.3
ORDER: 13   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 7 Multiple processors
   TITLE: The operands of the atomic instructions
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (atomic operands and encoding)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The atomic words (7.3.1) take three register fields in the grammar (four for CAS),
   with the address in a register and the size in the sub-opcode. The roles of the registers,
   the size encoding of CAS and any displacement are not fixed in the text. The statement as
   first written: the operands of the atomic words sit in three register fields (four for CAS),
   the roles are not fixed, and the study leans to fixing three register fields now, with the
   fourth reserved zero.
   WHY IT MATTERS: The placement decides how many instructions an atomic costs, how many bytes
   it takes, and what the lock and reference-count code of the kernel compiles to. CAS in the
   four-field form fills the last four-register slots of the encoding.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Register only, address in a register, three fields. rd receives the old value, rs is the
      operand, ra holds the address; CAS has the expected value in and the old value out in rd;
      the fourth field is reserved zero. For: densest; the simplest address path; a misaligned
      address is detected at one place. Against: one LEA (load effective address) when the
      address is a structure field at a nonzero offset.
   B) Option A and a small scaled displacement in the spare field. The address is ra plus d
      times the size, d from 0 to 15. For: removes the LEA for the first sixteen words of a
      structure, where lock owners and reference counts usually sit. Against: uses the spare
      field; one small adder in the atomic unit.
   C) Full memory operand modes as for loads and stores. For: indexed atomics. Against: a
      general address generator in the atomic unit and side effects (auto-increment) that
      complicate restart after a fault.
   D) CAS with fixed registers, as x86. For: frees two register fields. Against: a fixed
      register is a permanent constraint on the compiler in a 16-register machine.
   E) Double-width CAS as a register pair. For: lock-free lists with a tag. Against: not needed
      while a pointer has spare bits to hold a version count.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A now, with the spare field reserved, and a
   displacement taken only when the OpenBSD port is compiled and the LEA count in front of
   atomics is measured.
   PRECEDENTS: RISC-V's AMO words take three registers, address in rs1 with no displacement
   (unverified: from memory); ARM's large-system atomics take registers; x86 takes any memory
   operand with LOCK (unverified). OpenBSD's mutex is one compare-and-swap on the owner word and
   its ticket lock one add (verified in the local source).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: None named beyond the PDP-11 and VAX atomics (DATIP/DATO, interlocked
   queue instructions).
   BODY: Section 7.4.6.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.7.4
ORDER: 14   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 7 Multiple processors
   TITLE: Atomics on uncached memory and on device memory
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (atomics on uncached memory)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The read-modify-write atomics are atomic with respect to every initiator, cores and
   devices alike (7.4.3). That is easy on cached memory, where the cache line is held
   exclusively. On a page of uncached memory or a device page the access goes to the bus, and a
   PCI Express transaction has atomic operations (AtomicOp) only on devices that choose to
   implement them. The mapping of the machine's atomics onto the bus is therefore not stated.
   WHY IT MATTERS: The rings of every virtio device sit in memory that may be uncached; a
   program that issues CAS on such a word needs either a defined result or a defined fault. The
   software cannot be silent about it, and a silent non-atomic update is the worst outcome.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Abort. An atomic to a device or uncached page raises an access fault. For: easy to detect
      and cheap to build. Against: a driver cannot use an atomic on a shared uncached ring.
   B) Performed at the memory controller (far atomics). The operation travels to the memory and
      the result returns. For: constant cost under contention; works for uncached normal memory.
      Against: the memory side needs an arithmetic unit and a defined form for every word, and a
      clear rule for the interaction with reservations.
   C) Forwarded as PCI Express atomic operations where the device advertises them, abort
      otherwise. For: uses what the bus offers. Against: only compare-and-swap, swap and
      fetch-and-add exist on the bus; the other words would still abort.
   D) Defined only for cached normal memory, undefined elsewhere. For: nothing to build.
      Against: undefined behaviour in a place where a fault would be better.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A for device pages and option B or an abort for
   uncached normal memory, decided with the transport of the bus (errata 80); a fault that is
   easy to detect is better than a silent non-atomic update.
   PRECEDENTS: PCI Express defines AtomicOp for fetch-and-add, swap and compare-and-swap
   (unverified). AXI5 has atomic transactions performed near the memory (unverified). The
   PDP-11's DATIP/DATO pair locked the Unibus for the read-modify-write (verified in the
   repository's sources).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: None named.
   BODY: Section 7.4.5.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.8.3
ORDER: 15   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 8 Vector work and the accelerator
   TITLE: Packed integer lanes in the ordinary registers
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (packed lanes in ordinary registers: new instructions)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The processor has no vector register file (8.3.1). A smaller question remains: whether
   instructions should treat a 64-bit integer register as eight bytes, four halves or two words
   (a lane is one of those slots), adding, comparing or taking the minimum in every lane at
   once. The technique without such instructions is SIMD within a register (SWAR): ordinary
   instructions with masks that stop a carry crossing a lane boundary. Adding eight bytes at a
   time, with H the constant 0x8080808080808080: (a code example is omitted here; see the
   manual) Clearing the top bit of every byte leaves room for the carry; [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.8.3.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The opcode map has room for few words; the compiler would need to recognise
   the loops; and the gain is a measurement that nobody has made. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) None. Processor: no change. For: no opcodes; the processor stays small; the block
      operations already serve the string cases. Against: byte-parallel loops run one byte at a
      time or in SWAR sequences of four to six operations.
   B) SWAR helpers only: a zero-byte marker and its relatives. ORCB (each byte becomes 0xFF if
      nonzero and 0x00 if zero; RISC-V's Zbb extension has it as orc.b, read in LLVM) makes the
      zero-byte test two operations, and a few lane-wise selects are of similar size. Processor:
      1 to 3 instructions. For: the smallest set that speeds hand-written scanners and is near
      POPCNT in cost. Against: little over what MSCN does; no arithmetic.
   C) A full packed integer set. Add, subtract, saturating add and subtract (signed and
      unsigned), minimum and maximum (signed and unsigned), average, compare for equality and
      for greater-than (signed and unsigned) and shifts, on 8, 16 and 32 bits: about 14
      operations times 3 widths = 42 mnemonics, which is one leaf with a 6-bit sub-opcode
      (9.1.2; the encoding is being re-optimised, so the cost is measured again then).
      Processor: an instruction-set extension, with lane-masking in the adder. For: serves every
      byte-parallel loop and pixel operation. Against: 42 opcodes, assembler and compiler
      support, and a width fixed at 64 bits; the vectoriser must find the loops. Precedents are
      mixed: ARM's A32 instruction set has packed operations in the general registers (SADD8,
      UADD8, QADD8, USAD8, SEL are defined in LLVM's ARM target, read) and its A64 set dropped
      them for NEON (no such definition in LLVM's AArch64 target, read); Alpha's MVI is 13
      instructions (read in QEMU: minub8, maxsb8, perr, pkwb and others); PA-RISC's MAX-2 has 17
      halfword patterns (hadd, havg, hshl, mixh, permh, ..., read in QEMU); SPARC's VIS has 39
      helper functions in QEMU (fpmerge, pdist, fmul8x16, fpack16, the lane compares fcmpgt16,
      array addressing); the RISC-V P extension is in LLVM as an experimental extension at
      version 0.14 with a partial definition (read). MMX began as packed integers in the
      floating-point registers, MDMX is MIPS's, and TI's C6x has packed halfword and byte
      operations (unverified).
   D) Only the integer dot product. DOT8S, DOT8U, DOT8SU and DOT16S: multiply eight bytes of one
      register by eight of another and add the eight products into a 64-bit accumulator (four
      mnemonics, a 2-bit sub-opcode). Processor: 4 instructions. For: it is the packed operation
      that inference needs, the 8-bit counterpart of XNORPOP, which does 64 one-bit
      multiply-accumulates per instruction. A 4096 by 4096 layer of 8-bit weights is 16,777,216
      multiply-accumulates; with a scalar load, multiply and add at about 3 instructions each,
      50.3 million instructions, 252 ms at 200 MHz and one instruction a cycle; with DOT8S and
      two loads per 8 products, 6.3 million instructions, 31 ms; memory at 2 GB/s needs 8.4 ms
      (all computed under those assumptions), so the processor would be within a factor of four
      of memory-bound. Against: 8 multiply-accumulates an instruction is still two orders below
      a matrix engine; the accumulator and the sign variants add four opcodes for one workload.
      Precedent: x86 VPDPBUSD (VNNI) and ARM SDOT and UDOT are in LLVM's targets (read); RISC-V
      Zvqdotq (read).
   E) Packed lanes on the engine only. Processor: no change. For: nothing in the processor.
      Against: the setup cost (a descriptor, a doorbell, uncached readback) is far above the
      cost of a short string or a pixel row (8.4).
   F) A lane prefix: one prefix over the existing ALU operations. The prefix names the lane
      width and the next instruction (ADD, SUB, MIN, MAX, the compares, the shifts) runs once
      per lane; this is the closest thing to "normal operations with a SIMD bit" (J.8.5).
      Processor: one prefix leaf in the prefix code (9.1.3) and lane-masking in the ALU. For:
      the smallest opcode cost; the operations keep their names. Against: every ALU operation
      must define its behaviour per lane (carry, overflow, saturation, and what a flag-writing
      instruction does when there are eight results); the decoder pairs a prefix with the next
      instruction. x86's VEX and EVEX prefixes are the large precedent (unverified in detail).
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Decide by measurement, as before: compile the corpus at
   higher optimisation and count the loops that would vectorise, measure the share of time in
   byte-parallel loops after the block operations, and estimate the cost in block RAM before any
   opcode is spent. Of the options, 4 is the one with an inference argument behind it (R139,
   inference "in cpu or gpu whatever makes most sense"); 2 and 3 add little over MSCN and MCMP.
   PRECEDENTS: Verified in the sources named above: ARM A32 against A64; Alpha MVI (13), PA-RISC
   MAX-2 (17), SPARC VIS (39 helpers), RISC-V P (experimental), orc.b, SDOT, VPDPBUSD, Zvqdotq.
   Unverified: MMX, MDMX, C6x. The PDP-11 had none.
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "the simd/avax stuff" as the thing to settle by the device route (R136);
   the bit instructions that exist (9.9) were his.
   BODY: Statement 8.3.4.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.8.4
ORDER: 16   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 8 Vector work and the accelerator
   TITLE: Which engine is built first
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (encoding)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The engine is standard (8.4.1) and the architecture says what it must do (J.8.1,
   J.8.2); what remains is what the first implementation is. The author's words point two ways.
   On 2026-10-20 (R136) he asked what "a gpu ... optimized purely for samsung's littlebit-2
   encoding" would look like, which points to an engine for one family of binary-weight models.
   On 2026-10-23 (R278) he wrote "if we can plug a regular card into the bus, why not", and on
   2026-11-04 (R315) "for now we will have a standard pcie set of audio/video devices", which
   point to cards from the field. [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.8.4.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The first engine sets the first descriptor format, which every later one
   copies, and the first operation set that libraries are written against. An engine built for
   one model family is fast on it and idle on the rest. Until one is chosen, "inference on the
   engine" (8.5.1) has nothing to run on.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The LittleBit-style binary inference engine (LB64). Sign-flip accumulate with no
      multipliers, driven by chains of descriptors, one chain per transformer block (H.8). The
      LB64 document computes 1,409,024 sign-flip accumulates for one 4096 by 4096 layer at 0.1
      bit a weight: 11,008 cycles with 128 lanes, 110 microseconds at 100 MHz, in step with the
      105 microseconds of streaming. Processor: no change. Device and driver: a small device
      (adders and sign flips), a chain-building driver. For: the strongest fit to the workload
      the author named; the best use of memory bandwidth by a factor of 80 against 8-bit weights
      (8.4 ms against 105 microseconds); small logic. Against: one model family; no help for the
      array languages or for attention (activations against activations: the LB64 document says
      so); a standard part that serves one family is a poor standard.
   B) A general matrix engine. A tile or systolic array with accumulate, over 8-bit and 16-bit
      formats (J.8.1, option D). Processor: no change. Device and driver: a multiply-accumulate
      array with local tile memory; tile-shape-aware descriptors. For: serves all dense
      inference and training; the form of the TPU, tensor cores, AMX and SME. Against: by the
      arithmetic above it is starved at batch one on a 2 GB/s memory; multipliers cost the most
      in an FPGA; nothing for the array languages.
   C) A general element-wise vector engine. The array-language set (J.8.1, option A) with fused
      short expressions in one descriptor. Processor: no change. Device and driver: streaming
      units behind the ring engine, one request kind. For: serves APL and K, graphics work and
      the element-wise parts of inference; the cheapest hardware; it is the engine the author's
      R277 describes. Against: a single pass is memory-bound, so the processor loop is within a
      factor of two of it (50 against 83 million elements a second, J.8.1), and the benefit
      depends on fusion and on offloading the processor, not on speed.
   D) A standard PCI Express GPU or NPU card driven through virtio, and no engine of the pdp64's
      own. The accelerator is whatever card has a driver (R278). Processor: no change. Device
      and driver: the card's own command formats. For: no hardware to design. Against: it
      contradicts 8.4.1, because a card is optional and its command set is its maker's, not the
      architecture's, unless the card implements the architecture's engine interface (a
      possibility only if it speaks the descriptor of J.8.2); the Linux accelerator subsystem
      shows each such card with its own driver and its own user-space stack (AMD, Qualcomm and
      Rockchip accelerators are documented in Documentation/accel, read), and the virtio headers
      define no compute device (J.8.2), so the card is not driven through virtio today.
   E) No silicon yet: the architecture specifies the engine, and the first implementation is
      software. A core assigned by the kernel or the processor itself runs the operation set
      behind the same descriptors and the same virtio queue; hardware engines follow when a
      measurement justifies one. Processor: no change. Device and driver: a virtio device
      implemented in software; the driver is the same as for hardware. For: the interface is
      settled and exercised before any silicon is committed; it satisfies "every system has the
      engine" at once; QEMU already implements devices of other classes as software behind
      virtio (read). Against: no accelerator to measure the decision against; a software engine
      is no faster than the loop it replaces and may be slower.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option E first, then option C: specify the engine and
   implement it in software, so that the descriptor and the operation set are exercised by
   programs, then build the element-wise engine with fusion as the first hardware, with LB64
   (option A) as an optional set of J.8.1's option 6 and the matrix engine when a batch workload
   exists to feed it.
   PRECEDENTS: Verified: the Linux accelerator documentation and its three categories; the LB64
   numbers (the proposal's own); QEMU's software virtio devices. Unverified: ARM systems attach
   Ethos and Mali engines as memory-mapped devices; Google's TPU is a PCI Express card; the
   PDP-11's attached array processors took library calls and a fixed function list.
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "what would a gpu look like that is optimized purely for samsung's
   littlebit-2 encoding" (R136); "if we can plug a regular card into the bus, why not?" (R278);
   "for now we will have a standard pcie set of audio/video devices" (R315); "just optimize
   inference whether in cpu or gpu whatever makes most sense" (R139); and the 2026-11-10 words
   on the standard part (errata 131).
   BODY: Statement 8.4.8.

DECISION: 
NOTE:
