ENTRY J.8.5
ORDER: 17   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 8 Vector work and the accelerator
   TITLE: Operations on regions of memory in the instruction set
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (registers)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author asked (2026-11-10, errata 131): "how does simd work, how many instructions
   are there, is simd just normal ops fused with a 'simd' bit or something like that? would it
   make sense to have simd ops that work on regions of memory instead of register files, since
   we'd be load/storing register files anyways? I guess then we'd be invisibly load/storing into
   a specialized register file of a size which user code doesn't need to worry about." Already
   decided: the processor has no vector register file and no SIMD instruction set (errata 59,
   8.3.1), and the wide work goes to the standard engine (8.4.1). [...]  (Full text: Appendix J,
   J.8.5.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The answer decides whether the instruction set gains a group of instructions
   (opcodes, restartability, a rule for hidden state, an addition to the progress category of
   6.4.1) or the engine stays the only path for wide work, and whether short arrays, which the
   engine's setup cost makes slow (8.4), have a fast path on the [...]
   FURTHER: How SIMD works. The idea. A SIMD instruction (single instruction, multiple data)
   applies one operation to several numbers at once. The numbers sit side by side in one wide
   register; each slot is a lane. A 128-bit register holds four 32-bit lanes, and one paddd
   instruction of x86 adds four pairs, cutting the carry at every lane boundary:
   FURTHER: (a code example is omitted here; see the manual)
   FURTHER: The parts. A SIMD design has a register file of wide registers (SSE: 16 of 16 bytes;
   AVX-512: 32 of 64 bytes, with 8 mask registers, read in LLVM); element widths (the same
   register read as 16 bytes, 8 halves, 4 words or 2 doublewords, chosen by the instruction or
   by a state register); masks or predicates (a bit for each lane saying whether it takes part:
   [...]
   FURTHER: Why the families differ. The question each answers is where the register width
   lives. SSE, AVX and AVX-512 (x86), NEON (ARM) and AltiVec (PowerPC) put the width in the
   instruction set: 128, 256 or 512 bits, so a wider generation is new instructions, new state
   and new kernel code, and an old binary cannot use the new width (8.2). [...]
   FURTHER: How many instructions there are. Each operation, times each element width, times
   signed and unsigned, times saturating or wrapping, times register forms (vector with vector,
   with scalar, with immediate, masked or not) multiplies. The counts below were made from
   sources on this machine; the unit differs between sources, and each is stated.
   FURTHER: | Instruction set | Count | Unit and source | |---|---|---| | RISC-V vector
   extension | 407 patterns, 235 distinct operations; 362 patterns and 205 operations without
   the crypto, bit-manipulation and bfloat16 additions | patterns in QEMU 11.1.2 insn32.decode
   whose names begin with v; [...]
   FURTHER: The explosion is shown by one operation, the integer add of vectors. LLVM's x86
   target has 194 definitions whose names begin VPADD: eight mnemonics (vpaddb, vpaddw, vpaddd,
   vpaddq and the four saturating forms vpaddsb, vpaddsw, vpaddusb, vpaddusw), each at 128, 256
   and 512 bits, each from registers or from memory, and each plain, merge-masked or
   zero-masked, with a broadcast form for the 32- and 64-bit widths. [...]
   FURTHER: Is SIMD "the normal operations with a SIMD bit"? Not in any real design, with one
   partial exception; each design differs in precisely this respect.
   FURTHER: RISC-V V has its own major opcode (1010111, the same for all, read in the decoder)
   and its own operation fields, with names that echo the scalar ones (vadd, vand, vsll). Three
   forms (vv, vx, vi) and one mask bit. The element width and the vector length are not in the
   instruction: they are state set by vsetvli. So it is separate vector instructions that share
   the scalar names, with width as state.; [...]
   FURTHER: So the answer to the author's question is: partly, in x86, where a prefix reuses the
   opcode map; elsewhere the vector operations are a separate group with their own opcodes. A
   lane prefix is possible in the pdp64's prefix code (J.8.3, option 6).
OPTIONS:
  Options The five options of the author's brief, with what user code sees and what each costs.
     "Opcodes" counts mnemonics in the pdp64's terms: a leaf with a sub-opcode field of n bits
     holds up to 2 to the n of them (9.1.2), and the encoding is being re-optimised, so each
     count is measured again then:
   A) (a) Memory-to-memory vector instructions with a hidden internal buffer, restartable, with
      the progress in registers. One instruction takes destination, source and count in
      registers, for example VADD rd, rs, rt, rn; the processor streams the data through an
      internal buffer that no program can name and that lives only inside the instruction. User
      code sees: a block operation that does arithmetic; no vector register, no state across
      instructions. Context switch and faults: nothing to save beyond the registers, since the
      buffer is dead between instructions; a page fault stops it as MCPY stops, with the
      pointers and the count showing the position (progress, 6.4.1, 9.11.4), and an interrupt
      does the same. A reduction (a sum) keeps its partial result in a general register;
      floating-point sums depend on the order, so the architecture must name the order, as
      RISC-V names ordered and unordered sums (vfredosum, vfredusum, read). Precise exceptions:
      as for the block operations, consistent with element-at-a-time progress wherever an
      exception is taken (9.11.5). Memory ordering with other cores (chapter 7): each element
      access is an ordinary access in the single order (7.5.1); an implementation may read ahead
      only if it replays when a store from another core touches the data (7.7.3), and the rule
      for overlapping source and destination must say which element goes first (MMOV is
      overlap-safe; the forward-only rule is the cheaper). Caches and IOMMU: the accesses go
      through the processor's data cache and MMU, so they are coherent, and the IOMMU is not
      involved; a streaming operation pollutes the cache, which is the matter of J.9.8. Cost in
      opcodes: with the operation and format in the mnemonic, about 24 operations times 7
      formats (four integer widths, binary16, binary32, binary64) is 168 mnemonics, an 8-bit
      sub-opcode; with the format in a 3-bit field, 24 mnemonics and a 5-bit sub-opcode; with
      one instruction VEXEC rd that executes a descriptor in memory (the engine's descriptor,
      J.8.2), 1 or 2 mnemonics; Intel's LDTILECFG also loads a block of configuration from
      memory (its size is unverified). Compiler: a library or an intrinsic lowers to it, as
      memcpy lowers to MCPY; a loop-recognising vectoriser is easier than for SIMD because the
      count is a register (no tail loop, no alignment, no width). Processor: an instruction-set
      extension plus a sequencer and a buffer. Device and driver: none. For: no architectural
      state; short arrays have a fast path with no setup; uses the progress mechanism already
      decided. Against: the byte traffic above for chains; a long-running instruction (the
      question of J.9.7, the worst-case latency of a block operation, applies); the progress
      category grows.
   B) (b) The same operations executed by the standard engine through the descriptor route, with
      an instruction that submits and waits. The operations of J.8.1 run on the engine (decided
      direction, 8.4.4); the processor gets at most two conveniences: an atomic submit
      (ENQCMD-like: one 64-byte store to the doorbell with a success flag) and a wait (the WFE
      of 7.10 already waits for an event). User code sees: a library call; or an instruction
      VSUBMIT and the existing wait. Context switch and faults: no processor state; the engine
      cannot take a page fault, so the buffers are pinned and mapped for the device (5.10, 2.11)
      and a process's pending requests need the kernel's attention at exit. Precise exceptions:
      none involved; completion is by status. Memory ordering: by FENCE and the doorbell
      (7.5.7); the buffers are uncached (5.10.1), so data the processor produced in cached
      memory is not what the engine reads unless it was written to uncached memory, and a result
      is read back slowly. Caches and IOMMU: device addresses through the IOMMU (2.11), each
      device its own domain. Cost in opcodes: 0, or 1 for the atomic submit. Compiler: none;
      runtime and library. Processor: no change, or one instruction. Device and driver: the
      engine of J.8.4. For: no change to the processor; it is the decided path; nothing hidden.
      Against: the setup and the uncached hand-off make short arrays slower than a scalar loop;
      the processor cannot use it from a signal handler or the kernel without the driver.
   C) (c) A vector register file of implementation-defined size, behind an instruction set that
      never names its length. This is what the author's last sentence describes: loads and
      stores into a hidden register file whose size the program need not know, the VLA idea
      applied to memory regions. Instructions VLOAD (region into the hidden file), VOP (operate
      in the file), VSTORE, and a length query. User code sees: vectors of unknown length, a
      loop that asks how many elements it may do (strip mining, 8.2); the registers themselves
      are still architectural state, though unnamed in length. Context switch and faults: the
      hidden file is live across instructions, so it must be saved, which needs an instruction
      to save and restore it and a way to ask its size. The precedents show the price: ARM SVE's
      registers go up to 256 bytes each (32 of them, 8,192 bytes plus predicates; QEMU's limit,
      read); SME's ZA array is up to 256 by 256 bytes, 64 KiB (read); Intel AMX's tiles are 8
      KiB, and Linux does not enable them by default: the first use traps, because the 2 KB
      minimum signal stack that old programs compile in is too small for the state (xstate.rst,
      read); RISC-V made vector use an opt-in per thread (prctl, vector.rst, read); for
      comparison the FP11's state is 50 bytes (8.1) and a 32-register file of 16 bytes is 512.
      Interrupts must save or defer the file; a page fault in VLOAD or VSTORE must restart
      without corrupting it. Precise exceptions: the file is a visible state after every
      instruction, so each instruction must be precise; a long VOP needs progress too. Memory
      ordering: as option A for each load and store. Caches and IOMMU: as option A. Cost in
      opcodes: RISC-V's vector set is 362 patterns without extensions (read); a restricted
      design is 40 to 60 (loads, stores, 20 operations, reductions, shuffles, the length and the
      save). Compiler: LLVM already vectorises for RISC-V V and SVE (their targets are in the
      LLVM sources, read); the ABI needs a rule for the hidden file in calls and signals.
      Processor: a second register file in hardware, the thing 8.3.2 declined. For: the standard
      shape of modern vector machines; keeps intermediates in registers. Against: it is the
      decision of errata 59 reversed in all but name (a register file the program cannot size
      but must save), with the kernel and ABI work that decision avoided. Whether a file that is
      "invisible" counts as a vector register file in the sense of errata 59 is the author's to
      say.
   D) (d) Fixed-width SIMD. 128-bit or 256-bit lanes in a register file, as SSE, AVX2 and NEON.
      Cost in opcodes: 925 in the AVX family of x86 and 1,546 mnemonics in all of A64 (LLVM,
      read); a width in every instruction. Context switch: 256 bytes (SSE) to 2,112 bytes
      (AVX-512: 32 registers of 64 bytes and 8 mask registers). Rejected by the author's
      decision (errata 59, 8.3.3); listed for completeness. For: no setup, the largest installed
      base of software. Against: all of 8.3.2 and 8.3.3.
   E) (e) None: scalar loops and the engine only. User code sees: loops, MCPY and its kin, the
      bit instructions (9.9), and the engine for wide work. Cost in opcodes: 0. For: nothing to
      build; the decision stands as it is. Against: short arrays are scalar loops, and every
      wide operation pays the engine's setup.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B (b) stays the path for wide work, as decided.
   Region operations are a sound idea and a real design (the STAR-100 was one, and the pdp64's
   block operations are the same mechanism without arithmetic), but their weakness is the 24
   bytes an element and operation, which fusion cures only by turning the instruction into a
   small program, and at that point it is the engine's descriptor executed by the processor. If
   the author wants a fast path for short arrays without the engine's setup, the form to
   consider is VEXEC of option A (one instruction, a descriptor in memory, progress in
   registers), measured against the loops of J.8.3's corpus count first. Options C and 4 are the
   register file that errata 59 declined.
   PRECEDENTS: Verified in sources on this machine. Memory-to-memory data movement by
   descriptor: Intel DSA's 17 operations (idxd.h). The pdp64's own block operations and x86 REP
   MOVS with fast-string microcode, and ARM's CPYP, CPYM and CPYE (LLVM AArch64 defines the CPYE
   family; chapter 9 cites FEAT_MOPS). Register groups: RISC-V LMUL (the whole-register forms
   for 1, 2, 4 and 8 registers: vl1re8, vl2re8, vl4re8, vl8re8, read). Hidden state and its
   price: AMX tiles (8 KiB, xstate.rst), SME's ZA (up to 64 KiB, QEMU), SVE's registers,
   RISC-V's opt-in, the lazy-switch leak of x86 recorded in 8.2. Unverified. The CDC STAR-100
   (1974) and Cyber 205 were memory-to-memory vector machines, with vectors up to tens of
   thousands of elements and a start-up cost per instruction that made short vectors slower than
   scalar code; [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "how does simd work, how many instructions are there ... would it make
   sense to have simd ops that work on regions of memory instead of register files" (errata
   131); "the simd/avax stuff" (R136); "mmap device arm style route" (R277). The block
   operations and their progress rule are the decided statements 9.11.3 to 9.11.5.
   BODY: Statement 8.3.5.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.9.1
ORDER: 18   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: The conditional select and the expansions of ABS, MIN, MINU, MAX and MAXU
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (select and min/max words, status word or flags)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A conditional select is an instruction that picks one of two values by a condition,
   with no branch (x86 CMOV, ARM64 CSEL). The pdp64 has none, except the pair MASKEQZ and
   MASKNEZ (each tests one register against zero and gives either zero or the other operand: rd
   = rc == 0 ? 0 : ra, and the opposite), and SET cc, rd, which turns a flag condition into 0 or
   1. The author has decided that ABS, MIN, MINU, MAX and MAXU are assembler aliases (words the
   assembler expands into other instructions), not native instructions, and that the manual
   entry of each shows the alias (errata 98). [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.9.1.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Until the expansion is fixed the entries of the five words cannot show it
   (section 9.7.6). Kernel code uses clamps and minimum and maximum in size computations, and a
   constant-time select is wanted for checking message-authentication codes and handling keys.
   [...]
   FURTHER: Counting convention. CMP, MOVQ, BIS and NEG between registers are 2 bytes; SET cc,
   MASKEQZ, MASKNEZ, BSEL and any new select are 4 bytes. A native MIN is 1 instruction of 4
   bytes. These are estimates to be checked against the assembler.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A flag-reading select, CSEL cc, ra, rb, rd. One new 4-byte instruction that reads N, Z, C
      and V and picks ra or rb. MIN is CMP a,b ; CSEL lt,a,b,d (2 instructions, 6 bytes); ABS is
      NEG a,t ; CSEL mi,a,t,d (2 or 3 instructions). One select also serves floating-point
      values, which live in the general registers. For: the natural select for a machine with
      flags; the shortest alias of the real options; 15 condition words fit in the 7-bit
      sub-opcode space of the three-register group (51 of 128 used). Against: after CMP the
      flags describe the comparison, not the result, so N and Z are wrong and V and C are the
      comparison's: the flag rule above breaks unless a TST is added (3 instructions, 8 bytes);
      needs a flag read on the select unit; a second select beside MASKEQZ multiplies entities
      (Ockham, the author's rule: do not multiply entities without benefit); effort in the
      assembler, simulator, compilers and both manuals; 250 LUTs or less (estimate).
   B) SET plus MASKEQZ and MASKNEZ, no new instruction. The aliases expand over what the author
      already adopted (errata 85). MIN a,b,d is CMP a,b ; SET lt,c ; MASKEQZ a,c,t ; MASKNEZ
      b,c,d ; BIS t,d (5 instructions, 16 bytes, two scratch registers). ABS needs no select:
      ASR a,#63,m ; XOR m,a,d ; SUB m,d (3 instructions, about 6 bytes, V not set at the
      minimum). For: no new opcode or flag reader; one select for everything; constant time by
      construction; no gates. Against: 4 more instructions and 12 more bytes per MIN or MAX site
      (on the corpus about 890 instructions and 2.7 KB, about 0.3% of instructions, an estimate
      from amd64 counts); C is changed; the assembler needs scratch registers and nothing is
      reserved except R12, which is the linker's, so an alias that silently uses a register is a
      strange side effect; five hidden instructions behind one word.
   C) A register-conditioned select, SELECT rc, ra, rb, rd. rd = rc != 0 ? ra : rb, three source
      registers in one instruction (the four-register 4-byte form exists: FMADD has four
      register fields). MIN is CMP a,b ; SET lt,c ; SELECT c,a,b,d (3 instructions, 10 bytes);
      MASKEQZ(a,c) is SELECT c,a,zero, so it replaces the two mask instructions or sits beside
      them. For: a constant-time select in 1 instruction instead of 3; no flag read. Against: it
      reopens the adoption of MASKEQZ (errata 85) or adds a third entity; MIN still costs 3
      instructions and the flags of the sequence are the comparison's.
   D) Bit select, BSEL rm, ra, rb, rd, as the only select. BSEL is decided (9.9.6, 9.20.4); this
      option is to use it also as the select for the aliases and drop the mask pair's role in
      them. rd = (rm & ra) | (~rm & rb), with the mask made from the condition. MIN is CMP a,b ;
      SET lt,c ; NEG c,m ; BSEL m,a,b,d (4 instructions, about 14 bytes). For: nothing new,
      since the instruction is adopted (it is the Ch and Maj functions of SHA-2 and the
      constant-time swap); selects any width and bit pattern. Against: the scalar select is
      long, with a comparison, a SET and a negate before it; a four-register form.
   E) A two-operand conditional move, CMOVcc rs, rd. 2 bytes, destructive, reads flags (the x86
      shape). MIN a,b,d is MOVQ a,d ; CMP d,b ; CMOVgt b,d (3 instructions, 6 bytes, no scratch
      register). For: the smallest code of any select; the shape compilers know. Against: needs
      a 2-byte slot, and the last spare slot in the arithmetic group would be spent (a 2-byte
      form writes flags); a read-modify-write destination makes the instruction depend on the
      old rd even when it moves nothing, which out-of-order machines dislike; the flags after it
      are the comparison's.
   F) No select: a compare and a short branch over a move. MOVQ a,d ; CMP d,b ; Bgt over ; MOVQ
      b,d. A core may fuse a forward branch over one instruction into a single micro-operation,
      so the sequence costs nothing on cores that fuse and is promised nothing on cores that do
      not. For: nothing new in the architecture; 3 or 4 instructions. Against: it branches on
      data, so MIN, MAX and ABS cannot stay on the list of always-constant-time instructions,
      which contradicts the secure core of errata 85 and the author's "no timing channel
      attacks"; a data-dependent branch mispredicts at a rate that depends on the data; the
      flags are the comparison's.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B for the architecture: MASKEQZ and MASKNEZ stay
   the one select (errata 85), ABS expands without a select (ASR, XOR, SUB), and the manual says
   plainly that the five words change C and that the assembler needs a reserved temporary
   register. If MIN and MAX in hot loops prove to matter, Option A can be added later in the
   same sub-opcode block and the same aliases then shrink from 5 instructions to 2 without
   anyone rewriting source. This is the assistant's view, not a decision. It would change if a
   compile showed MIN or MAX inside hot loops, or if the author wants the alias as short as the
   native form (then Option A now).
   PRECEDENTS: ARM64 CSEL, CSINC, CSINV, CSNEG select by flags, and Arm's
   data-independent-timing guarantee lists conditional select among the instructions covered
   (verified); the architecture's own reserved slot for a flag-based CSEL in the secure-core
   proposal (verified).; x86 CMOVcc, SPARC V9 MOVcc, s390x LOCR, Alpha CMOVxx (unverified:
   general knowledge).; LoongArch maskeqz and masknez and RISC-V Zicond czero.eqz and czero.nez
   are exactly the mask pair; RISC-V's base set has no select (unverified: general knowledge).
   RISC-V Zbb has native min, minu, max, maxu and no abs (unverified).; MIPS MOVN and MOVZ, and
   Alpha CMOVxx (which tests a register against zero), are register-conditioned selects
   (unverified).; ARM NEON BSL, BIT, BIF and the x86 ternary-logic instruction VPTERNLOG are bit
   selects (unverified); BSEL in the secure-core design (verified).; [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: cmov, csel, fsel (no source machine named): "I am interested in the
   cmov/csel/fsel ... am happy for abs/min/max to be aliases on top of it in the assembler". On
   ABS, MIN and MAX as opcodes: "add them. and if we have to teach the front end about
   abs/min/max nodes that is fine ... surely other cpus have these opcodes", and later "why do
   them as single opcodes instead of as an opcode that takes a flag?" Ockham's rule: "don't
   multiply entities without benefit" and "no weird side effects, no bumping other opcodes in a
   way that makes code size increase". The PDP-11 ROL as an alias of ROR ("the assembler could
   take rol and map it to ror"). Floats live in the general registers. [...]
   BODY: Sections 9.7.6, 9.20.3 and 9.21.1; and the dictionary entries of ABS, MIN, MINU, MAX,
   MAXU and MASKEQZ.

ENTRY J.9.2
ORDER: 19   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: Constant-time execution: what holds while DIT is clear, and where DIT lives
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (status word or flags)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A timing channel is a leak of secret data through how long an instruction takes, when
   the time depends on the operand values. "Constant-time" means that the architecture promises
   that an instruction takes the same time whatever the data (data-independent timing, the
   property that cryptographic code needs). It is necessary but not sufficient: a
   secret-dependent branch, address or table index still leaks through the branch predictor and
   the cache. The author wants "a constant time flag or something so instructions always take an
   equal amount of time no matter the input"; [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.9.2.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The fast paths (an early exit from divide, from the block scan MSCN, from a
   multiplier) are gates and speed, and a fixed-latency multiplier is smaller than an early-out
   one. A core must be able to disable each fast path while DIT is set. [...]
   FURTHER: The position. Bit 13, the next free bit above UA, keeps the used bits together; bit
   15 leaves 13 and 14 for later. For bit 13: no gap in the status word, and the save and
   restore is one mask. Against: a later mode bit that the kernel wants next to UA will not have
   the place.
OPTIONS:
  Options (for what holds while DIT is clear):
   A) Nothing: DIT is the only promise. Every instruction may have fast paths, and code that
      needs constant time sets the bit. For: nothing more to specify; fast paths stay for normal
      code. Against: software forgets to set it, and a default of "insecure" is the one that a
      2026 kernel post calls unacceptable; an out-of-order core has to hold the promise through
      the whole pipeline and not only in the arithmetic unit.
   B) A published core list that is always constant-time, with DIT for the rest. The
      architecture lists the instructions that always take data-independent time (add, subtract,
      logic, shifts and rotates, MUL, MULH, MULHU, CLMUL, POPCNT, CLZ, BSWAP, BITREV, SET,
      MASKEQZ, MASKNEZ, and the expansions of ABS, MIN and MAX once J.9.1 fixes them); DIV, MOD,
      floating point, MCMP, MSCN, MSCNN and MVAL need the bit. For: the common case is free, the
      rare instructions are covered when asked, and software may rely on the list on every core;
      a fixed multiplier needs fewer gates. Against: two things to specify and test; the list
      must be kept as instructions are added; any expansion in J.9.1 that branches on data is
      off it.
   C) Every integer instruction always constant-time, a prohibition list, DIT for floating point
      and the scanners only. DIV always takes its worst-case time. For: the simplest to specify
      and verify; fewest gates in the fast-path logic. Against: an average divide is slower
      (about 1% over a program, and loops that divide on every iteration weigh many times more);
      MSCN loses its early exit; it contradicts "not a priority" for non-sensitive code.
   D) The promise on a class of core. The strong promise lives on the secure class of errata 85
      (identified by a class register; DIT reads 1 and is fixed on it; every instruction
      fixed-latency, caches private, speculation absent). On general cores DIT is a promise of
      the instructions in the list of option B, and a core says in a feature register what it
      cannot hold. For: the strongest guarantee where it is needed, covering the cache and the
      bus that a mode bit cannot reach; the general cores stay fast. Against: a second core
      design to build and verify; general cores that run secrets have no promise beyond the
      list.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B with the secure class of option D as the strong
   form, the published list written into the manual now, DIT saved with the status word and the
   compiler treating the list as untouchable; and bit 13. This is the assistant's view, not a
   decision. It would change if a cycle measurement showed divide in hot loops (option B stays,
   option C is dropped), or if hardware cannot cheaply disable a fast path (the instruction goes
   off the list).
   PRECEDENTS: Arm's DIT bit in the processor state, which Linux on arm64 enables in the kernel
   "to be cautious" (verified).; Intel's DOITM, with the vendor saying it does not recommend
   enabling it globally and kernel posts (July 2026) saying it is best left on (verified).;
   RISC-V scalar crypto extension Zkt: an implementer declares a subset of instructions with
   latency independent of the data values, a promise and not a mode (verified).; x86 before
   DOITM: Intel published a guidance list and later found that AES-NI and ADD and XOR varied on
   later parts, so the drift is the precedent against a list that is only published (verified).;
   The repository's own secure class, with DIT reading 1 and fixed on it, a class register, a
   reserved bus slot and a private window (verified).; [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The failures to avoid: "no hammer-row, no spectre, no timing channel
   attacks; there should be constant time flag or something so instructions always take an equal
   amount of time no matter the input". On cost: "I wonder how much that would slow a processor
   down ... for non-sensitive things, constant time isn't a priority". "Mode bits are case by
   case, but in general should be visible, not hidden state. putting modes in bits that are
   saved anyway sounds like a freebie." Cryptography on separate devices on the PCI bus, "so
   they can pay the price of constant time computation".
   BODY: Sections 9.3.7 and 9.21.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.9.3
ORDER: 20   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: The atomic group: where the operands go, and the size of CAS
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (encoding, atomics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The atomic group holds CAS, SWAP, XADD, XAND, XOR_, XXOR, XMAX, XMIN, XMAXU, XMINU, LL
   and SC, each with a _DB variant that adds a barrier (9.13.1). Their semantics, ordering and
   the reservation monitor are decided (chapter 7). Not decided is the shape of the instruction:
   each names an address, an operand and a place for the old value, and CAS also an expected
   value. The grammar of the encoding takes three registers for all but CAS and CASDB (four),
   with the address in a register, in the order written (errata 87, assumption 4), and the text
   has not confirmed that. SC has to say where its success flag goes. [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.9.3.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The assembler syntax, the compiler's lock and reference-count sequences, the
   operating system's lock code and every disassembler depend on the order of the operands, and
   a mistake is a lock that corrupts memory and not a fault.
OPTIONS:
  Options (the placement):
   A) Address in a register, three registers, a separate destination; four for CAS. XADD Ra, Rb,
      Rd: the old word at (Ra) goes to Rd, and Rb is added. CAS names expected, new, address and
      the destination of the old value. For: no operand is destroyed; one leaf shape for the
      group; the assumption of the grammars. Against: CAS needs a four-register leaf, which is
      six bytes.
   B) Three registers throughout; the expected value is also the destination of CAS. CAS Rd, Rb,
      (Ra): Rd holds the expected value and receives the old value. For: three registers for
      every member of the group; the form of RISC-V's AMOCAS and of ARM's CAS. Against: the
      expected value is destroyed, so a retry loop reloads it; one more rule to remember.
   C) Two registers, destructive. XADD (Ra), Rd: Rd is the operand and receives the old value.
      For: the shortest form, two or four bytes; the shape of x86's XADD. Against: CAS has no
      room for four values without an implicit register (refused, 9.17.3); the operand is
      destroyed.
   D) The address as the ordinary memory operand rm. For: reuses the addressing forms of the
      rest of the instruction set; the same shape as ADDM. Against: allows modes with side
      effects (auto-increment) that an atomic must refuse, and the forms are longer.
  The size of CAS (4 or 8 bytes):
   E) (a) Two words, CASL and CASQ, spelled like MOVL and MOVQ: For: the size is in the
      mnemonic, as for the moves; Against: two sub-opcodes, and the _DB variants double again.
   F) (b) A size bit in the sub-opcode or leaf, one mnemonic with a suffix: For: one entry;
      Against: the assembler must carry the suffix, which no other instruction of the group
      does.
   G) (c) CAS is always 8 bytes, and a 4-byte compare-and-swap is a CAS of an aligned pair that
      includes the neighbour: For: nothing to encode; Against: it makes a 4-byte lock word a
      8-byte critical value, and the span-two-fields case of 9.13.2 would have to be written
      differently.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A, which is what the grammars already assume,
   with the size as two words (a). This is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: RISC-V AMO instructions take rd, rs2, (rs1) and AMOCAS takes the expected value
   in rd (unverified: general knowledge); ARM64 LDADD and CAS take the compare value in a
   register that is overwritten with the old value (unverified); x86 LOCK XADD is destructive
   and CMPXCHG has the accumulator as an implicit operand (unverified); the VAX interlocked
   instructions (BBSSI, ADAWI, INSQHI) use memory operands (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "Only one memory operand per instruction" and no implicit registers as
   rules of the instruction set (9.2.2, 9.17.3); the author has not spoken on the order of the
   operands.
   BODY: Sections 9.13.2 and 9.21.1; the atomic entries of section 9.13.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.9.4
ORDER: 21   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: The checked arithmetic: the number of the CHECK vector and the register form
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (checked arithmetic, registers)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: ADDV, SUBV and MULV abort at the instruction, with EPC at it and the destination
   unwritten, when the signed result does not fit in 64 bits; TRAPCC takes the same CHECK vector
   as a trap (errata 85). Two things are not fixed. The vector table has no vector of that name:
   numbers 16 to 31 are free between bpt (15) and the interrupt base (32). And the grammars give
   the two-register form (Rd <- Rd op Rs, like ADD), where the reason for the fused forms was
   one non-destructive instruction in place of ADD and TRAPCC, which a three-register form (Rd
   <- Ra op Rb) serves.
   WHY IT MATTERS: The vector number is the index of the handler in the table that the kernel
   writes; software that handles an overflow (a language runtime that promotes an integer to a
   bignum) names it. The register form decides how long the instruction is and whether a copy is
   needed before it.
OPTIONS:
  Options (the vector):
   A) Vector 16, the first free number. For: keeps the fault group together; matches the number
      the study used. Against: a later fault of the same group has no number before the
      interrupt base without a gap rule.
   B) A number at the top of the free range (31). For: leaves 16 to 30 for faults not yet
      defined. Against: an arbitrary number that the first reader will ask about.
   C) No new vector: a TRAP-range number. The overflow enters as TRAP #n would. For: no hardware
      vector to define. Against: the hardware has to synthesise a system call for a fault, and a
      handler cannot tell an overflow from a real TRAP.
  Options (the form):
   D) Two registers, destructive (the grammars). For: the shape of ADD; two parcels. Against:
      the operand is destroyed, so a check that needs the old value copies it first.
   E) Three registers. For: non-destructive; the reason the fused forms exist. Against: a longer
      leaf (three register fields), where ADD and TRAPCC is four plus two bytes.
   F) Both. For: the compiler chooses. Against: two leaves for one idea.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Vector 16 and the three-register form, because the fused
   forms were accepted as the non-destructive case. This is the assistant's view, not a
   decision.
   PRECEDENTS: x86 INTO traps on the overflow flag and is not in 64-bit mode; MIPS ADD and ADDI
   trap on signed overflow while ADDU does not; the VAX has an integer-overflow trap enabled by
   a bit in the status word (all unverified: general knowledge).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: Errata 85: TRAPCC is a trap and not an abort, and the fused ADDV, SUBV
   and MULV carry the register-only cases; errata 84 item 9, "overflow-trapping arithmetic
   (leaning yes)".
   BODY: Section 9.21.3; the entries of OVF and TRAPCC in section 9.7 and 9.14.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.9.5
ORDER: 22   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: Addressing-mode gaps: the scaled-index displacement and a store through d(DP)
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (encoding, instruction forms)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The scaled-index mode d(Rn)(Rx*s) exists for the array of structures. Its shortest
   form (two parcels) has a displacement of 6 or 8 bits, so ±32 to ±128 bytes before it takes
   three parcels and a 22-bit displacement (9.5.1). The mode d(DP), the direct page that serves
   the thread pointer and the per-CPU pointer, has a load form of six bytes and no store form in
   the grammars (9.3.5).
   WHY IT MATTERS: Code that walks an array of records touches fields beyond 32 bytes and pays
   two more bytes for each; a thread-local store needs a separate address computation.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Leave both as they are. For: nothing changes; the three-parcel forms exist. Against: the
      cost is paid in the hot loop of the array of structures and in every thread-local store.
   B) Reduce the scale to one bit (x1 or x8) and give the bit to the displacement. For: one more
      bit of displacement. Against: the scales 2 and 4, which index 16- and 32-bit arrays, are
      lost; the compiler's best case (bs_ec's inner loop, 31% fewer instructions) uses the
      scaled index.
   C) Add a two-parcel scaled-index leaf with hot registers for the base and the index. The
      register fields shrink, so the displacement grows. For: serves the common case with the
      registers the allocator prefers. Against: a prefix spent on one leaf; a compiler rule for
      which registers qualify.
   D) Add a store form through d(DP), six bytes, one word for each size. For: symmetrical with
      the load; one instruction for a thread-local store. Against: spends prefix space on a form
      that the compiler may use rarely.
   E) Add two-parcel d(DP) forms with a 12-bit displacement for loads and stores. For: most
      thread-local and per-CPU blocks are small; four bytes instead of six. Against: the 28-bit
      load form still stands beside it.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A until a compiled corpus shows how many array
   accesses lie beyond 32 bytes. This is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: x86 scaled index with a 32-bit displacement; AArch64 scaled register offset with
   no displacement and an unsigned scaled 12-bit offset; the VAX index mode that follows any
   other mode (all unverified: general knowledge). The 6502's zero page is the ancestor of DP
   (stated in chapter 9).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The PDP-11 as the base ("update and refresh of the pdp-11 instruction set
   and architecture"); Ockham's rule: "don't multiply entities without benefit".
   BODY: Sections 9.5.3 and 9.21.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.9.6
ORDER: 23   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: The page-bit mechanisms and the six reserved system words
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (opcodes, encoding)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author approved three mechanisms "to be specified" (errata 84): a page bit that
   gates landing pads, so that an indirect transfer that does not land on LPAD faults, with the
   fault attributed to the branch (item 5); a page bit that says a system call may be made from
   this page (item 8); and a stack-limit register checked when a system call or a fault is taken
   (item 4). The grammar of the encoding reserves six system words, SYSPG0 to SYSPG5, for these
   mechanisms, and the retire-stage study found that they need no opcode. What the words do, or
   whether they are released, is not said; [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.9.6.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The bits and registers must be in the page-table entry and the
   system-register list before the kernel and the linker are written, and software must be told
   not to use the six words until they mean something.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) No opcodes: enable bits in a system register and enforcement bits in the page-table entry.
      The words stay reserved and abort as illegal. For: nothing to execute; matches the
      retire-stage study. Against: needs two page-table bits, which compete with the
      wide-physical-address format.
   B) One system word for each mechanism. SYSPG0 and the others set or clear the mechanism for
      the current address space. For: explicit, and a privileged instruction is easy to trap.
      Against: a mode that an instruction changes (the rule of 9.2.5 is that modes are visible
      state).
   C) A page-attribute instruction that sets bits of the entry for an address. For: the kernel
      need not walk the table to change an attribute. Against: an instruction with a
      translation-buffer side effect, and the format of the entry must have room.
   D) Release the six words. For: the opcode space is freed. Against: the mechanisms approved by
      the author are still to be specified.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A, since the study found no opcode is needed, and
   the words released only when the mechanisms are specified. This is the assistant's view, not
   a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: Intel's indirect-branch tracking is enabled by a bit in a model-specific register
   and ends a branch on a missing ENDBR; ARM's branch-target identification is a page attribute
   in the translation-table entry (both unverified: general knowledge); OpenBSD's msyscall and
   pinsyscalls check where a system call comes from in software, and OpenBSD enforces landing
   pads by default where the hardware has them (appendix A, from the reports).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "Do it, no delay" for the colours of memory; landing pads gated by a page
   bit, "with the fault attributed to the branch"; "minimize recovery code".
   BODY: Sections 9.21.1, A.1.2, A.2.2 and A.3.2. The schedule of landing pads in the ABI is
   J.D.2.

ENTRY J.9.8
ORDER: 24   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: The block operations and the data cache
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (block operations and the cache)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A one-megabyte MCPY evicts the whole data cache for data that is never read again; it
   shows at once on a cache of 8 KiB. Every real memcpy on a machine with non-temporal stores
   uses them for this reason. An inference workload that moves tens of megabytes through a cache
   of a few kilobytes is the extreme case.
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel's working set is what a large copy destroys; and the fix is an
   opcode, a memory type or a rule inside the core, and only the first two are visible to
   software.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Do nothing: leave the cache alone. For: no new opcode or memory-type value. Against: the
      eviction is real.
   B) A non-temporal variant of the block operations. For: the program says that the data will
      not be read again. Against: a new sub-opcode and a rule that the data may not be cached.
   C) A streaming value of the memory-type field of the page-table entry. For: no opcode; a
      buffer is declared streaming once, and every access to it, by the block operations or not,
      follows. Against: the kernel must set it per mapping; a wrong type slows ordinary access.
   D) The implementation decides by length. For: invisible to software. Against: a performance
      cliff with no architectural visibility, the worst of the options.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option C, because the streaming workload is known by
   buffer, not by instruction. This is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: x86 non-temporal stores (MOVNT*) and fast-string moves that avoid the cache above
   a threshold; ARM non-temporal load and store pairs (all unverified: general knowledge).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The memory type as a page attribute (adopted from LoongArch, 9.20.2).
   BODY: Sections 9.11.5, 9.21.1 and A.9.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.9.9
ORDER: 25   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: The floating-point condition flags
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (status word or flags)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: FCMP writes the integer flags: N if the first operand is less than the second, Z if
   they are equal, V if they are unordered (either is a NaN), C cleared. The signed branches do
   not treat the unordered case as false: BLT after an unordered compare takes N xor V, which is
   1. The compiler must therefore avoid BLT and BLE for floating point. A reading of the flag
   rules by the assistant, not checked against the compiler: [...]  (Full text: Appendix J,
   J.9.9.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The compiler's rules for < and <= on floating point; the number of flag
   copies an out-of-order core must rename; the status word that is saved and restored.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Keep the integer flags and the swap rule. For: no change; the existing branches suffice.
      Against: the swap needs a register for the second operand; the shared flags stay a
      dependence.
   B) Two floating-point condition bits in the status word. For: separates floating-point
      compares from integer flags. Against: two more bits to save and restore, and a second set
      of branches or a condition mapping.
   C) Eight one-bit condition registers (LoongArch FCC). For: many independent comparisons.
      Against: new state to save at a context switch.
   D) A compare that writes 0 or 1 into a general register (RISC-V feq, flt, fle). For: no flags
      at all; the result feeds the one-parcel compare-and-branch against zero; fits floating
      point in the general registers. Against: a compare and a branch are two instructions, as
      FCMP and a branch are now.
   E) A fused floating-point compare-and-branch. For: one instruction; no flags. Against: a leaf
      with two registers and a displacement, six bytes in the common case.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A now, as it costs nothing, with option D the one
   to take if the flag dependence is measured to matter. This is the assistant's view, not a
   decision.
   PRECEDENTS: LoongArch has eight FCC registers (stated in the instruction-set text; unverified
   here); RISC-V's floating-point comparisons write an integer register (unverified: general
   knowledge); ARM64 sets NZCV from FCMP so that unordered makes the ordered conditions false
   (unverified: general knowledge).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "Floating point is done in the general registers" (9.2.7); the rule that
   no instruction writes part of {N, Z, V} (9.3.4).
   BODY: Sections 9.12.6, 9.20.3 and 9.21.1; the entry of FCMP.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.9.10
ORDER: 26   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: Directed rounding and the remaining half-precision operations
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (opcodes, instruction forms)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: Directed rounding. IEEE 754 requires rounding toward positive infinity, toward
   negative infinity and toward zero, as well as to nearest, for add, subtract, multiply,
   divide, square root, fused multiply-add and the conversions. The manual decides that there is
   no rounding-mode register or bit and that the rounding is in the opcode wherever it is not
   nearest-even (9.12.5); but no arithmetic opcode takes a rounding mode, only the FRINT family
   does. A conforming C library keeps the dynamic mode of fesetround in thread-local storage and
   dispatches on it. Half precision. [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.9.10.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Interval arithmetic and the C99 <fenv.h> conformance depend on directed
   rounding; machine-learning code on bfloat16.
OPTIONS:
  Options (directed rounding):
   A) None: software emulates the modes. For: nothing to add; the library's dispatch is the
      present answer. Against: it is slow, and the letter of the C standard is not met by
      hardware.
   B) A two-bit static rounding field in the instruction. The FESC two-register forms have a
      free nibble; the four top-level operations have none. For: the mode is in the opcode as
      9.12.5 says. Against: only the escape forms can carry it.
   C) Extra opcodes for the six core operations (add, subtract, multiply, divide, square root,
      fused multiply-add) in each of the three directions. For: no field. Against: eighteen
      opcodes.
  Options (half precision):
   D) Nothing more: the existing conversions and arithmetic. For: no cost. Against: the library
      does the rest through binary32.
   E) The missing half operations except bfloat16. For: a complete binary16 set. Against: tables
      for a format little used outside machine learning.
   F) Add bfloat16 conversions, or the arithmetic as well. For: the machine-learning format.
      Against: another format, and the accelerator of chapter 8 does the volume work.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A for directed rounding until a libm port asks,
   and option D for half precision. This is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: RISC-V's floating-point instructions carry a static rounding-mode field with a
   dynamic value; ARM has a rounding-mode field in FPCR; x86 AVX-512 has embedded rounding
   control; ARM and x86 have bfloat16 conversions and dot products (all unverified: general
   knowledge).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "No rounding-mode or precision mode bits" (9.12.5), the rule that a mode
   bit is by default visible state set by an instruction that names it (9.2.5).
   BODY: Sections 9.12.6 and 9.21.1; the entry of the half-precision conversions.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.9.11
ORDER: 27   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: The coherence protocol beyond four cores, and the verification of the memory model
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (memory model beyond four cores)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The memory model is sequentially consistent (9.2.8, decided), and the machine has at
   least four cores with sixteen or more as the preferred ceiling that no decision may preclude
   (errata 92). The protocol chosen for two to four cores is snooping (invalidation by watching
   a shared bus, the simplest protocol); at eight or more cores a snooping bus is the wrong
   protocol, and the one for sixteen is not chosen. Separately, a declaration of sequential
   consistency is not an implementation: [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.9.11.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The cache and its protocol must be chosen before the cache is built, not
   retrofitted; the litmus testing is a verification task that belongs in the plan before
   silicon.
OPTIONS:
  Options (the protocol):
   A) Snooping up to four cores, and the ceiling is four. For: the smallest design. Against:
      contradicts the sixteen of errata 92.
   B) Snoop filters or a directory per cache slice. For: scales to sixteen and beyond; the usual
      answer. Against: a large structure and a protocol with many states.
   C) Clusters of four that snoop, joined by a directory. For: small clusters stay simple.
      Against: two protocols to verify.
   D) Software coherence between clusters. For: cheap in hardware. Against: fragile, and it
      breaks the sequentially consistent model across clusters.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option C or option B, decided before the cache is
   designed; the litmus tests run on the model first. This is the assistant's view, not a
   decision.
   PRECEDENTS: x86 snoop filters and directories across sockets, ARM coherent interconnects
   (unverified: general knowledge).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "yeah let's have 4 cores minimum, 16 or more preferred" (errata 92).
   BODY: Section 9.21.1.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.9.12
ORDER: 28   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: The poison bit, the user-qualified moves and the cryptographic words: what is not yet
   specified
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (opcodes, select and min/max words)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author decided these instructions and left their details: the deferred-fault and
   user-qualified loads and stores (.D and .U: the three leanings of item 10 and the five of
   item 18 of docs/study-proposals.md, accepted in errata 86), the AES and SHA words of the
   secure core (errata 85: "or whichever opcodes would speed up and enable aes and sha"; the
   design approved in errata 86), and BSEL. What is not written down: [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.9.12.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel's copy routines, the dispatcher's test of argument registers for
   poison, the compiler's hoisting of loads and the crypto library each depend on the exact
   semantics; [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options (the result of the 32-bit SHA words):
   A) Zero-extended (the study). For: the value is a hash word, not a signed number, and a
      following 64-bit add of two such words does not need a mask. Against: it breaks the rule
      that every 32-bit result is sign-extended, so a compiler special-cases the words.
   B) Sign-extended, like RORI32. For: one rule for every 32-bit result. Against: a 32-bit hash
      word that is later used as a 64-bit unsigned value needs MOVZL first.
  Options (the poison of a deferred load):
   C) A poison bit beside each register, saved with it. For: precise and deterministic, as the
      author asked of the taint item ("no false positives"); the register file is small.
      Against: a bit for each register in the context switch and the exception frame; a store of
      a poisoned register must fault or carry it.
   D) A reserved value in the register. For: no new state. Against: the value cannot be a data
      value any more; a data-dependent condition that the author refused (a strange side
      effect).
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A, because the study chose it and the words feed
   32-bit additions in any case, and option C, because the author already leans to it (errata
   84, item 10). This is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: RISC-V's scalar cryptography extension (aes64es, sha256sig0, the zero-extended
   32-bit result) and the Itanium deferred-exception token (NaT) with ld.s, which is exactly a
   poison bit per register (unverified: general knowledge); Arm's LDTR and STTR for the
   user-qualified access (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "Constant-time AES and SHA instructions" for the secure core (errata 85);
   "i like your design. approved." and, of the select instructions, "looks good" (errata 86); of
   item 10, the three leanings accepted; of item 18, the five leanings accepted; of the secure
   core, "no timing channel attacks".
   BODY: Sections 9.6.7, 9.9.7 and 9.21.3; the dictionary entries of MOVD, MOVUSER, AESR, AESK
   and SHA256.

ENTRY J.A.1
ORDER: 29   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: Appendix A
   TITLE: The canonical-address rule and the pointer bits that colours spend
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (pointer bits and the address check)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A virtual address is canonical when its bits above the translated width repeat the top
   translated bit (39, 48 or 57 bits of address, 9.2.10); a non-canonical address aborts. A
   64-bit pointer therefore has 25 spare bits with three levels of page table, fewer with four
   or five, and the rule today refuses software any use of them. The author approved granule
   colouring and reserved colours for uninitialised and freed memory (errata 84, items 1 and 2),
   and "to be specified": a colour travels in the pointer, so the hardware, not software, must
   define and check those bits.
   WHY IT MATTERS: The rule is stated in the architecture, in the kernel's address-space layout,
   in the compiler's pointer arithmetic and in the debugger; a colour that software may set
   changes all four. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Keep the canonical rule; colours live outside the pointer. The colour of a granule is a
      table in memory, indexed by address, and a check compares the table entry against a colour
      register. For: the rule stands; no pointer bits spent. Against: a table lookup on every
      access that is checked; more memory traffic.
   B) The colour travels in a fixed field of the spare bits, ignored by translation and checked
      by the hardware. For: no table; the check is a compare. Against: every pointer use must
      mask (the hardware, as on ARM, can ignore the field in the address path); the canonical
      rule is restated; the field shrinks with each level of page table added.
   C) The colour field depends on the translation width. Three levels leave the 25-bit field,
      five levels fewer, and the width is read from a register (J.6.1). For: uses what each
      configuration has. Against: software that depends on the field must read the width first.
   D) Choose the field with the page-table level, once: the smallest field that every
      configuration has. For: one rule. Against: wastes the extra bits of the narrow
      configurations.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option C, because the width is read anyway for the
   address space (J.6.1). This is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: ARM's top-byte-ignore, pointer authentication and the memory tagging extension
   (unverified: general knowledge); SPARC's application data integrity colours the pointer and
   the memory (unverified); the study of item 3 of the retire-stage candidates, which considered
   the same bits for a capability bit (verified: docs/retire-stage-candidates.md, cited in
   errata 84).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "Do it, no delay" for granule colouring; the capability bit "asked
   whether it can be done with 64-bit pointers and flags outside the 64 usable bits" (errata 84,
   item 3).
   BODY: Section A.5.2.

DECISION:

   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (privileged or system registers)

   reads one block into low memory and starts core 0. The assistant's sketch of the first pages
   of physical memory (the body states only that they hold the boot code and boot data, with the
   vector table; errata 102) has the vector table, the block, the information block and the
   stack: (figure omitted) The vector table is at address 0 because VBR is zero at reset. The
   pdp64's block 0, the 512 bytes the ROM reads from the disk, goes to 0x1000, an address still
   marked "to be chosen" (docs/boot.md), so the first stage runs above the table. The
   boot-information block (memory ranges, core count, the address of the root complex, the boot
   device, the console, a checksum and a version, 4.6.2) is pointed to by r1; its size and place
   are not specified. r14, the stack pointer, is the top of the first 64 KB of memory. The 64 KB
   of r14 is only the stack's place.
OPTIONS:

      assistant's, not yet the author's.

ENTRY H.7.2
ORDER: 32   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 7 Multiple processors
   TITLE: 7.2 and 7.11 Commentary
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (memory ordering)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: Total store order as the retreat. If a large out-of-order core is
   ever built, total store order is the least painful retreat from sequential consistency (from
   the commentary of 7.2). The decision is reversible in the direction that matters: total store
   order can be the retreat for a large core (from the commentary of 7.11). The smp-decisions
   record leans to writing the retreat down now as a one-way, announced switch for the whole
   machine, with no per-core or per-page weaker mode.
OPTIONS:

      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.

ENTRY H.8.3.5
ORDER: 33   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 8 Vector work and the accelerator
   TITLE: 8.3.5 POPCNT and XNORPOP are not what the accelerator uses:...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (POPCNT and XNORPOP as instructions)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 8.3.5 POPCNT and XNORPOP are not what the accelerator uses: its
   activations are 16-bit floating-point numbers, and the processor's bit instructions (8.5.2 of
   the chapter) serve the binary-activation case. Commentary . Chain. PDP-11: heavy numerical
   work went to an attached array processor (8.1). Neural-network engines of today: a ring of
   commands and data by DMA (8.2). A ring of descriptors in memory, each with an ownership bit,
   is how the DEUNA Ethernet controller of 4.3BSD worked (if_de.c, chapter 5), and the partition
   into small single-purpose engines fed by chains is the idea of the 144-processor GA144 chip
   applied to a workload that suits it (unverified). Trade-offs. The accelerator is fast at what
   it does and does nothing else: attention is not helped, the element-wise work needs another
   engine, and a training accelerator is a different design again. The accelerator's descriptor
   is larger than the 16-byte virtio descriptor of chapter 5 (5.4.2), so the two must be
   reconciled; how the two are reconciled is the open item J.8.2, whose options include a
   device-defined work block that the ring points to and a command header in the first buffer of
   the chain, as virtio-blk and virtio-gpu do. Benefits. The lane count is set by the memory
   bandwidth and not by the amount of logic, so a small board can run a model the processor
   alone could not. Modern comparables. ARM's Ethos engines, Google's TPU, and the inference
   accelerators on small boards (unverified). Impact. Kernel: [... full text in Appendix H]
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

   TITLE: The instruction entries held back from chapter 9

------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: The entries below were in the instruction chapter, in the section

   the composition. Loads only, base and indexed operands. Why it is here. See MOVD and MOVUSER.
   The record gives no separate reason for the combination; it is listed because both qualifiers
   exist and a probe of a user pointer wants both.

ENTRY H.9.s-msha-hash-whole-blocks-of-memory
ORDER: 41   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: MSHA: Hash whole blocks of memory
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (opcodes, encoding)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: Mnemonics: MSHA256, MSHA512 (section 9.11 of the instruction
   chapter) Syntax (a code example is omitted here; see the manual) Operation (a code example is
   omitted here; see the manual) Flags None written. Exceptions Memory operand: page fault,
   protection fault, alignment fault or bus error (a read of non-existent memory is a precise
   abort with cause BUSFAULT). Every such fault is precise and the instruction restarts after
   it, because the register update of an auto-increment or auto-decrement mode is held until the
   instruction retires. Stage 2 of the crypto proposal (docs/crypto-secure-core.md section 3.4):
   the whole-buffer form, in the manner of the s390 CPACF instructions, to be built "only after
   measurement"; the grammars reserve its encoding. The unit would need about 2,000 look-up
   tables and 1,000 flip-flops (the proposal's estimate). Why it is here. Stage 2 of the crypto
   proposal, in the manner of the s390 CPACF instructions (KIMD and kin): one instruction hashes
   a buffer, the padding and chaining are done in hardware and so cannot be got wrong, and
   progress is held in registers, so a fault or an interrupt restarts it, as for MCPY. The
   estimate is 64 cycles for a 64-byte SHA-256 block against about 1,200 instructions, roughly
   18 times; the cost is gates in proportion to the algorithms frozen into opcodes. The proposal
   says to build MSHA256 first and the others only after measurement, and the author has not
   decided it.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

DECISION:

   one-parcel leaves would remove them. Commentary. Precedent. AArch64's W forms and RISC-V's

   of opcodes in the page-2 map and a little ALU decode.

ENTRY H.A.10.1
ORDER: 44   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: Appendix A
   TITLE: A.10.1 A small set of lane operations (add, subtract, saturating add, compare,...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (lane operations: new instructions)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: A.10.1 A small set of lane operations (add, subtract, saturating
   add, compare, minimum, maximum, average, sum of absolute differences, shuffle on 8, 16 and
   32-bit lanes) in the existing registers could speed up string, codec and image loops.
   Commentary. Precedent. Alpha's motion-video instructions and PA-RISC's multimedia
   instructions did exactly this, in integer registers. Trade-offs. It adds no state and little
   hardware, but it is 64 bits wide so the gain per instruction is modest, and compilers use it
   poorly. Verdict. Collect the evidence first: the compiler corpus was built at a size setting
   that hides how much vectorisable code there is (appendix B, S1). Impact. Kernel: String and
   checksum routines get faster. Compiler: Vectorisation into integer lanes is weak, so the
   library routines would be hand-written. Hardware: A lane-aware ALU: little extra hardware and
   no new state.

      assistant's, not yet the author's.

ENTRY H.B.1.2.1
ORDER: 46   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: Appendix B
   TITLE: B.1.2.1 The proposal is RDTIME rd, an unprivileged read of a free-running,...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (privileged or system registers, named instructions)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: B.1.2.1 The proposal is RDTIME rd, an unprivileged read of a
   free-running, constant-rate, monotonic counter. Commentary. Precedent. The PDP-11 and VAX
   kept the time in memory updated by a line clock and read it with a system call. Trade-offs. A
   user-readable counter is a timing side channel, so the kernel needs an enable bit; the
   counter's width and rate become architecture. Benefits. A clock read without a trap, which is
   what a fast clock_gettime in a shared page needs. Modern comparables. x86 RDTSC, RISC-V
   rdtime, PowerPC mftb, s390 STCKF, MIPS RDHWR, LoongArch rdtime; the x86 counter's history of
   unreliability is the argument for fixing monotonicity and constant rate at the start. Use.
   Timestamps, timeouts, and the fast path of every library's clock call. Impact. Kernel: A fast
   clock_gettime with no trap. Compiler: An intrinsic for the counter read. Hardware: One
   counter, a user-read enable bit and a SYSX decode.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.

   TITLE: B.1.3.1 The proposal is a block of read-only system registers (numbers 13 and...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (privileged or system registers)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: B.1.3.1 The proposal is a block of read-only system registers
   (numbers 13 and up) giving the version, the optional features, the cache geometry, the page
   sizes and the address width. Commentary. Precedent. The VAX had a system-identification
   register the kernel switched on; x86 has CPUID, which has grown hundreds of leaves and traps
   under virtualisation. Trade-offs. Each register's layout must be written once in a grammar so
   it cannot drift. Benefits. One binary adapts to the machine, and a kernel need not patch
   itself per model (OpenBSD carries 75 references to its boot-time patching). Modern
   comparables. AArch64's identification registers, RISC-V's misa. Impact. Kernel: The kernel
   need not patch itself per model. Compiler: Runtime selection of routines by feature bits.
   Hardware: A small block of read-only registers.

ENTRY H.B.1.4.1

   TITLE: B.1.4.1 The proposal is one new instruction, TLBIASID, that invalidates every...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (named instructions)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: B.1.4.1 The proposal is one new instruction, TLBIASID, that
   invalidates every translation of one address space, broadcast like TLBI. Commentary.
   Precedent. The VAX flushed process translations on every context switch and kept system ones;
   x86 added tags (PCID) and INVPCID much later. Trade-offs. A flush-all works until the first
   rollover of the tag numbers, after which every process loses its translations. Benefits.
   Process exit and tag reuse become cheap. Modern comparables. AArch64 TLBI ASIDE1IS, RISC-V
   sfence.vma with an address-space operand, x86 INVPCID. Impact. Kernel: Process exit and tag
   reuse become one broadcast. Compiler: None. Hardware: A tag compare in the translation
   buffer's invalidate path.

ENTRY H.B.1.5.1
ORDER: 49   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: Appendix B
   TITLE: B.1.5.1 The proposal is a rule and a test:...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (named instructions)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: B.1.5.1 The proposal is a rule and a test: every multi-access
   instruction (PUSHM, POPM, PUSHX, POPX, the block operations) is either all-or-nothing or safe
   to repeat from the start, and an emulator test interrupts each at every access and checks
   that a restart reaches the same state. Commentary. Precedent. The PDP-11 needed a status
   register (MMR1) and, on the 11/40, a kernel routine that decoded the faulting instruction to
   undo it; the VAX needed a first-part-done bit; ARM's load-multiple restarts with the base
   register restored. Trade-offs. The area below the stack pointer is dead memory, so a partial
   push is invisible; the risk is another observer (a device or core) seeing a half-written
   area. Benefits. The fault handler restarts blindly, with no instruction-specific cases, which
   is what recoverability means. Modern comparables. RISC-V has no multi-register instruction;
   ARM and the VAX restart as above. Impact. Kernel: The fault handler restarts every
   multi-access instruction blindly. Compiler: None. Hardware: Each multi-access instruction
   checks every address before acting, or is repeatable.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

   copy code; an in-order core with no data cache gains nothing. Modern comparables. AArch64

   warm the cache. Compiler: PREFETCH would need compiler heuristics. Hardware: A non-faulting
   load path with no destination; nothing on a core with no data cache.

ENTRY H.B.1.11.2

   TITLE: B.1.11.2 Decided (errata 59, chapter 8):...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (opcodes, registers)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: B.1.11.2 Decided (errata 59, chapter 8): data-parallel work lives
   in memory-mapped devices, and the processor has no vector register file and no SIMD
   instruction set. What remains is the number formats and operations of the devices (half
   precision, bfloat16, 8-bit integer dot products, 8-bit floating point), the reconciliation of
   the device descriptors with the standard descriptor, and whether packed integer lanes are
   added to the integer registers (A.10). Commentary. Precedent. The PDP-11 sent heavy numerical
   work to attached array processors; the VAX had a vector extension on its 6000 series, with 16
   vector registers of 64 elements, supported by VMS; neither Unix used it much. Trade-offs. A
   fixed-width vector file brings a second register file and its context-switch cost, opcodes,
   and a new width for every generation (the history of SSE, AVX and AVX-512); a scalable design
   runs one binary on a small and a large implementation at the price of the most complex
   specification. Lazy saving of vector state was a security hole on x86, and Linux went back to
   eager restore. Benefits. The coprocessor needs no opcodes and no processor state. Modern
   comparables. ARM SVE and RISC-V V (scalable), ARM NEON, x86 AVX, PowerPC VSX, Alpha MVI. Use.
   Neural-network inference (the LB64 accelerator), graphics, signal processing and the
   library's string and memory routines. The evidence needed first: [... full text in Appendix
   H]

   AFFECTS OPCODES: yes (opcodes)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   uncached, so no cache instruction is needed until a non-coherent device appears. Commentary.
   Precedent. The 11/70's cache watched DMA writes; the VAX's adapter had data paths to purge.

   three reserved sub-opcodes. Modern comparables. ARM, MIPS and embedded PowerPC with
   non-coherent DMA wrap cache maintenance in a DMA-mapping layer; x86 DMA is coherent. Impact.
   Kernel: Drivers need no cache code. Compiler: None. Hardware: No cache-maintenance
   operations.
