ENTRY J.3.2
ORDER: 83   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: The form of the terminal device and its name
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (device interface)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: On 2026-11-10 the author described "our virtio-console device" as "an attempt to bring
   the nice properties of the blit terminal and c64 for doing simple and easy peek poke style
   display of text and also doing graphics, but with modern color depths and screen sizes"
   (errata 105). That there is such a device, with peek and poke text and graphics and glyph
   slots, is decided (3.9.1); what is open is its form and its name. No document defines a
   device of that name: not its identification, its queues or its registers. The real
   virtio-console is a serial text device (device type 3; [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.3.2.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel needs one driver for the text display and one for graphics, and
   the author wants no more drivers than the standard demands (errata 78). Until the form is
   chosen, the terminal cannot be specified, the boot console cannot be named and the glyph-slot
   question (J.3.4) has no device to belong to.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A new virtio device type of the pdp64's own, with the cell array and the glyph slots as
      the device's memory. For: one device that matches the author's words; virtio's transport,
      queues and discovery are reused. Against: no stock host implements it, so the device
      model, the hardware and the driver must all be written; a number in the shared virtio
      registry needs care.
   B) virtio-gpu extended by a text layer and glyph slots. For: one display device, not two; the
      host's existing virtio-gpu driver still works for the parts it knows. Against: the
      extension is a fork of a shared specification; stock hosts ignore the new commands, so
      text would fall back to pixels.
   C) The real virtio-console as the text stream for existing tty programs (3.2), beside a
      separate display device. For: nothing to invent; every existing tty program works; it is
      the form the boot console already assumes. Against: it is a stream, so it gives none of
      the peek and poke the author wants; two devices must be coordinated.
   D) The native device family, renamed (the deferred devices of J.3.1). For: the closest match
      to the author's description; the C64 and Blit designs are kept. Against: errata 95
      abolished the register model they used; each one is one more driver.
   E) A library over virtio-gpu that offers the peek and poke look in the program's own memory
      (the fourth sketch of J.3.3), as a first realisation of the device. For: no new hardware
      or driver; runs now. Against: not yet the "device" the author names; the chip does not
      draw text while the program does other things.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option C for the console stream and Option E as the
   first display, with Option A or 2 as the device the author describes.
   PRECEDENTS: The Linux header virtio_console.h and the device-type list in virtio_ids.h (types
   3 for console, 16 for GPU, 18 for input, 25 for sound) (verified: the headers on this
   machine). The repository mentions virtio-console only as a candidate console for the boot ROM
   and kernel (5.7.1). Plan 9's draw device, a stream of small commands written to a file
   (unverified: general knowledge).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The Blit terminal and the C64; "peek poke style"; "glyph maps ... a ttf
   engine generate glyphs and they will go into their memory slots"; the author's earlier
   terminal chat of 2026-06-18, a separate project in which a cell holds colours, a character
   and attributes and existing tty programs run through a shim.
   BODY: Section 3.9.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.3.3
ORDER: 84   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: The mashup: how the display composes text, pixels and sprites
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (device interface, multiprocessor behaviour)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author asked on 2026-11-10 "what would a mashup of gpu, vram, c64 and blit look
   like" (errata 105). Five shapes were drawn, all proposed (appendix H, 3.21): (S1) layers
   composed at scanout, with no command queue; (S2) the same plus a small blitter and a glyph
   atlas; (S3) a device whose main interface is a ring of commands, with a peek and poke window;
   (S4) virtio-gpu 2D with the conveniences in a library; (S5) a small core with the C64's
   chips, as a variant of any of them. VRAM is memory the display device owns, behind a window
   on the bus; [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.3.3.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: It decides whether the display needs hardware beyond the standard virtio-gpu,
   how many drivers the kernel carries, and how much of the author's "as simple and pleasurable
   as the c64 was" is delivered by the machine and how much by a library.
   FURTHER: The questions to answer in order to choose. Who composes the layers (the hardware,
   the device's engine, or software); where glyphs live; whether sprites exist; whether there is
   a blitter, as registers or as commands; whether commands or pokes are the main interface;
   which virtio shape; VRAM or system memory; what pixel format (J.3.6).
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) S1, layered scanout. A text layer, a pixel layer and a sprite layer in memory, composed by
      the display hardware as it reads them out. For: keeps T, C, G and S; the C64's model with
      a modern screen; per-layer flip and a cursor come free. Against: no blitter, no scaling or
      rotation; needs a device type of its own; scanout traffic is the pixel layer's 498 MB/s at
      1920 by 1080 (4 bytes, 60 Hz), plus the cells.
   B) S2, S1 plus a blitter and an atlas. For: adds B; scrolling and window moves cost the
      processor no pixels; the blitter is about 1,500 lookup tables (docs/pdp64-fpga-plan.md).
      Against: a bus-master port on video memory and its arbitration with scanout; its own
      device type.
   C) S3, a command-queue device with a peek and poke window. For: the natural virtio shape
      (queues of 2.7); fence and atomic commit are the form of a commit; U in a fixed form.
      Against: more gates and a memory manager; two ways to reach one cell must be reconciled; T
      only through the window.
   D) S4, virtio-gpu 2D with a library. For: it is the standard (errata 79, 95); no gates.
      Against: no sprites, no blits, no layers; C only to 32 bits; the processor draws every
      pixel; the host reads the resource when it likes.
   E) S5, a small core with the C64's chips as the terminal. For: the author's phrase at its
      most literal; the host sends messages only. Against: a second program to write, load and
      secure; see J.3.11.
   F) A combination, for example S4 now and S2 or S3 as a second device later. For: nothing is
      wasted while the author decides. Against: two display models to keep consistent.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): S4 is what the standard already is. Of the hardware
   shapes S2 matches his words most closely, and S1 is S2 without the blitter, which can be
   added later because it only reads and writes video memory; S3 is nearer if he wants commands
   rather than pokes as the main interface.
   PRECEDENTS: The C64's text, bitmap and sprite compositing at scanout (unverified: general
   knowledge); the Amiga's blitter (unverified); the Blit, which did every pixel in software on
   a 68000 (verified: the Blit papers as recorded in docs/kernel-pain-points.md section 14I); a
   GPU's fixed-function scanout planes (unverified); virtio-gpu 2D as the first iteration
   (verified: errata 79).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The Blit, the C64, "gpu, vram, c64 and blit"; "graphics programming
   should be as simple and pleasurable as the c64 was".
   BODY: Section 3.9.1.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.3.5
ORDER: 85   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: Sprites and the hardware cursor
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (device interface)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A sprite is a small picture the display hardware overlays at a position the program
   gives, without touching the screen memory beneath. The C64 had eight. The Blit drew its
   cursor in software, in exclusive-OR, at 60 Hz. The author's description of 2026-11-10 does
   not mention sprites; the earlier terminal chat put them on a separate plane. virtio-gpu has a
   cursor in its protocol (the cursor queue, UPDATE_CURSOR, MOVE_CURSOR), and no sprites.
   WHY IT MATTERS: A moving cursor or game object either costs a redraw of the picture beneath
   it, which tears, or costs gates and a limit on the number.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) None: the program draws the cursor in software. For: no hardware. Against: save, draw and
      restore, about 20 lines a program; may tear.
   B) The virtio-gpu cursor queue only. For: standard; nothing to build. Against: one cursor; no
      sprites; the host decides the timing.
   C) A hardware cursor, two registers. For: cheap; never tears. Against: a native device.
   D) A sprite layer of several entries. For: the C64's model; games and moving objects without
      redraw. Against: gates in the scanout path; a limit on the number.
   E) Sprites as library objects over the cursor queue or a layer. For: a program sees sprites.
      Against: not hardware; the speed is the library's.
   PRECEDENTS: The C64's eight sprites (unverified: general knowledge); the Blit's software
   cursor (verified: kernel-pain-points.md 14I); the virtio-gpu cursor queue (verified:
   virtio_gpu.h).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The C64 ("the c64 did games, so it had sound and color and sprites as
   well"); the Blit.
   BODY: Section 3.9.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.3.8
ORDER: 86   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: Anti-tearing conveniences
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (tear-free flip: maybe a barrier or a register)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A tear is a seam in the picture when a program changes it while the scanout reads it.
   The author asked for "nice architectural sugar to avoid 'tearing' and all those other issues
   graphics programmers had to use double buffering to fix" (errata 105) and asked whether
   tearing is a concern with HDMI at all. It is not a property of HDMI: it happens in the
   display controller. A program that writes straight into the framebuffer with no operating
   system can still tear. Variable refresh (the monitor waits for the frame) reduces the need to
   flip at the blank but does not remove it. [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.3.8.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Every game and every compositor depends on how a flip is timed.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A scanout base register that latches at the vertical blank, with an interrupt or a fence.
      For: no tearing, no raster polling. Against: two pictures of memory; a rule for two writes
      in a frame.
   B) Per-layer latching. For: a cursor moves every frame while text flips only when it changes.
      Against: one register set per layer; needs layers (J.3.3).
   C) Atomic commit of several registers at the blank (Linux's "atomic modesetting"). For: a
      change of mode, picture and cursor cannot be seen half-done. Against: shadow copies cost
      flip-flops.
   D) A raster-position register and a raster interrupt. For: the C64's mid-frame tricks.
      Against: ties programs to the display's timing.
   E) Versioned buffers: the device keeps what is being read and the program writes a fresh
      version that replaces it atomically. For: nearest to "nothing to do". Against: memory and
      copying; page-granular copy-on-write needs the machinery of chapter 6.
   F) Damage rectangles and a fence only, as virtio-gpu has. For: standard; nothing to build.
      Against: no timing at the blank; the host decides.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option F for the standard device, and Options A and 3
   together if a native scanout engine is ever built.
   PRECEDENTS: The C64's raster register (unverified); the VGA retrace (unverified); Linux
   page-flip and atomic ioctls with DRM_MODE_PAGE_FLIP_ASYNC (verified: drm_mode.h); "vblank
   evasion" in a Linux graphics driver (verified: kernel-pain-points.md 0.6 #13); HDMI VRR and
   adaptive sync (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "with modern hdmi is tearing even a concern?"; the C64's raster
   interrupt; "Flip on vsync unconditionally with an overrun interrupt instead of a barrier"
   (c64stuff.txt line 5647).
   BODY: Section 3.15.1.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.3.9
ORDER: 87   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: The graphics boilerplate sugar
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (sugar may be new instructions)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author's goal: "basically, anything that made graphics programming annoying or
   cumbersome or boiler platey, we want to sugar away to almost nothing" and "graphics
   programming should be as simple and pleasurable as the c64 was" (errata 105). They are goals,
   not designs. The candidates are in the rows of the table of 3.14: a device that powers up in
   a sane default mode, in the monitor's preferred mode, which a register reports; a fixed
   canonical pixel format; stride equal to width; a framebuffer window always mapped; one colour
   space with the transfer curve applied by the device; [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.3.9.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Each one costs gates or flexibility, and the standard virtio-gpu device keeps
   its own protocol (five commands to put a picture up).
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Nothing in the architecture; a library over virtio-gpu sugars it. For: no hardware.
      Against: every system writes its own.
   B) A default mode and a mode-report register, the device reads the monitor itself. For:
      nothing to set. Against: a small I2C engine and a policy.
   C) A canonical pixel format with conversion at scanout. For: no format to get wrong. Against:
      a conversion stage; wasted memory for fewer bits.
   D) Stride equal to width and the framebuffer window always mapped. For: no mmap, no bank
      register. Against: a fixed place in the address space; a rule for two framebuffers.
   E) One defined colour space (linear or sRGB). For: no gamma code. Against: the choice is
      open.
   F) Physical units for cells and fonts. For: HiDPI without a scale factor in every program.
      Against: the TrueType engine must know the screen.
   PRECEDENTS: VESA mode lists; Linux DRM/KMS; Vulkan (unverified for the line counts, which are
   illustrative); virtio-gpu's GET_DISPLAY_INFO (verified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The Abrash books ("800 page michael abrash books"); the C64.
   BODY: Section 3.14.2.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.3.10
ORDER: 88   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: A write-combining memory type for the framebuffer
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (a memory type for the framebuffer: page attribute)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The framebuffer is mapped uncached (3.11.1). An uncached single write is slow, and a
   long run is no slower per byte. A write-combining memory type (writes are gathered and sent
   as one burst) would make processor drawing faster. The four memory types of 2.3.3 do not name
   it.
   WHY IT MATTERS: It decides how fast pixel-by-pixel drawing from the processor is, and whether
   a fifth memory type is added.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Uncached only. For: simple; no stale data. Against: slow single writes.
   B) Add a write-combining type to the page-table entry. For: fast runs. Against: a fifth type;
      ordering rules for a FENCE.
   C) Let the display's buffer be device memory with its own merge rules. For: the device
      controls it. Against: depends on the device.
   D) Draw into cached memory and copy by the device. For: fast drawing. Against: the cache must
      be cleaned, which chapter 2 removes.
   PRECEDENTS: Write-combining types on x86 and ARM (unverified: general knowledge).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: None.
   BODY: Section 3.11.3.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.3.11
ORDER: 89   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: The C128 core and the c64 mode
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (multiprocessor behaviour)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The c64 mode is decided (3.16.1, 3.16.2); what follows are its details. The author,
   2026-11-10: "we will have a c64 mode, where a c128 is built onto the die with sid etc, so a
   bit of code loaded into its address space by pcie dma will let it act as a terminal the way
   the blit and vt55 did"; "the mode is just enabling/disabling the c128 as a pcie device so it
   takes over the graphics and audio output" (errata 105). The C128 is an optional PCI Express
   device, not part of the standard (errata 77). Open: which core (the C128 had an 8502, a Z80,
   the VIC-IIe, the 8563 VDC and a SID, with a memory-management chip); [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.3.11.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: It decides whether the die holds a second machine, and whether the terminal
   is a program the host can replace, as in the Blit.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A hard core on the die, a real 6502-family core with the chips. For: fastest and smallest
      per core. Against: permanent area; fixes the idea in silicon before the questions are
      answered.
   B) A soft core in the FPGA phase only. For: no silicon cost; the MiSTer framework does it.
      Against: gone from a later chip unless made hard.
   C) Software emulation of the C64 on a pdp64 core. For: no hardware; easy to change. Against:
      uses a core; loses the C64's exact timing.
   D) None: the terminal is a virtio device served by a host process. For: the standard route of
      errata 95. Against: departs from the decision that the C128 is built onto the die
      (3.16.1); the terminal is no longer a computer of its own, which the author values in the
      Blit.
   E) A core of the pdp64's own instruction set with the C64's registers. For: one instruction
      set. Against: no C64 software runs.
   PRECEDENTS: The Blit loaded its terminal program from the host (verified:
   kernel-pain-points.md 14I; the protocol is unverified); the C128 held several computers in
   one box and MiSTer runs C64 and C128 cores in programmable logic (unverified: general
   knowledge); the earlier device chat's "8502 compatibility island"
   (docs/c64-devices-from-chat.md section 4, nothing decided).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The Blit and the VT-series terminals; "a bit of code loaded ... by pcie
   dma"; the C128.
   BODY: Section 3.16.1.

ENTRY J.3.12
ORDER: 90   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: The compositor path
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (multiprocessor behaviour)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author prefers path 3 below, the core drawing into a buffer that the host's
   compositor treats as a surface (3.16.3); the other paths show what it was preferred over, and
   the open part is the details of path 3. A compositor is the program or hardware that combines
   the pictures of several windows or sources into the one picture that is scanned out. The
   author wrote of the c128 that it "takes over the graphics and audio output... or sends them
   to a compositor. not sure what that would look like. is there virtio-compositor?", and later
   "c128 drawing into a buffer makes most sense" (errata 105, 106: [...]  (Full text: Appendix
   J, J.3.12.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: It decides whether the core is a screen of its own or a window of the host's.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Exclusive takeover. The core owns scanout 0 and the audio path. For: an enable bit and a
      mute. Against: the host shows nothing meanwhile.
   B) One more virtio-gpu scanout and virtio-snd stream. For: nothing new; the shape of errata
      95. Against: the core must speak the protocols.
   C) A surface for the host's compositor (the author's preference). The core renders into a
      resource or blob. For: a window with the host's window management. Against: the compositor
      must know the surface is the core's; a copy or a shared blob.
   D) Layers at scanout. For: no copy; per-layer latch. Against: needs the layered display of
      J.3.3.
   E) A new virtio device type for compositing. For: one model for every client. Against: a
      specification to write and have accepted, and a driver for every host; it repeats what
      virtio-gpu says.
   PRECEDENTS: QEMU's virtio-gpu-rutabaga, "gfxstream rendering and Wayland display passthrough"
   (verified: docs/system/devices/virtio/virtio-gpu.rst); crosvm shows a guest's windows as
   windows of the host's Wayland compositor (unverified: general knowledge).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "c128 drawing into a buffer makes most sense" (errata 106); rio's windows
   as buffers.
   BODY: Sections 3.16.2 and 3.16.3.

ENTRY J.3.13
ORDER: 91   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: Audio mixing
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (multiprocessor behaviour)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A mixer combines the sound of several programs into the one stream a converter takes:
   it converts each stream to a common rate and format (resampling), applies a volume, sums and
   limits, balances latency against underrun and absorbs clock drift. virtio-snd defines one
   stream at a time, as one card, and says nothing about who mixes; QEMU mixes on the host. The
   pdp64 is a machine where the mixing may have no host. The author asked "what do those mixers
   do to combine multiple programs audio?" (errata 106). For the audio of a c64-mode core
   (J.3.11) the options parallel those for pixels: exclusive takeover; [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.3.13.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: It decides who resamples and whose clock rules, and whether two programs can
   play at once on the first board.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The kernel mixes. For: one place; programs see one stream. Against: kernel code and
      floating-point or fixed-point work in the kernel.
   B) A library mixes in the program (as a sound server does). For: policy outside the kernel.
      Against: only one program may hold the device; a server is needed.
   C) The device mixes. For: no CPU time. Against: gates for rate converters; a fixed policy.
   D) A mixing process with the device's clock driving it, as PipeWire and PulseAudio do. For: a
      proven design. Against: a user-level server.
   E) One stream only, no mixing at all. For: nothing to decide. Against: no two programs at
      once.
   PRECEDENTS: PulseAudio, PipeWire, ALSA dmix, CoreAudio and WASAPI shared mode (unverified:
   general knowledge; the programs pulseaudio and libasound are present on this machine); QEMU's
   mixing engine audio/mixeng.c (verified: the QEMU sources); virtio-snd as one stream
   (verified: virtio_snd.h); the SID mixes its voices inside itself (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "what is the audio equivalent, what do those mixers do to combine
   multiple programs audio?"; the SID.
   BODY: Section 3.17.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.4.1
ORDER: 92   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: The first few pages
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (interrupt vectors, start-up state)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author's rule (errata 102) is that device windows can go anywhere in physical
   space "except the first few pages where the boot stuff is loaded". How many pages are "the
   first few", and what exactly lives in them, is not fixed. The rule is a reservation: a range
   of physical addresses that no device window may overlap, so that the boot ROM and the first
   stage can assume plain memory there. What the documents already put in that range: the vector
   table (the table through which an exception or an interrupt finds its handler: [...]  (Full
   text: Appendix J, J.4.1.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Before translation is on, the kernel needs a known place for the vector
   table, the boot-information block and the early stack. The root complex must refuse to decode
   a window below the limit, and the boot ROM's load address and the reset value of VBR are tied
   to it. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Two pages (8 KB). Page 0 is the vector table; page 1 holds block 0 at 0x1000 with the
      boot-information block after it. For: the smallest reservation that holds what is already
      named; a window can start at 0x2000; the decoder compares against one constant. Against:
      block 0, a block of a few hundred bytes and a stack can overflow 8 KB when the device
      table grows; moving the limit later breaks every board that placed a window at 0x2000.
   B) 64 KB (sixteen pages), matching the rule for r14. Everything below the top of the first 64
      KB is boot territory. For: reuses a number the reset state already defines, so the
      reservation, the initial stack and the load area agree; room for the vectors, block 0, the
      boot-information block, a first stack and the second-stage loader; costs nothing at 40
      bits or more. Against: wasted if a board's RAM does not start at 0.
   C) 1 MiB (256 pages), the low megabyte of the PC. For: room for a second-stage loader, the
      first segment of a kernel image, a hand-off area for a frame buffer and the pages where
      other cores start. Against: wastes the low megabyte of a small FPGA board with tens of
      megabytes; invites sloppy use; invents a number.
   D) Nothing fixed by the architecture. The platform states the limit in the boot-information
      block or the root complex; the architecture says only that page 0 is never a device
      window. For: every board picks; one page is enough for the vector table at VBR 0. Against:
      a loader and a kernel can assume nothing about low memory and must parse the block first,
      though r1 itself points into the reserved area; the documents lose a number to quote.
   E) Move the boot area to the top of physical memory, or of the implemented space. For:
      address 0 becomes an unmapped page, so a null pointer used as a physical address is
      caught; the reservation moves with the width, so no device meets low RAM. Against: VBR
      zero at reset must change; every sentence of the reset state (r14, the load address of
      block 0) is rewritten; the top depends on the width, which is itself open.
   F) Reserve by type, not by count. The root complex refuses windows only over ranges that the
      memory map in the boot-information block marks as RAM or reserved; there is no "first few
      pages" rule. For: "a window anywhere" becomes literally true. Against: whoever enumerates
      the bus needs the map before placing any window, and the same code that reads the block
      makes the first decisions; more code than a constant.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B, 64 KB, because the reset state already defines
   the first 64 KB through r14, so no new number is invented; the vector page and block 0 fit
   with room; and the cost is nothing at 40 bits. It would change if a board's RAM does not
   start at 0, or if the boot-information block grows beyond 8 KB (then Option C or 4). To
   decide it, the author's size for the boot-information block and for the second-stage loader
   would help, and whether the vector table stays at 0 after boot (VBR can be moved).
   PRECEDENTS: The PDP-11 kept its trap vectors in the first octal 0 to 0377 bytes of memory,
   with the boot ROMs elsewhere (unverified: general knowledge). The RISC-V virt board of QEMU
   has a debug region at 0x0, the boot ROM at 0x1000 and devices from 0x2000000, and 0x1000 is
   the default reset vector (verified: QEMU hw/riscv/virt.c and target/riscv/cpu_bits.h). The PC
   reserves the first megabyte of real mode for vectors, BIOS data and the boot sector loaded at
   0x7C00 (unverified: general knowledge). The VAX console passes a boot block and a restart
   parameter block that lives in low memory (unverified: general knowledge). The PDP-11 I/O page
   sat at the very top of its address space (verified in the project's chapter 2 commentary of
   the V7 drivers), and x86 resets at 0xFFFFFFF0, at the top of 4 GB (unverified: general
   knowledge). The PC BIOS E820 map and the UEFI memory map type each range (unverified: [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The PDP-11 boot ("load 512 byte block 0 then jmp 0"; "not precisely true,
   but directionally true"); "follow the majority as far as boot block offset"; the VAX's I/O
   space (memory low, I/O above; "it should be noted in the book how the vax does this io space
   idea"); UEFI ("is it worth it at all?", since decided against); the GPT as the disk label.
   BODY: Sections 2.3.5, 4.4.1 and 6.12.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.4.2
ORDER: 93   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: How the first stage reads blocks
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (start-up state, device interface)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The first stage is the program in block 0, one 512-byte block, and its job is to read
   the next stage from the disk. The ROM has done the same job once, with a virtio-blk request:
   a virtqueue (the shared ring through which a driver hands requests to a virtio device), three
   descriptors (a request header, a 512-byte buffer and a one-byte status), a write to the
   doorbell register, and a poll until the device returns the chain. The PDP-11's first stage
   carried its own disk driver; the PC's called the BIOS. Which of those the pdp64's first stage
   does is not decided. [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.4.2.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The size of the first stage, what the ROM must leave behind for it, and
   whether the ROM offers anything after boot all follow from the answer. The ROM and the first
   stage cannot be written until it is settled.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A small polled virtio-blk reader inside the first stage. The PDP-11 way. For: no
      dependence on the ROM; the ROM keeps no service after it has run. Against: the reader, the
      list of what to read and a stack-and-queue set-up may not fit in 446 bytes; it duplicates
      the ROM's reader.
   B) The ROM leaves a read routine in memory and tells the first stage where it is. For: the
      first stage is a few instructions. Against: it is a firmware call in miniature; the
      routine's address and calling convention become part of the architecture and the ROM's
      code can then never change.
   C) The ROM loads more than block 0, for example the whole 8 KB boot area. For: room for a
      reader and for more than a list. Against: the ROM must learn a size and a place, so it is
      no longer "read block 0 and jump"; it conflicts with a block 0 that is the protective MBR,
      since the rest of the 8 KB belongs to the GPT.
   D) The ROM reads the second stage itself, from a list in block 0 that the ROM parses. For:
      there is no first-stage reader at all. Against: the ROM is larger and the list format is
      frozen in ROM; block 0 becomes data and not a program.
   E) A simple programmed-I/O read register on the boot device or the controller, beside virtio.
      For: a few instructions, no queue. Against: a second device interface that every board
      must implement.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A if a measured reader fits, because it needs
   nothing from the ROM; Option C if it does not. A measurement of a polled virtio-blk read in
   assembler for the pdp64 would decide it.
   PRECEDENTS: The V7 primary bootstrap for the RM02/3 writes the disk's registers itself
   (verified: sys/mdec/hpuboot.s). OpenBSD's PC first stage calls INT 0x13 (verified:
   biosboot.S). OpenBSD's installboot hard-codes the block numbers of the second stage into the
   first stage (verified: installboot(8) for the sparc, and biosboot(8) for the PC). The
   4.3BSD-Tahoe VAX raboot is documented as working with the 750's boot ROM and reading 7.5 KB
   (verified for the header; how the ROM helps it is unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The PDP-11 boot ("load 512 byte block 0 then jmp 0"); "follow the
   majority as far as boot block offset"; the GPT wrapper of errata 50.
   BODY: Section 4.5.2.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.4.3
ORDER: 94   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: How the ROM finds and maps the boot device
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (device interface)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: Section 2.4 says that the processor assigns the windows of the bus's devices. The ROM
   is the first software to run, and to read block 0 it must reach the boot device's registers
   and queues, which lie in a window that nobody has assigned yet. It is not stated whether the
   ROM assigns every window or only the boot device's, where it places them, and whether it
   leaves them for the kernel to keep or to change. The author's rule (errata 102) keeps windows
   out of the first few pages (J.4.1).
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel's own enumeration starts from the state the ROM leaves. The root
   complex's reset state, the ROM's size and the boot-information block's description of the
   root complex all depend on it.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The ROM enumerates the whole bus and assigns every window, as PC firmware does. For: the
      kernel finds a mapped machine. Against: a long program in the ROM, the largest of the
      options.
   B) The ROM assigns only the boot device's window, at a fixed address, and the kernel
      enumerates afresh. For: a few lines; the ROM needs one function. Against: the kernel must
      keep that window or move it, and the second stage must be told which.
   C) The boot device's window is decoded from reset at an address fixed by the board, so that
      the ROM assigns nothing. For: no ROM code for it. Against: it makes the boot device a
      special case beside the rule that the processor assigns windows; the address is per board.
   D) A table in the ROM lists where the boot devices are, per board. For: no scan. Against: a
      port to a new board is a new ROM; the table duplicates what the bus reports.
   E) The ROM maps, reads, then unmaps everything it assigned, and the second stage and the
      kernel begin clean. For: one rule for every later stage. Against: the second stage must
      map the device again to read the next block, so the code is written twice.
   PRECEDENTS: PC firmware assigns the base address registers of the devices before boot
   (unverified: general knowledge). QEMU's RISC-V virt board places its devices at fixed
   addresses, and 0x1000 is its reset vector (verified: hw/riscv/virt.c). The PDP-11's I/O page
   was fixed by the hardware at the top of the address space (verified in chapter 2's commentary
   of the V7 drivers).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: PCI Express enumeration (2.4); the PDP-11 and VAX I/O space.
   BODY: Section 4.4.1.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.4.4
ORDER: 95   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: The boot-information block: fields, who fills them, layout
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (start-up state, multiprocessor behaviour)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The kernel must be told about the machine it is on: memory ranges, the number of
   cores, the address of the root complex, the boot device, the console, a command line, and,
   when the loader has set one up, the screen (decided: 4.6.3). That the kernel is told through
   a boot-information block, and that the bus lists the devices (so the block need not), is
   decided (4.6.2, errata 109 and 49). It has been assumed that the ROM fills what it knows
   (memory, cores, the device) and the second stage adds the command line and the screen, and
   that the address of the block is passed in r1; none of that is decided. [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.4.4.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The ROM, the second stage and the kernel are written against it; the
   generated headers and the ABI appendix cannot carry it until it is fixed.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A fixed-layout record, with a version and a checksum, generated from a grammar. The ROM
      fills the fixed part; the second stage adds the rest. For: simple to read in a few
      instructions; the header cannot disagree with the loader. Against: a new field means a new
      version.
   B) A list of tagged records (tag, length, value). For: extends without breaking old readers;
      a loader adds its own. Against: a parser in the kernel, though a short one.
   C) A flattened device tree built by the ROM or the second stage. For: OpenBSD's arm64 and
      riscv64 drivers attach from a device tree, so reusing them would save kernel code.
      Against: a parser and a second description of the devices beside bus enumeration, which
      already lists them.
   D) A record plus a device tree for the devices that enumeration cannot find. For: both
      worlds. Against: two things to build and keep consistent.
   E) Registers only, as on the PDP-11 and VAX. For: no structure. Against: a kernel for any
      pdp64 needs the memory ranges, which a few registers cannot hold.
   F) A memory map alone; everything else by enumeration. For: the smallest. Against: the
      console, the core count and the command line have no place.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A, with a version field: the bus describes its
   own devices, so the block only has to carry what enumeration cannot (memory, cores, root
   complex, boot device, console, command line, screen). It changes if the OpenBSD device-tree
   drivers are to be reused, which would favour Option D.
   PRECEDENTS: The VAX restart parameter block and the Alpha hardware restart parameter block
   are fixed-layout records of the machine (verified: 4.3BSD vax/rpb.h, OpenBSD
   alpha/include/rpb.h). UEFI passes a memory map and configuration tables for ACPI or a device
   tree, and OpenBSD's loaders look for both (verified: efiboot). OpenBSD's arm64 and riscv64
   kernels parse a device tree (verified through efiboot's FDT_TABLE_GUID).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: UEFI ("is it worth it at all?"), since decided against; "load 512 byte
   block 0 then jmp 0"; errata 109: the hand-off "kept simple enough that a UEFI shim could be
   written".
   BODY: Section 4.6.2.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.4.6
ORDER: 96   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: Starting the other cores
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (start register or an instruction to start cores)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: At reset only core 0 runs, and the controller chip provides the way the others are
   started (4.7.1; the author, R256: "we'll need a way for the controller chip to 'boot' the
   other chips"). The open part is the mechanism. The kernel must start the others, each with
   its own stack and its own identity. The mechanism is not decided: [...]  (Full text: Appendix
   J, J.4.6.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel's secondary-start code, the controller's register block and the
   boot-information block's core count are written against it.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A register block in the controller: write an entry address and an argument to the core's
      start register, and the controller releases the core into kernel mode at priority 7. A
      core-reset register restarts a core that sticks. For: no trampoline and no firmware call;
      one write. Against: a register block with a start and a reset register for every core.
   B) The x86 way: INIT and START-UP signals through the interrupt controller. For: a known
      protocol. Against: the signal carries only an address below a limit; the x86 needs a
      trampoline because the woken core starts in a mode ladder, which the pdp64 does not have.
   C) A firmware call (PSCI on arm64, the SBI hart start on RISC-V). For: a standard form on two
      architectures. Against: it needs resident firmware after boot, which the project does not
      otherwise have.
   D) A spin table: every core runs from reset and waits on a word in memory until core 0 writes
      an address there. For: no register block. Against: the cores run before the kernel has set
      up memory, and they must be told where the table is.
   E) All cores start at the same address and choose a role from their number. For: no start
      protocol. Against: the first stage and the ROM's hand-off must handle every core at once.
   F) An inter-processor interrupt from core 0 carrying the entry address. For: uses the
      interrupt controller already needed. Against: the interrupt controller must be programmed
      on the started core first, a circle.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A: it removes the trampoline and the firmware
   call and recovers a stuck core by one write.
   PRECEDENTS: Linux counted: smpboot.c 1,528 lines and arch/x86/realmode 1,258 for x86; PSCI
   124 and the spin table 127 on arm64; the SBI start 108 and the spin wait 58 on RISC-V
   (verified, re-counted in Linux 7be4bc52). The VAX console processor started the other
   processors (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The PDP-11, which had one processor; "we'll need a way for the controller
   chip to 'boot' the other chips; do a thorough review of how all the main smp systems do it,
   which is best/easiest" (R256). He has not chosen among the options.
   BODY: Sections 4.2 and 4.7.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.4.8
ORDER: 97   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: Where the second stage lies, and a dedicated boot partition
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (start-up state)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The first stage loads the second stage from somewhere on the disk. Three places are
   candidates: the 8 KB boot area at the start of the BSD partition (4.5.3), a file in the root
   file system, and a small partition of its own with a partition type chosen for it. The second
   stage holds a virtio-blk driver, a GPT reader, a label reader and a file-system reader;
   OpenBSD's amd64 second stage is 189 lines beside a libsa of 6,319 lines (counted), and
   whether such a program fits the 7.5 KB left in the boot area is not measured.
   WHY IT MATTERS: It decides whether the disk layout needs another partition type, what the
   installation tool writes and where, and whether the second stage has a size limit.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The 8 KB boot area of the BSD partition. For: the BSD tradition (sector 0 the primary
      bootstrap, sectors 1 to 15 the label and a secondary bootstrap); no new partition.
      Against: a hard size limit of 7.5 KB, and the label occupies sector 1.
   B) A file in the root file system, its blocks written into the first stage by installboot.
      For: no size limit. Against: the list must be rewritten whenever the file moves
      (biosboot(8) says to re-run installboot "as its inode may change"), and a damaged root
      file system stops the boot before the second stage.
   C) A dedicated boot partition with its own type. For: a place that no file system moves; room
      for a larger loader. Against: another partition to create, to size and to keep, and a type
      to assign.
   D) The EFI system partition (FAT). For: the same disk serves a UEFI route (4.9). Against: a
      FAT reader in the second stage, and a partition that the pdp64's own boot does not need.
   E) The gap between the GPT and the first partition. For: no partition entry. Against: a tool
      that repartitions the disk may overwrite it.
   PRECEDENTS: 4.3BSD-Tahoe and 2.11BSD reserve 15 sectors after block 0 for the bootstraps
   (verified: disklabel.h). OpenBSD on the PC patches biosboot with the location of /boot
   (verified: biosboot(8)). OpenBSD defines both the OpenBSD-type and the EFI-system partition
   GUIDs (verified: disklabel.h). Other systems keep a "BIOS boot partition" for a loader
   (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The GPT wrapper (errata 50); "follow the majority as far as boot block
   offset" (errata 51); the 2026-11-10 choice of block 0 with a UEFI route kept possible (errata
   109).
   BODY: Sections 4.5.2 and 4.5.4.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.4.9
ORDER: 98   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: The state core 0 starts in
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (initial processor state at reset)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The ROM starts core 0 at block 0 (4.4.1, decided). What core 0 holds when it starts is
   not decided: its mode and priority, whether translation is on, VBR, the user-memory guard,
   which registers carry the boot device and the boot-information block (4.1 says the pdp64
   passes r0 and r1, after the PDP-11 form), and the stack.
   WHY IT MATTERS: The first stage is written against it; it must not guess.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Kernel mode, priority 7, translation off, VBR zero, guard clear; r0 the device, r1 the
      boot-information block, r14 the top of the first 64 KB, the rest zero. For: the first
      stage has a stack and every fact before it runs an instruction; the PDP-11 handed on
      registers the same way. Against: the top of 64 KB is a number to fix with J.4.1.
   B) As option A but the stack is left to the first stage. For: no stack number. Against: 446
      bytes spend instructions on it.
   C) Only the boot-information block's address in one register; everything else a documented
      reset state of the core. For: fewest registers. Against: the device number moves into the
      block.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A.
   PRECEDENTS: 2.11BSD passes r0, r1, r3 and a check word in r2 (verified: pdpstand/srt0.s,
   boot.c); the VAX boot passes r1 to r5 (verified: raboot.c).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "load 512 byte block 0 then jmp 0".
   BODY: Section 4.4.1.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.5.6
ORDER: 99   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 5 Drivers and devices
   TITLE: Which virtio devices are built in silicon, and syntactic sugar for devices
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (syntactic sugar for devices: maybe new instructions)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: On 2026-11-09 the author wrote that "we will support all the virtio devices in silicon
   (the ones it makes sense to support, that is)", and asked what "syntactic sugar" (notation
   that makes a thing easier to write, not new opcodes) could make devices easier to use "like
   the 12 register + dma thing" (R359). Both are decided in principle (statement 5.2.1). Two
   details are not. The standard devices are fixed (block, network, graphics, audio: errata 79,
   101); virtio has others (console, entropy, balloon, SCSI, file system, socket, input) and it
   is not settled which count as "it makes sense". [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.5.6.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The list decides what the hardware contains and what OpenBSD drivers are
   used. The sugar decides how short a driver is, and whether the instruction set or only the
   assembler and the compiler change.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Only the four standard devices. Block, network, graphics and audio; everything else is an
      ordinary PCI Express device with its own driver. For: the least silicon. Against: the
      console and the entropy source of J.5.1 would have to be in this list or outside virtio.
   B) The four plus the console and the entropy source. For: the machine can boot and run with
      virtio alone; both have OpenBSD drivers (viocon, viornd). Against: the console's form is
      itself open (J.5.1).
   C) Every device that QEMU and OpenBSD both have. That adds the balloon (viomb) and SCSI
      (vioscsi). For: free drivers. Against: silicon for devices that few uses need.
   D) Sugar: assembler macros. The post, fence and notify sequence of 5.4.3 as one macro, and
      the reap loop as another. For: no change to the instruction set, no change to the
      compiler. Against: only assembly programmers benefit.
   E) Sugar: a C header of inline functions. post and reap of chapter 2 as inline functions over
      the intrinsic of J.5.5. For: C drivers become short; no hardware. Against: depends on
      J.5.5.
   F) Sugar in the device: helper registers. The bus chapter allows "helper registers" for
      convenience (2.3.4); for example a doorbell that also performs the fence. For: the
      driver's sequence shortens. Against: not in the standard, so a driver that uses it works
      on the pdp64 only.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B for the list, and options D and 5 together for
   the sugar.
   PRECEDENTS: The RK11's register set (a command register and a go bit) was the "12 register +
   dma" style the author refers to (verified: 5.1). virtio drivers share virtio_enqueue and
   virtio_alloc_vq helpers in OpenBSD (verified: virtio.c).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "like the 12 register + dma thing" (R359).
   BODY: Section 5.2.1.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.6.1
ORDER: 100   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: Discovering the implemented widths
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (feature or width register: system registers)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: An implementation chooses its physical address width (from 40 to 64 bits) and its
   number of page-table levels (3, 4 or 5, giving virtual addresses of 39, 48 or 57 bits). The
   author's requirement is that this be "discoverable easily at runtime", so that one kernel
   binary adapts to the machine without a build option. How software learns the two numbers is
   not specified: no register or field reports them. System registers 0 to 12 are defined and 13
   upward are reserved. A feature register is a read-only register whose fields describe the
   hardware; a probe is a write of a value followed by a read of what stuck.
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel must choose the walk depth and the width of every page number it
   writes. User code that tags pointers with spare high bits needs to know which bits are free:
   the top bit of the virtual address, which decides the canonical test, is bit 38, 47 or 56
   according to the levels (6.9.1). [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A read-only feature register read by MFSR (number 13), with fields for the physical bits,
      the maximum levels and the set of levels supported. For: one instruction in one place, no
      side effect and no mode change, room for later feature bits, and a read is always safe.
      Against: one register number and a few gates; privileged only, so user code that needs the
      canonical bit must ask the kernel (or see option E); the layout is fixed forever once code
      depends on it.
   B) Probe by write and read back (the register accepts any write and holds only a legal
      value). The kernel writes the level count it wants into a field of MMUCTL and reads back;
      an unsupported value leaves the old one. The physical width is found by writing all ones
      to a field that holds a page number and reading what stuck. For: no new register, no new
      instruction. Against: a write with a side effect (it changes the translation mode, needs
      ISYNC, can crash if translation is on); the probe code is longer than one MFSR; the width
      probe needs a writable field of the right width, which may not exist; a probe is a place
      for error.
   C) Both: a read-only register for what exists, and a writable field to choose. The register
      reports the maximum and the set supported, as a bit mask with one bit per level count; a
      field in MMUCTL selects the number in use, legal only if its bit is set. For: one read to
      learn, one write to choose; the kernel may run three levels on a five-level machine for
      compatibility or to save walk cost; a bad write is detectable. Against: a register and a
      field, the most state of any option; whether a machine must support all lower level counts
      becomes a rule (three levels on every machine is what the author's "System v1" form
      implies, errata 96, so the mask would say 3 always, 4 and 5 optional).
   D) The boot ROM states the widths in the boot-information block and the processor reports
      nothing. For: the block already lists the memory ranges and the root-complex address, so
      the early kernel reads it with the rest; the processor is unchanged. Against: the facts do
      not come from the hardware itself, and a stale or wrong block gives wrong widths that
      cannot be detected; useless to a second-stage loader run by hand or a bare-metal program
      that has no block.
   E) An unprivileged read-only register or instruction (a counterpart of x86's CPUID, readable
      in user mode). For: a JIT compiler or a pointer-tagging runtime learns the canonical bit
      without a system call; the information is not secret. Against: a new user-visible
      instruction, or an exception to the rule that system registers are privileged (6.2.5); a
      virtualiser or sandbox cannot hide it; it lets a program fingerprint the machine.
   F) A profile number instead of fields. One read-only identification register says "profile 1
      is 3 levels and 40 bits, profile 2 is 4 levels and 48 bits", and the manual defines each
      profile. For: a few well-tested combinations in place of the 3 by 25 that would have to be
      verified. Against: every new width forces a new profile; the freedom "40 to 64 anywhere"
      (errata 102) is lost; the kernel needs a table.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A first: a read-only MMUCFG at system register 13
   with the physical bits, a mask of supported level counts and the virtual bits. It needs no
   probe, so a bad value cannot arise, and the number 13 is already reserved. The writable
   selector of option C is added only if the author wants a kernel to run fewer levels than the
   maximum. Option B would be the choice if the author wants no new register, and option E as a
   user-readable copy if user code needs the canonical bit.
   PRECEDENTS: x86 reports physical bits in bits 7:0 and virtual bits in bits 15:8 of CPUID leaf
   0x80000008, and a 57-bit virtual address by the LA57 bit of leaf 7 (verified: QEMU
   target/i386/cpu.c); CPUID is readable in user mode (unverified). AArch64 reports the physical
   range in the PARange field of ID_AA64MMFR0, bits 3:0 (verified: QEMU
   target/arm/cpu-features.h). LoongArch reports PALEN and VALEN in fields of CPUCFG1 (verified:
   QEMU target/loongarch/cpu.h). RISC-V's satp register ignores a write of an unsupported mode
   (verified: QEMU target/riscv/tcg/csr.c), and QEMU keeps a bit mask of supported modes and
   checks the write against it (verified: target/riscv/cpu.c); x86 turns on five-level paging by
   setting CR4.LA57 only where CPUID reports it (the CPUID bit verified, the control bit
   unverified). Linux on RISC-V and ARM learns the memory mode from the device tree the loader
   passes (unverified); [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "as long as this info is discoverable easily at runtime" (chosen); "good
   thing we already had dmw in the design!" (chosen); of LoongArch, "looking at loongarch64,
   they are pragmatic; what can we learn from them and adopt" and "what other parts of the
   design are effected if we have 5 level tlb like loongso[n]" (both considered); "what is the
   largest physical ram supported right now by any cpu? how many table levels?" (considered).
   BODY: Sections 2.3.5 and 6.6.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.6.2
ORDER: 101   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: The page-table entry for wide physical addresses
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (page-table entry format: virtual memory)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The page-table entry is 64 bits, with the physical page number in bits 12 to 39 (28
   bits, a 40-bit physical address) and bits 10, 11 and 40 to 63 reserved and zero. The author
   has decided that the physical address may be anywhere from 40 to 64 bits (errata 102) and
   that the old rule of using bit 39 to tell device space from memory is gone, because "if we
   are going to mmap petabytes, we need those bits". A 64-bit physical address needs a 52-bit
   page number, bits 12 to 63, and that leaves no bit above bit 11 for any flag. The question is
   where the page number sits, how wide it may be, and where spare flag bits go. [...]  (Full
   text: Appendix J, J.6.2.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Every fork, mmap and fault handler writes entries, so the position of the
   page number is shift-and-mask code on the hot path. In hardware, the width of each
   translation-buffer entry and of the physical tag of every cache grows with the page number:
   [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The page number in place, bits 12 to 63. For: no change for the 40-bit machine (bits 12 to
      39 stay), the present text is a subset, and the hardware extracts the same field; cheapest
      in hardware. Against: no spare bit remains for any later flag (a no-execute extension, a
      wider memory type, a protection key, software bits), so any later flag forces a new
      format.
   B) Cap the page number at 40 bits (a physical address of 52 bits), in place at bits 12 to 51,
      with bits 52 to 63 for flags and software. For: twelve spare bits and 4 PiB of reach,
      which already covers "petabytes"; room for protection. Against: it contradicts "up to full
      64 bit" (errata 102), which becomes "40 to 52 in page tables"; a device mapped above 2^52
      cannot go through a page table (see option E).
   C) Move the page number down to bit 10, as RISC-V does. The page number occupies bits 10 to
      61 (52 bits) and bits 62 and 63 are free. For: a 64-bit address fits and two bits remain
      at the top. Against: the physical address is (pte >> 10) << 12, one more step in the
      walker and the kernel; every existing entry format is rewritten; only two spare bits, and
      a 64-bit device address still uses all of them; no gain in reach over option B if option
      B's cap is accepted.
   D) Keep the 64-bit entry, but the page-number width equals the implemented physical width.
      Bits above the implemented width are reserved and must be zero, and the spare bits are
      defined only as "the bits above the physical width". For: a 40-bit machine keeps bits 40
      to 63 for future flags, a 64-bit machine has none; one format with varying free space; a
      reserved-bit fault is a good detector. Against: the position of a flag depends on the
      implementation, so code that uses a spare bit works on 40-bit machines and not on 64-bit
      ones; the detection works only above the implemented width.
   E) Page tables reach 52 bits (option B), and everything above is reached by the
      direct-mapping windows DMW0 and DMW1, whose physical base is a full 64-bit field. A
      direct-mapping window (appendix I, 6.9.2) maps a range of virtual addresses to a range of
      physical addresses with no table walk. For: "good thing we already had dmw in the
      design!"; one window maps a petabyte-scale device or a very large memory without any
      entry, and the entries keep their spare bits. Against: only two windows; user processes
      cannot map above 2^52, because a window is kernel-only; the window format, which the
      author has deferred (appendix I, 6.9.2), would have to be designed now, and the registers
      are at present stored without effect.
   F) A 16-byte entry (128 bits). For: no cramping; a full 64-bit address, memory type,
      protection key and 40 free bits. Against: 256 entries per 4 KB table, so 8 bits per level,
      and three levels cover only 36 bits and five levels 60; the whole 39, 48 and 57-bit design
      of errata 96 changes; twice the memory per walk step; larger translation-buffer entries.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B for page tables with option E for the rest: a
   40-bit page number (a 52-bit physical address through page tables, 4 PiB) and the windows for
   anything above. A 64-bit page number consumes the whole entry, and every later need
   (protection, memory type, a no-execute bit separate from X) would force a new format, whereas
   2^52 already covers "petabytes". If the author insists that user processes map above 2^52,
   option C (page number shifted to bit 10, two spare bits) is the alternative.
   PRECEDENTS: x86-64 addresses bits 51 to 12 in place, with the no-execute bit at 63 (the mask
   verified in QEMU target/i386/cpu.h, the rest unverified), and puts protection keys in the
   upper bits (unverified); reserved bits above the implemented physical width must be zero and
   fault (unverified). AArch64's large-physical-address extension stops at 52 bits (verified:
   QEMU target/arm/ptw.c, the table of sizes ends at 52), and output-address bits above the
   implemented range are reserved (unverified). RISC-V puts the page number at bit 10, with up
   to 44 bits at 10 to 53, flag fields at 63 to 61 and bits 60 to 54 reserved (verified: QEMU
   target/riscv/cpu_bits.h). LoongArch direct-mapping windows are named in the ISA text, and
   MIPS's KSEG0 is the older example (MIPS behaviour unverified). IBM's z/Architecture has
   region and segment tables of another shape (unverified); [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "if we are going to mmap petabytes, we need those bits" (chosen); "good
   thing we already had dmw in the design!", and his question "what are dmw registers? part of
   pcie?" (chosen); "what other parts of the design are effected if we have 5 level tlb like
   loongso[n]" (considered).
   BODY: Sections 6.7.1 and A.6.1.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.6.3
ORDER: 102   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: Interrupt delivery and vector numbers
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (vector numbers, system registers)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: How does a device's interrupt reach one of the cores, how many vectors are there, how
   are they numbered and prioritised, and how is an interrupt moved from one core to another?
   The old bus made this trivial: each slot had a number and the vector came from it. PCI
   Express devices have no slot in that sense, and the author retired slot-derived vectors
   (errata 29, 49 and 75). The PCI Express answer is MSI-X (message-signalled interrupts: the
   device performs an ordinary memory write whose address and data say which core and which
   vector, and the write itself is the interrupt, with no wire). [...]  (Full text: Appendix J,
   J.6.3.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: OpenBSD needs an interrupt-controller driver and a number of vectors. Linux's
   complaint, on x86, is that 256 vectors per core are shared by every device class; a device
   with many queues (a network card, an NVMe disk) wants one vector per queue per core. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A per-core interrupt file, messages only. Each core has a 4 KB page in the controller at
      base + 4096 * core. An MSI-X write of a vector number sets that vector's pending bit in
      that core's file; each vector has a 3-bit level and an enable bit; the core takes the
      highest pending enabled vector above the priority in the status word and puts its number
      in ECAUSE. Vector n of the file maps to a table entry in the interrupt range (so the file
      has 224 vectors, 32 to 255, or entries are remapped). For: scales to any core count; the
      same store is the inter-processor interrupt; no claim read (one memory fetch fewer than
      RISC-V's PLIC or ARM's GIC); the status-word level stays the only threshold; the pending
      file sits outside the pipeline, which suits an out-of-order core. Against: about 1,300
      bits of state per core by the estimate of the multiprocessor record (a few hundred look-up
      tables in an FPGA, or a small RAM); a wired legacy source needs a converter; on its own it
      gives no protection, because a hostile device's write can reach the wrong core (that is
      option B).
   B) Option A plus an IOMMU interrupt-remapping table. The device's write carries an index, not
      a core number; the IOMMU (the unit that translates and checks the addresses of devices)
      maps the index to a core, a vector and a level in a 64-bit entry; retargeting is one
      atomic 64-bit store; a hostile device reaches only the entries the kernel gave it. The
      kernel owns the vector numbers of each core, and devices see only indices. For: gives many
      vectors, atomic retargeting and masking during a change by construction; forged interrupts
      cannot arise; invisible to cards (errata 81); an IOMMU already exists for DMA (errata 85).
      Against: a table walk or entry cache on every interrupt write, and a structure to size
      (about 1,024 entries of 8 bytes per device range for the first iteration); the IOMMU
      becomes a dependency of interrupts, so a broken IOMMU silences them; the author must
      decide whether remapping belongs to the first iteration.
   C) A GIC-style distributor with an ITS. A central block holds all state, a distributor
      forwards to per-core interfaces and a translation service (the ITS) turns messages into
      interrupts; 16 software-generated interrupts per core serve as inter-processor interrupts.
      For: mature and well tested; it would match ARM software; full affinity control from one
      place. Against: the largest block and by far the largest driver (2,166 lines in OpenBSD
      against 375 plus 228 for RISC-V's IMSIC and APLIC); no benefit over option B in a design
      with no legacy; more state to get wrong.
   D) Wired lines to core 0 with a per-line target register. One wire per device into a small
      controller whose per-line register names a core; messages may be added later; the vector
      can be 32 plus the line number. For: the smallest (PDP-11 style: bus-request lines BR4 to
      BR7, with the device supplying its own vector); enough for a four-core machine with a
      UART, a timer and a few virtio devices. Against: virtio devices raise messages, not wires,
      so an adapter is needed anyway; every interrupt of sixteen cores goes through one front
      door; a per-queue vector cannot have its own target; shared level lines have the "nobody
      cared" interrupt storms recorded in the Linux sources.
   E) Interrupts as events in a per-core memory ring, with one doorbell per core. The controller
      appends (device, queue, level) to a ring in the core's memory and raises one wake signal;
      the handler reads the ring; the vector is the single doorbell number. For: consistent with
      the choice for virtio that progress lives in memory (5.2.4); one hardware vector per core,
      so the 224-entry problem disappears; priority is eight rings or a field; the handler reads
      memory it was going to read anyway. Against: the controller becomes a bus master that
      writes memory on every interrupt, so it needs the IOMMU treatment; the ring can overflow,
      and the overflow rule is new; hardware priority arbitration is replaced by software
      ordering of rings; no OpenBSD driver exists to copy.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B as the end state, in two steps: option A in the
   first iteration, since a four-core machine with virtio devices gains little from remapping,
   and the remapping table before sixteen cores or untrusted devices. For the numbering, the
   file's vector number is 8 bits and is delivered in ECAUSE at a fixed offset (entry 32 + n for
   n from 0 to 223, entries 224 to 255 of the file unused), or the TRAP range moves; the author
   has not yet been shown the 224-entry fact and should decide this. The fixed devices (timer
   and UART, J.5.1) enter through a small wired-to-message converter, as RISC-V's APLIC does. A
   first iteration that never exceeds a dozen vectors would favour option 6 as a stand-in; a
   wish for ARM-compatible software favours option C; a finding by the OpenBSD port that a
   per-core threshold register is needed would add an IMSIC-style threshold to option A.
   PRECEDENTS: RISC-V's AIA has an interrupt file per hart with enable, pending and threshold
   registers (the IMSIC); OpenBSD's driver is 375 lines and keeps a threshold table per priority
   level, and Linux has the same driver in files of 308, 390 and 977 lines plus a header
   (verified: line counts and sources of the local tree). Intel's VT-d interrupt remapping puts
   a table between the device's message and its destination (unverified); ARM's GICv3 ITS
   translates a device identifier and an event identifier to an interrupt through tables (file
   existence verified, 5,903 lines in Linux; behaviour unverified). The ARM GIC with GICv3
   redistributors and the ITS has OpenBSD's agintc.c of 2,166 lines (verified). The PDP-11 had
   bus-request lines and device-supplied vectors (verified in the repository's sources); [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "go with priority 7 on entry and document its throwback to pdp-11 design"
   (chosen); "what if all interrupts were suspended/defered for one instruction giving time for
   that spl #7" (considered); "iommu will allow us to map in c64/c128" (considered); "survey of
   what all major modern os and cpu do, plus vax/pdp" (considered); "virtio across the board"
   (chosen).
   BODY: Sections 2.6.1, 5.9.1, 5.9.4, 6.3.7, 7.10.4 and 7.10.5.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.6.11
ORDER: 103   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 6 Kernel, modes and memory management
   TITLE: Hardware breakpoints and watchdogs, performance counters, machine-check reporting
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (system registers for debug, counters)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: Three groups of kernel-visible facilities are not specified: debug registers (hardware
   breakpoints and watchpoints), performance counters, and what a machine check reports beyond
   the halt of 6.4.3. A watchdog (a timer that resets the machine if the kernel does not touch
   it) is the fourth. None has a system-register number: 13 and up are reserved.
   WHY IT MATTERS: A kernel debugger without hardware breakpoints cannot stop on a data access,
   and a profiler without counters cannot count cache misses. A machine check that reports
   nothing leaves a kernel nothing to log. None is needed to boot; each would have to be present
   before the system is used for serious work.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) None in System v1. For: smallest. Against: each is requested by a debugging and operations
      workflow later, and a software replacement is slow.
   B) A fixed small set (two breakpoint registers, one cycle counter, one machine-check status
      register). For: enough to start a debugger and a profiler. Against: the set is then fixed
      forever.
   C) Adopt RISC-V's sets (the debug trigger module, the hardware performance monitor counters
      and the mcause convention). For: ready-made specifications and software. Against: much
      larger than a first iteration needs.
   D) Leave them to the platform, not the architecture. For: each implementation chooses.
      Against: a kernel then has no portable way to use them.
   E) The memory-mapped debug unit of the bus (PCI Express's own capabilities). For: no new
      registers. Against: the processor's state is then not reachable in the processor's own
      terms.
   PRECEDENTS: x86 has debug registers DR0 to DR7, performance counters selected by MSRs and
   machine-check banks (unverified); AArch64 has the debug, PMU and RAS extensions (unverified);
   RISC-V has a trigger module, hpmcounter registers and a mcause (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: None recorded.
   BODY: Section A.10.1.

ENTRY J.7.6
ORDER: 104   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 7 Multiple processors
   TITLE: How block operations order their accesses
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (ordering rule of existing block instructions; a publish instruction
   is possible)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The block operations are MCPY, MMOV and MSET, which write memory, and MCMP, MSCN,
   MSCNN and MVAL, which only read it (9.11.3). Each keeps its progress in its registers
   (9.11.4) and an implementation may move more than a byte per step (9.11.5). One part of the
   ordering is decided: the accesses inside one block operation are unordered with respect to
   one another, and only the final contents of memory and the register progress are defined
   (7.5.8, errata 39 item 7; the text was never written into the instruction-set document, and
   the decision list of the ledger does not carry it). Nothing is decided about the rest: [...]
   (Full text: Appendix J, J.7.6.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The flag idiom (copy a buffer, then store a flag that another core polls) is
   the commonest use of a copy between cores, and under sequential consistency (7.5.1) it needs
   no barrier for ordinary stores. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options Options A, 2, 4 and 5 differ in what another core sees; option F is about device
     memory and combines with 1, 2 or 4:
   A) Unordered inside, ordered as a whole. Every access of the instruction is after all earlier
      accesses of the core and before all later ones, as the other cores see them; inside, any
      order, any intermediate state. A plain store of a flag after the instruction is safe. For:
      the flag idiom works with no fence; the decided rule of 7.5.8 holds. Against: the engine
      must finish before the next access issues, so a large copy cannot overlap later work; an
      interrupted copy has published part of its data.
   B) Unordered inside and with respect to later accesses; a fence after the instruction
      publishes. The engine runs in the background as a DMA device does, and FENCE is widened to
      all memory. For: the cheapest hardware and the most overlap. Against: breaks the rule of
      7.5.1 for the code after a copy and brings back the barrier a kernel writer must remember;
      FENCE changes meaning (7.5.7).
   C) Ordered as the loop of the instruction-set text, byte by byte. For: nothing new to learn;
      the model is the PDP-11 loop. Against: contradicts the decided 7.5.8; a wide engine must
      hold back stores to keep the order seen by others.
   D) Option A, and every aligned 8-byte piece is single-copy atomic. The head and the tail that
      are not aligned are byte accesses. For: a reader of copied pointers or counters never sees
      a torn word (7.5.3 and 7.5.6 already promise this for ordinary accesses); the engine moves
      aligned quads. Against: an engine that moves lines must still be able to split them; MSET
      of one byte into a word needs a byte access.
   E) The block operation is atomic against other cores. For: nothing to reason about. Against:
      an unbounded hold on memory against interrupts and the progress rule, and a latency no
      real-time claim survives (J.9.7).
   F) Device and uncached pages are accessed in element order, one access of the program's size
      per element, never combined or repeated. Ordinary pages follow option A, 2 or 4. For: a
      block write to a device register ring means what a loop would mean. Against: the behaviour
      depends on the memory type of the page, and the same instruction runs at two speeds.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option D with option F: ordered as a whole against the
   other accesses of the core, aligned quads atomic, and element order on device pages. The
   leaning keeps the flag idiom free of fences, which is what the decision for sequential
   consistency was bought for. This is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: x86 REP MOVS keeps its progress in registers and, in the fast-string forms, is
   allowed to order its stores differently from ordinary ones; Linux's memcpy uses rep movsb
   where the processor has the fast form (arch/x86/lib/memcpy_64.S, read; the rules in the Intel
   manual are unverified). ARM64 FEAT_MOPS splits a copy and a set into prologue, main and
   epilogue instructions (cpyp, setp in Linux's arch/arm64/lib/memcpy.S and memset.S, read),
   updates the registers as it goes and leaves the order and size of the accesses to the
   implementation (the architectural wording is unverified). The VAX MOVC3 and MOVC5 were
   interruptible, with the registers as state and a first-part-done bit in the status word (9.11
   and J.9.7; the NetBSD VAX copy routines use movc3, subr.S, read; the memory ordering is moot
   on a machine that was one processor in most models). The PDP-11 had no block move: [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "rep movsb r1,r2,10;" and "how about mmov mcpy mset ? like ibm system z?"
   (the author, 2026-10-20); "doesn't mcmp/mmov count?"; VAX MOVC5 as the argument against
   hidden state (9.11); errata 39 item 7.
   BODY: Sections 7.5.8, 7.5.9 and 9.11.
