ENTRY J.7.7
ORDER: 105   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 7 Multiple processors
   TITLE: Secure mode on every core, or one secure core
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (secure mode: mode bits and the instruction words of J.9.12)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author decided on 2026-11-10 (errata 132): "at least one core that can be in
   'secure' mode; if it doesn't take too many gates, let that be a mode for all the cores, but
   if it slows things down too much then return to a single secure core" (7.11.3). Two things
   are not decided: what counts as "too many gates" and "too much slowdown", and how the two are
   measured. Secure mode here is the whole of 7.11.3 to 7.11.5: [...]  (Full text: Appendix J,
   J.7.7.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: A mode of every core means every core carries the extra logic and a kernel
   may place a secret-handling process anywhere; one secure core means a smaller, simpler design
   for the rest and a scheduling rule that sends secret work to one core. On four cores, one
   secure core is a quarter of the first device. [...]
   FURTHER: The measurement plan. Gates: synthesise one core twice for the FPGA of the plan,
   with and without the mode, and compare the lookup-table counts (and block RAMs) of the logic
   the mode adds: the fixed-latency window, the locked translation buffer, the clear engine, the
   clock hold, the speculation disable and the AES and SHA datapaths. Slowdown: [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) One dedicated secure core. The design of crypto-secure-core.md: a small in-order core with
      the window, the clear engine and the bus slot, and no mode on the other cores. For: the
      other cores pay nothing; the guarantee is by construction (no speculation, no sharing);
      the design is already approved (errata 86). Against: a core that general work cannot use;
      secret work must move to it, and a process that needs more than the window does not fit.
   B) A secure mode on every core, entered by a privileged instruction or by a status-word bit.
      The mode relates to DIT (9.3.7): DIT makes ordinary instructions constant-time, and secure
      mode adds the private window, the locked buffer, a fixed clock and no speculation. For:
      any core can run a secure domain; no core is set aside. Against: every core carries the
      logic; an out-of-order core must turn speculation off and drain its predictors on entry (a
      cost in time); the critical path may lengthen.
   C) Secure mode on a subset of cores (a core class). The class register of 7.11.1 says which.
      For: a middle way: more than one secure core without the cost on all. Against: a
      scheduling rule that depends on the machine; the subset size is another number to choose.
   D) Secure mode as a core class chosen at build time. A configuration parameter of the die, a
      number of secure cores from none to all. For: one design serves every product; the first
      device can have one and a larger die more. Against: the kernel must read the class and
      plan for any count; more variants to test.
   E) A time-shared secure domain on any core, with the clear-on-switch rule only. The kernel
      clears the core's state when it enters and leaves a secure domain, and the core has no
      other mode logic. For: the least hardware. Against: a shared cache, translation buffer and
      predictors cannot all be cleared at a cost that hides the leak; it removes no channel that
      a clear does not reach.
   F) No secure mode, only DIT. For: nothing added; DIT (9.3.7) covers constant-time
      instructions. Against: leaves table lookups indexed by secrets exposed to the cache and
      the translation buffer, which the private window and locked buffer exist to close;
      contradicts the decided 7.11.3.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option D: build the mode as a class that a die parameter
   sets, measure the gates and the slowdown on the first device, and let the author's threshold
   decide between one and all. The gate figures should come before the leaning is trusted. This
   is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: Arm TrustZone gives every core a secure state (unverified; Linux's PSR_DIT_BIT in
   arch/arm64/include/uapi/asm/ptrace.h and PSTATE_DIT in sysreg.h show the
   data-independent-timing bit on Arm, read). Intel SGX and TDX are modes of every core
   (unverified as designs; Linux has an SGX driver, arch/x86/kernel/cpu/sgx, and TDX
   configuration in arch/x86/Kconfig, read); the repository records that enclaves on a shared
   core suffer every microarchitectural channel (hardware-vulnerabilities.md, the SGX and TEE
   attacks row, and rule C15 against enclave claims without partitioned hardware, read). The
   RISC-V physical memory protection registers (Linux arch/riscv/include/asm/csr.h, PMP_R, read)
   and the security extensions are modes of every core (unverified beyond the register names).
   Arm CCA realms are a state of every core (unverified). [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "i like your design. approved." (errata 86); "constant-time AES and SHA
   instructions" (errata 85); the decision of 2026-11-10 above; DIT (9.3.7).
   BODY: Sections 7.11 and 7.11.3, and 10.4.3.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.9.7
ORDER: 106   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 9 The instruction set
   TITLE: The worst-case latency of the block operations
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (latency bound of block operations)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: An ordinary instruction is bounded: at most six bytes of fetch across two pages and
   one memory operand across two pages. A block operation (MCPY, MSET, MCMP, MSCN and the rest,
   9.11.3 is not bounded by its definition; it keeps its progress in registers and may be
   interrupted between steps, and an implementation may move more than a byte per step provided
   the registers are consistent where an exception can be taken (9.11.5). So an
   interrupt-latency figure depends on the step.
   WHY IT MATTERS: A claim of real-time response rests on a bound. Until the step is stated, the
   claim is an argument and not a number.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) No architectural bound: each implementation states its granularity. For: freedom to move a
      line or a page at a time. Against: software cannot rely on a latency.
   B) An architectural maximum between interrupt checks (one cache line, 64 bytes). For: a bound
      that holds on every core. Against: ties a fast engine to a small step.
   C) An architectural maximum of one page between checks (4 KiB). For: the largest unit with a
      single translation; bulk engines work at full speed. Against: tens of microseconds of
      latency on a slow memory.
   D) A feature register that reports the granularity. For: software reads the figure. Against:
      it is information and not a promise.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Options A and 4 together: no bound in the architecture,
   the granularity reported in a register. This is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: x86 REP MOVS is interruptible between iterations and its progress is in
   registers; ARM's FEAT_MOPS leaves the progress in registers; the VAX MOVC kept a
   first-part-done bit in the status word (all unverified: general knowledge).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The VAX MOVC5 as the argument against hidden state (9.11); the registers
   as the only progress.
   BODY: Sections 9.11.5 and 9.21.1.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.10.1
ORDER: 107   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 10 Conclusion: FPGA to silicon
   TITLE: Which rules keep a faster core from the speculative and timing flaws
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (data-independent timing mode)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author decided (errata 85; 2026-10-22: "no hammer-row, no spectre, no timing
   channel attacks") that the architecture does not repeat the flaws of the last decade, and
   that a secure core has every timing channel removed (10.4.1, 10.4.3). What is not yet chosen
   is the exact rule set for a future out-of-order core, and the mechanism that makes execution
   time independent of data.
   WHY IT MATTERS: A rule fixed while the specification is being written costs little; one
   retrofitted after silicon costs a respin.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Rules in the report only. The rules (for example: no speculation across protection
      domains; no predictor state shared across a privilege boundary without a flush) stay
      advice in the hardware-vulnerabilities report. For: nothing to implement now. Against:
      nothing makes an implementer follow them.
   B) Rules in the architecture text. They are stated as requirements of the specification, with
      a declared set of instructions whose timing is independent of operand values. For:
      testable. Against: the declared set and a data-independent-timing control (a mode bit or a
      core class) are not chosen (proposal P17).
   BODY: Section 10.4.1.

ENTRY J.D.1
ORDER: 108   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: Appendix D, the ABI
   TITLE: Stack alignment
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (stack pointer rule; ABI)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The stack pointer R14 must be kept at some multiple, so that pushes and locals land at
   addresses a quad (8-byte) access can use. Version 1 of the ABI says the stack pointer is a
   multiple of 8 at all times (D.1). The author asked: "why not 2 bytes instead of 8?" Two is
   the right number only when the machine pushes 16-bit words, as the PDP-11 does. Here a push,
   a return address and an argument slot are each 8 bytes (a call pushes the return address),
   and a quad access to an address that is not a multiple of 8 aborts with ALIGN (section 7.4 of
   the instruction set: [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.D.1.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: In hardware, the 8-byte rule is checked by the existing ALIGN abort and costs
   no gates; a stricter rule (16) could be checked when R14 is written or left to the first
   access. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) 8 at all times, checked by the quad ALIGN abort (the present ABI). For: the smallest
      number that every push and spill obeys; no padding beyond rounding frames to 8; a
      violation is caught by the first quad access. Against: a future 128-bit type or vector
      needs a declared realignment (option F) or an ABI version change.
   B) 2 (what the author asked). The stack pointer is a multiple of 2, so a byte-sized local is
      not rounded to 8. For: saves on average 4 bytes per frame on a machine with little memory
      (the PDP-11's reason); matches the 2-byte parcel of instructions. Against: pushes of quads
      would land at misaligned addresses and abort, so each push becomes two accesses (MOVUQ) or
      the compiler rounds every frame anyway and nothing is saved; PUSHM, CALL and exception
      entry would be unaligned stores; the pointer-tag trick of the instruction set depends on
      quad alignment; any frame with a quad local needs rounding.
   C) 4 (long alignment). For: a halfway number that matches int; no padding for a frame of
      32-bit data only. Against: the same problem as option B for every quad push; the save area
      stays 8-aligned only by luck; any quad local needs 8, so there is no gain.
   D) 16 at all times. The usual hardware-friendly number. For: room for a 128-bit pair (R0:R1)
      and future vectors without an ABI change; the same as the three most used ABIs, so ported
      code and compilers assume it. Against: on average 8 bytes of padding per frame and about
      one extra instruction in some prologues; the variadic save area plus the return address
      needs 8 padding bytes to keep va_list contiguous, so D.2 would change; every exception
      frame must round; a stronger constraint than the one the author called horrible.
   E) 8 at all times, 16 at calls only. The caller guarantees 16 when it executes the call; the
      callee may move the stack pointer by pushes. For: only functions that use a 16-aligned
      object pay; leaf frames keep 8-byte steps. Against: a callee cannot know the alignment at
      an arbitrary point unless it tracks the parity of its own pushes; interrupt and signal
      handlers have no guarantee; hand-written assembly is harder; it adds a rule hidden in the
      parity of the stack pointer.
   F) 8 is the ABI, and a function that needs more declares it. Its prologue realigns the stack
      pointer and keeps the old one in the frame pointer. For: pay only where needed; no ABI
      version change when a wider type arrives; longjmp is still correct because it restores R14
      whole. Against: makes R13, optional today, mandatory in those functions; breaks alloca and
      unwinding unless the unwinder knows; a compiler feature used only once something wider
      exists.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A, keeping 8, and answering the author's question
   in the ABI text: 2 would be right only if a push were a 16-bit word, and every push here is a
   quad while a misaligned quad access aborts. Option F is reserved for the first type that
   needs more. This is the assistant's view, not a decision. It would change if a 128-bit type,
   a vector or a 128-bit long double enters the language ABI, or if unmodified third-party code
   that assumes 16 is to run.
   PRECEDENTS: The ABI of version 1 itself: 8 at all times (verified).; The PDP-11 kept the
   stack pointer even (a word) and trapped on an odd one (unverified: general knowledge); the
   VAX stack pointer is longword-aligned by convention (unverified).; 32-bit ARM before its EABI
   and early i386 ABIs used 4 (unverified).; x86-64 System V: 16 at the call instruction, 8
   after the return address is pushed; AArch64 AAPCS64: 16, enforced by hardware at
   stack-pointer-based accesses if enabled; RISC-V psABI: 16 (all unverified: general
   knowledge).; gcc -mstackrealign and __attribute__((force_align_arg_pointer)), with the AND
   SP,#-16 prologue (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The PDP-11 as the base ("update and refresh of the pdp-11 instruction set
   and architecture", "upgrade it to 32/64 bit keeping nice properties"). On alignment: "only 2
   byte memory alignment; don't put 8 byte memory alignment constraints that would be horrible",
   and later "align 64 bit values the natural way; as long as we have circuitry to access
   individual bytes i'm ok with loading in 64 bits". "Stack alignment why not 2 bytes instead of
   8?" The VAX-11 register roles ("SP is R14 and FP is R13 to match it"). Ockham: "don't
   multiply entities without benefit".
   BODY: Sections D.1 and D.4.2.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.D.2
ORDER: 109   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: Appendix D, the ABI
   TITLE: Binding, landing pads and the PLT stub after version 1
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (ABI and PLT stub code)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: Three matters of the ABI are version 1 decisions with a question behind them. Landing
   pads: LPAD marks a legal target of an indirect jump or call; version 1 leaves enforcement off
   and the assembler provides .lpad; the author approved a page bit that enforces them (errata
   84, item 5). When enforcement is mandatory, and whether it is optional first, is not decided:
   a tagged function pointer reached by JMP -k(Rn) must still land on an LPAD, and JTAG, which
   jumps through a table, would need a pad at each entry. Binding: imports are called through
   CALLI got_slot(PC) with no PLT stub (decided, 9.16.2). [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.D.2.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Every binary built before landing pads become mandatory lacks the pads, so
   the later the decision the more binaries break; a linker, a loader and a compiler must agree
   on the binding rule; the page-bit mechanism of J.9.6 enforces whatever is chosen.
OPTIONS:
  Options (landing pads):
   A) Mandatory from version 1. For: no binary ever lacks the pads. Against: two bytes at every
      address-taken function and indirect call target, now, before the hardware enforces
      anything.
   B) Optional in version 1, mandatory in version 2, enforced per page by the page bit. For: the
      ABI version number exists for this; a binary opts in. Against: binaries built in the
      meantime must be rebuilt.
   C) Optional for ever, enforced only for the pages that carry the bit. For: no flag day.
      Against: a binary that does not opt in is unprotected, and the attack surface is the part
      that does not.
  Options (binding):
   D) Eager binding only. All imports resolved at load, the table made read-only afterwards.
      For: no resolver in the program; the table is read-only after load. Against: the load time
      grows with the number of imports.
   E) Lazy binding by a stub per import. The table entry points at a stub that identifies the
      symbol and calls the resolver. For: fast start. Against: reintroduces the stub that the
      one-instruction call avoids, and a writable table.
   F) Lazy binding through a resolver that finds the symbol from the return address. For: no
      per-import stub. Against: the resolver must decode the call site; the table stays
      writable.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B for landing pads, and eager binding with a
   read-only table (option D), because the call is then one instruction with no stub and there
   is no writable table to attack. This is the assistant's view, not a decision.
   PRECEDENTS: Intel's indirect-branch tracking and ARM's branch target identification are
   enforced by OpenBSD where the hardware has them (appendix A); ld.so with LD_BIND_NOW and -z
   now against lazy binding, and RELRO, which makes the table read-only after load (unverified:
   general knowledge); the i386 position-independent executable that kept a register for the
   table base (9.16 commentary).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: Landing pads gated by a page bit, "with the fault attributed to the
   branch" (errata 84); "minimize recovery code".
   BODY: Sections 9.16.6, 9.21.1, A.1.2, D.1 and D.4.2.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY H.3.17
ORDER: 112   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: 3.17 Anti-tearing sugar
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (atomics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: The sugar is wanted (3.15.1 in the body, the author's). "So that
   the common program needs no more than a store" is the assistant's aim. The candidates, with
   what each costs, are proposals: * A scanout base register that latches at the vertical blank
   (a page flip: the register holds the address of the picture to show and takes the new value
   only in the blank), with a completion interrupt or a fence (a counter that the device
   advances when the flip has happened, which the program can read or wait on, so it knows that
   the old picture is free). Gives: no tearing, no polling of the raster; the program writes one
   register and goes on. Costs: two pictures of memory for the program that uses it, and a rule
   for what the register shows if it is written twice in one frame (the first is dropped, or the
   second waits). Already in 3.8.9, except the fence. * Per-layer latching, in a design with
   layers (3.21, sketches 1 and 2): each layer has its own base register and flips
   independently, so a cursor or a sprite layer can move every frame while the text layer flips
   only when its text changes. Gives: the cheapest case for text, a moving cursor with no
   redraw. Costs: one set of registers per layer. * An atomic commit of several registers:
   several registers are staged and applied together at the blank (this is what ARM of 3.8.9 is
   for the single set; [... full text in Appendix H]
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

ENTRY H.3.21.3

   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (atomics)

   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 3.21.3 A device whose main interface is a ring of commands in the
   manner of virtio-gpu, with a tiny shader-like fixed program for drawing glyphs and sprites
   and video memory managed by the device, and a peek/poke compatibility window on top for the
   programs that want to poke cells and pixels. (figure omitted) What the programmer sees.
   Mostly commands: "put this text at this cell", "move this sprite", "fill this rectangle",
   "present"; and, as a convenience, the window: cells and pixels as plain memory that mirrors
   the device's. The device allocates its own video memory, so a program does not choose
   addresses. In video memory: what the device decides; the program sees handles. At scanout:
   the device renders the commands into whatever it scans out, at its own pace, and flips at the
   blank by itself (the sugar is free: a commit is a command). Costs: a command processor (a
   queue engine, as the virtio devices of chapter 2 already are), a drawing engine of fixed
   programs (the small piece of the GPU that the terminal needs: a textured quad with a glyph
   and two colours, a sprite), a memory manager for video memory and a consistency rule between
   the window and the queue; more gates than sketches 1 and 2 by a clear margin (a judgement of
   mine, unmeasured), and more software in the driver. Gives up: the plainness of sketch 1; the
   program must know the device's model, and two ways to reach one cell must be reconciled.
   Properties kept: [... full text in Appendix H]
OPTIONS:

   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: The ROM's details (moved; its job as pseudocode, and 4.4.1, are in
   the body). The ROM does five things in order: find a boot device, read block 0 into low
   memory, check that it looks like a boot block (the 0x55AA signature), set core 0's registers,
   and start core 0 at the block. The assistant's fuller pseudocode, with the register state: (a
   code example is omitted here; see the manual) read_block (the standard disk: one virtio-blk
   request, 5.6.2): build a three-descriptor chain (request header, a 512-byte buffer at
   address, a one-byte status); FENCE; write the queue's doorbell; poll the used ring until the
   chain returns. The ROM polls (checks in a loop) rather than using interrupts. Inference, not
   stated: the ROM runs before any software has programmed the interrupt controller (2.6.3: how
   the interrupt controller is programmed is not specified), so there is nothing to interrupt
   it; polling needs no set-up, and 2.7.8 shows the polled form. It needs a driver for each kind
   of boot device; for the standard disk that is the virtio-blk reader of chapter 5: find the
   function in the bus's configuration space, set up one queue, read one block. Which device
   comes first, and how the ROM names a device in r0, are not specified (F10 of
   decisions-pending.md leaves the order after the disk open). The reset state of core 0 and the
   load address . Block 0 is loaded above the table, at 0x1000 in this chapter's example (the
   address is "to be chosen"; [... full text in Appendix H]

ENTRY H.4.7
ORDER: 117   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: 4.7 Starting the other cores
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (start register for the other cores)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: The pdp64's rule: one register write. A pdp64 core has no mode
   ladder, so it can start in its normal mode at any address. The controller holds every core
   but core 0 in reset; to start core n, the kernel writes, to a register block in the
   controller that belongs to core n, the address where the core should begin and one argument,
   and the controller releases the core into kernel mode at priority 7 with its core number in
   r0 and the argument in r1. By the kernel's convention the argument points to a structure that
   belongs to that core (its stack, its per-CPU data). A core that sticks is reset by a write to
   a core-reset register and started again. (a code example is omitted here; see the manual)
   (figure omitted) The cost is one register block in the controller, with a start register and
   a reset hold per core. The started core has no translation or stack of its own; the kernel's
   entry code sets them up in its first instructions, as the first kernel does (6.12). Whether a
   started core begins with translation off, and what MMUCTL it sees, are not specified.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

ENTRY H.4.7.2
ORDER: 119   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: 4.7.2 A stuck core is reset by a write to a core-reset register.
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (memory ordering)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 4.7.2 A stuck core is reset by a write to a core-reset register.
   Other chapters. This is option 1 of boot decision B2 and of R2 in decisions-pending.md, which
   recommend it and leave it proposed (the other options were the x86 signals, a firmware call,
   a spin table and a table that all cores start from). Chapter 7 stated the same rule (H.7,
   7.8.1) and loaded r0 only, where 4.7.1 loads r1 too; the difference is to settle (4.13).
   Waiting uses WFE and SEV (7.10); an inter-processor interrupt, a signal from one core to
   another, is a write to a per-core register of the interrupt controller (H.7, 7.8.2).
   Commentary. Chain. PDP-11: one processor, nothing to start. VAX: the console processor
   started the others (unverified). x86: INIT and START-UP signals and a trampoline through
   three modes. arm64 and RISC-V: a firmware call or a spin loop. pdp64: a register write.
   Trade-offs. With no mode ladder the whole protocol is one write, at the cost of a small
   register block in the controller. Benefits. No trampoline, no firmware call, fewer lines than
   any system counted, and one recovery path, a core reset. Impact. Kernel: starting a core is a
   register write. Compiler: none. Hardware: a start register and a reset hold per core.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: The author decided that inference is the target (8.5.1 of the

   docs/pdp64-lb64-accelerator.md after the author asked "what would a gpu look like that is
   optimized purely for samsung's littlebit-2 encoding" (register row R136). No decision of the
   author names the design, and it was written for the register model of the first device
   family, which errata 95 later set aside for virtio. It is kept here as a proposal; the
   statements below, which the chapter had tagged decided, are entered as proposed for that
   reason. LB64 is option 1 of J.8.4, and, if the engine is made standard with optional sets
   (J.8.1, option 6), it would be one of those sets; that reading is the assistant's, and no
   decision of the author's says it. LB64 is for one kind of model, in which the weights are
   stored as one sign bit each, with a few 16-bit scale factors per row and column. With weights
   of plus or minus one, a multiplication of an activation by a weight is the activation itself
   or its negative, so a layer needs adders and a sign flip and no multipliers: the accumulator
   adds or subtracts each activation according to its weight bit (sign-flip accumulation). The
   device streams the weights from RAM at the memory's speed.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

DECISION:

ENTRY H.A.4.1
ORDER: 135   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: Appendix A
   TITLE: A.4.1 There are four ways to protect the return address:...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (return-address protection: new instructions or checks)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: A.4.1 There are four ways to protect the return address: software
   canaries and OpenBSD's return-address protection in the compiler, a hardware return stack, a
   shadow stack, and nothing. The mirror shadow stack is approved (errata 85, study item 6); the
   others are the alternatives that were weighed. Commentary. Trade-offs. A shadow stack is the
   strongest and brings the most recovery code (a second stack to overflow, switch and unwind),
   so the analysis did not recommend it by default; software detection costs a few instructions
   per function and no hardware. Verdict (the assistant's, before the decision). Start with the
   compiler; consider a hardware mechanism only if code size allows. Benefits. Detection with
   little or no recovery code. Impact. Compiler: The compiler adds a canary or a return-address
   check per function. Hardware: Software costs nothing; a hardware return stack costs a second
   stack structure and its control.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

ENTRY H.B.1.8.1
ORDER: 136   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: Appendix B
   TITLE: B.1.8.1 The proposal is two free-running counters, cycles and instructions...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (instruction-set chapter)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: B.1.8.1 The proposal is two free-running counters, cycles and
   instructions retired, readable in user mode, added after the clock (P3) with the same enable
   bit. Commentary. Precedent. Unix profiled by sampling the program counter at clock ticks.
   Trade-offs. A user-readable cycle counter is a timing channel; a full counter block with
   event selection costs a large block and interrupt machinery. Modern comparables. x86 RDPMC,
   the AArch64 PMU, RISC-V's hpm counters. Impact. Kernel: A profiler reads counters instead of
   sampling at ticks. Compiler: Intrinsics. Hardware: Two counters and an enable bit.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

ENTRY H.B.1.10.1
ORDER: 137   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: Appendix B
   TITLE: B.1.10.1 The proposal is an ABI rule:...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (an ABI rule)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: B.1.10.1 The proposal is an ABI rule: literal pools move out of
   .text into a read-only section beside it, so code pages hold only instructions and can be
   execute-only. Commentary. Precedent. The PDP-11's immediates sit in the instruction stream,
   and with separate instruction and data spaces a program could not read its own instructions
   as data. Trade-offs. The PC-relative reach limits how far the pool may be, and the linker
   must keep the two sections near each other. Benefits. Zero hardware; a memory-disclosure bug
   no longer reveals code. Modern comparables. OpenBSD makes code execute-only on amd64 and
   arm64; ARM compilers offer a flag to keep pools out of code. Impact. Kernel: None. Compiler:
   Pools are emitted into a read-only section beside the text. Hardware: None; the linker keeps
   the sections within PC-relative reach.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

ENTRY H.B.2.2.1
ORDER: 138   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: Appendix B
   TITLE: B.2.2.1 The proposal is none:...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (instruction-set chapter)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: B.2.2.1 The proposal is none: everything goes through devices, with
   a required reset register and a watchdog in the bus controller. The kernel survey disagrees
   and would specify a firmware-call convention on reserved trap vectors. Commentary. Precedent.
   The PDP-11 kernel never called firmware; the VAX had console registers the kernel read and
   wrote. Trade-offs. No firmware means no firmware to trust and no extra privilege level, and
   every platform must say where its console and reset registers are, which the others get from
   firmware. Modern comparables. RISC-V's supervisor binary interface, ARM's PSCI and secure
   monitor calls, Alpha's PALcode, SPARC's Open Firmware. Impact. Kernel: Reboot and power-off
   through a device register and a watchdog. Compiler: None. Hardware: A reset register and a
   watchdog timer in the controller.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.

   TITLE: B.2.14.1 Whether USB is a conventional ring-based host controller driven by an...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: maybe (instruction-set chapter)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: B.2.14.1 Whether USB is a conventional ring-based host controller
   driven by an existing operating-system USB stack, the proposal of chapter 5, or a controller
   that does the protocol itself. Commentary. Trade-offs. A controller that does the protocol
   moves it into the chip and takes the stack out of the operating system; the conventional
   controller uses code that already exists. virtio reuses OpenBSD's existing drivers in the
   emulator and in silicon. Modern comparables. xHCI for the conventional controller;
   virtio-mmio on every emulated RISC-V and ARM board. Impact. Kernel: A disk driver serves a
   USB disk; virtio reuses the existing drivers. Compiler: None. Hardware: USB protocol in the
   controller costs gates; virtio costs nothing for the emulator.

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.2.1
ORDER: 175   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 2 Bus
   TITLE: Locating devices without a selecting bit
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (addresses and boot data only)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: On the Unibus, an address whose top bits were all ones meant "a device register", so
   every driver knew where its device was. The pdp64 has no such bit (errata 97, 102): a device
   window is an ordinary range of physical addresses, and the physical address is at least 40
   and at most 64 bits wide. Software must therefore learn from somewhere where each window is,
   and something must choose where it goes. There are three ways to do it: [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.2.1.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel cannot attach a driver before it knows a base address. With no
   address bit to mark a device page, the memory type of a mapping is the only marker of what a
   page is. The root complex must decode exactly the windows that were assigned, and must refuse
   a device's DMA (direct memory access: [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) PCI Express enumeration, with the root complex address and a window pool in the
      boot-information block. The kernel assigns each function's base-address registers (the
      registers by which a device states the size of a window it wants and is told where it
      sits) from a range of addresses that the block gives it. For: one mechanism for everything
      on the bus; only two numbers are passed (the base of the configuration space and the
      window pool); the platform may move them freely, so the architecture fixes nothing;
      drivers learn their base from the device. Against: the kernel must trust the pool, and a
      pool that overlaps RAM can be detected only by comparing ranges; the console needs a
      separate base before enumeration has run.
   B) A device table in the boot-information block (kind, base, size, interrupt for each
      device), with no enumeration for the platform's own devices. For: suits devices that are
      not on PCI Express at all; needs no walk of the bus. Against: duplicates what enumeration
      finds, so there are two mechanisms; a device that arrives later is not in the table.
   C) A fixed platform map written in the architecture: the root complex and a window range at
      fixed addresses. For: no table to trust and no problem early in boot; very simple for a
      small board. Against: it fixes an address below 2^40, the smallest width, and so brings
      back the habit of the upper half of the address space that the author dropped; a wider
      machine cannot put its windows high; the VAX shows that a fixed map is changed from model
      to model.
   D) Top-down placement: the root-complex window ends at the top of the implemented physical
      address space, so it moves with the width the processor reports. For: never collides with
      RAM, which grows up from zero; one rule for 40 and for 64 bits. Against: depends on the
      width being discoverable (an open question of its own); a program that assumes a width
      computes a wrong address; memory sizes near the top collide with it.
   E) An address-map list handed over at boot, each range typed as RAM, device or reserved, with
      the memory type of a page as the only runtime marker. For: one place says what every range
      is; a window can sit anywhere; the kernel can refuse to map RAM as a device and the
      reverse. Against: the list can be wrong; a wrong memory type on a device page is no longer
      caught by hardware, as the old address bit caught it; more kernel code.
   F) A self-describing window-map register block at one fixed low address, just above the first
      pages, through which everything else is found. For: only one address is architectural, and
      it is in the reserved boot area; no block is needed. Against: a new device and a new fixed
      address; the reservation of the first pages must cover its page.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A, with the memory type of the page as the safety
   check and a kernel rule that a device mapping is made only on an enumerated window. It is the
   text the manual already has, it adds no table and it works from 40 to 64 bits. It would
   change if the author wants platform devices without PCI Express (a timer, a console, an
   interrupt controller); then Option B's table is added for those alone.
   PRECEDENTS: The RISC-V virt board of QEMU gives the configuration space at 0x30000000 and the
   PCI Express windows at 0x40000000 and describes them to the kernel (verified: QEMU
   hw/riscv/virt.c). The x86 ACPI table that names the configuration-space base (unverified:
   general knowledge). The VAX had the Interconnect (the bus of the VAX-11/780) and a table that
   its adapter slots filled; its I/O space began at 0x20000000 with one 8 KB window per adapter
   slot, changing from model to model (verified: the 4.3BSD nexus.h read for chapter 2). The
   PDP-11 I/O page at the top of the 16-bit space, and the extended spaces of the later models
   (verified for the 16-bit page; the 18- and 22-bit extensions unverified). The VAX RPB and the
   Alpha HWRPB (restart and hardware parameter blocks that hand a device table to the operating
   system) and device trees on ARM and RISC-V (unverified: general knowledge). [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: USB, enumerated over a PCI Express bus ("arbitrary buses ... can be
   connected to it and enumerated"); virtio "across the board"; the VAX's I/O space ("it should
   be noted in the book how the vax does this io space idea"); "mmio allows larger windows as
   needed"; and, of the old address bit, "if we are going to mmap petabytes, we need those
   bits".
   BODY: Sections 2.2 and 2.4.6.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.2.2
ORDER: 176   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 2 Bus
   TITLE: The bus controller
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (bus side; no instruction)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: An early decision (errata 49) made the bus a device in its own right, with registers
   by which the processor could detect what is on it, ask it to map a device to a given address
   range, and be alerted when a device enters or leaves. The register interface was later
   dropped (errata 95), but the three jobs were kept: the bus enumerates (lists what is on it),
   maps (assigns each device a window) and alerts (tells the processor of arrivals and
   departures). Under PCI Express the three jobs already have standard mechanisms: enumeration
   by reading each function's configuration space through the root complex; [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.2.2.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel needs one recursive walk of the bus (section 2.4.3) and one
   routine to recover from a departure; the mature PCI code of OpenBSD can be reused only if the
   machine presents a standard PCI Express bus. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Pure PCI Express. The root complex is the bus controller; there is no pdp64 device;
      enumeration, window assignment, hot-plug and error reporting are the standard ones
      (configuration space, slot status, message-signalled interrupts, advanced error
      reporting); the pdp64's own rules live in how the processor treats the root complex. For:
      the least new design; compatible with every card; OpenBSD already enumerates PCI Express.
      Against: needs a root complex the cheap boards lack; the notorious configuration
      difficulties of PCI (about thirty boot options in the BSD and Linux kernels for bus
      numbers and window assignment; guessed window sizes for hot-plugged devices) come with it;
      a removed device reads as all ones, the opposite of the precise fault of 2.4.5, which
      would need extra circuitry anyway.
   B) PCI Express plus a small pdp64 root-complex function (the shape of the proposals of
      appendix H.2). Enumeration and hot-plug are standard; the root complex adds a few
      processor-side registers and rules: a bus reset write, the departure alert, the precise
      bus fault and drop-and-count for a departed window, and a reset that produces no removal
      events. These stay invisible to cards. For: it keeps the three verbs of errata 49 and
      gives them to standard mechanisms; departure is detected and recoverable. Against: still
      needs the root complex; the extras are proposals whose detail is not specified; the
      precise-fault rule needs presence tracked per function, which standard PCI Express
      provides only for hot-plug slots.
   C) virtio-mmio devices behind a native bus controller (errata 49 as written, with virtio
      devices). The controller is a small memory-mapped device with a table of present devices
      (type, base, size, interrupt) and an alert register; each device is a virtio-mmio
      transport (virtio's form for machines without PCI) in a window the controller assigned.
      For: no root complex, so the first boards work; it gives errata 49 exactly. Against: gives
      up compatibility with PCI Express and the plan to attach USB as a PCI Express function; a
      real card (a network adapter, a graphics card, NVMe, a USB controller) cannot be attached;
      the drivers carry two transports; it reverses decisions of 2026-11-03.
   D) A soft host-bridge facade. The processor always sees a configuration space and virtio-PCI
      functions; on the FPGA, a small block in the fabric presents simple devices as PCI
      functions with no PCI Express link; on silicon, a real root complex. For: software sees
      one bus on every board; no hard block is needed on the first boards. Against: custom logic
      that must emulate enough configuration space for the kernel's PCI code to accept it, so a
      fault in it looks like a driver fault; no real card on that board; one more block to test.
   E) A static description for the fixed machine; hot-plug only for USB. The boot-information
      block or a small device tree lists the root complex and every fixed device; the kernel
      enumerates once at boot; only the USB host controller reports arrivals and departures; PCI
      Express slot hot-plug is software only (the fault and event rules, no slot hardware) until
      a board has slots. For: fewest moving parts; the bus controller becomes boot data.
      Against: no live add or remove of anything but USB; a device tree is another artefact the
      boot ROM must carry and keep in step with the hardware; statement 2.4.4 would shrink to
      USB. It is compatible with Option A or 2 underneath.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B as written, with Option E's limit on hot-plug
   (USB live; PCI Express in software only), and two things put to the author: where the
   configuration space and the windows sit (for example a fixed base outside the first few
   pages), and a short table of the controller's registers (reset, departure alert, presence).
   It delivers the three verbs with standard mechanisms and costs a few registers beyond PCI
   Express. It would change if the first board has no PCI Express block and no budget for a soft
   core (then Option D or 3), or if the departure fault turns out not to be buildable on
   root-port hardware (then a presence register per function).
   PRECEDENTS: On x86, ARM and RISC-V servers the configuration space is reached through a
   memory-mapped window (ECAM, the enhanced configuration access mechanism) and hot-plug uses
   the slot capability and its interrupt (unverified: general knowledge). PCI sizes a window by
   writing all ones to the base register and reading back (not confirmed in the project's own
   sources). The VAX scanned its interconnect through an adapter on the nexus: probenexi() reads
   each slot's status register, and the UNIBUS adapter's own registers hold the map registers
   (verified: the 4.3BSD sources vaxuba/ubareg.h and vax/autoconf.c, read for chapter 2). Amiga
   AutoConfig, in which each board announces itself and is told where to live (unverified:
   general knowledge). virtio-mmio is what QEMU's virt and microVM machines use where PCI is
   absent (unverified: general knowledge). [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The first idea, in his words: "the bus itself is another device with its
   own set of mmap registers so we can detect what is on it and request it to map a device to a
   particular address range, we do need some way for the bus to alert us when a device enters or
   leaves the bus"; the eight registers were later rejected ("forget the native 8 register
   devices. gone."). USB enumerated over a PCI Express bus; "we're going with pcie and nvme and
   usb"; and "didn't we talk about hot swap?".
   BODY: Section 2.4.4.

ENTRY J.2.3
ORDER: 177   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 2 Bus
   TITLE: The IOMMU: table format, place, faults and virtualization
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (device translation, not the processor's)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author has decided that the pdp64 has an IOMMU (a unit that translates the
   addresses a device uses, with a page table of its own for each device) and accepted five
   rules for it (errata 75, 85): translation on by default, one domain for each device, no
   kernel pointers in descriptors, permanent mappings for the rings, bounce buffers for small
   data. The leaf entry shares bits 0 to 39 with the processor's page-table entry and bits 40 to
   63 are free (errata 85). Four things are not decided: [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.2.3.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel's mapping routines, the driver interface (a descriptor holds a
   device address, not a physical address) and the interrupt controller (J.6.3, which may use
   the same unit to check where a device's interrupt write goes) all depend on it. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A device table indexed by the bus-device-function number, one page table for each entry.
      For: the shape PCI Express already identifies devices with; each device its own domain
      falls out. Against: a large sparse table unless it is indexed in levels; functions behind
      a bridge may share one identity.
   B) A small table of domains, with the device-to-domain map in a register file in the root
      complex. For: cheap in hardware; fits the first FPGA boards. Against: a fixed number of
      devices; a hot-plugged device may find the table full.
   C) Same, with a second-level table, so a guest's mapping is composed with the host's (nested
      translation). For: virtualization costs no emulation. Against: two walks for every access;
      a larger unit.
   D) Report a fault only to the kernel, as an interrupt with a log entry (device, address,
      kind), the device's access being refused or dropped. For: one path. Against: a device
      waiting for a read completion hangs unless the unit completes it with an error.
   E) Report a fault to the device as a failed completion and also log it. For: the device's
      driver sees an error at once. Against: not every device handles it; the log can overflow
      under attack.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A with a two-level device table, and Option E (an
   error completion and a log entry), because both keep the same code for boot and for a
   hot-added device. It would change if virtualization is wanted early (then Option C).
   PRECEDENTS: x86 has the IOMMU of Intel (VT-d) and of AMD, indexed by bus, device and
   function, with a fault-recording ring and an interrupt (unverified: general knowledge). ARM
   has the SMMU with stream identifiers and stage 1 and stage 2 translation (unverified). The
   VAX Unibus adapter's map registers were per adapter, not per device (verified: 4.3BSD
   ubareg.h).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "iommu will allow us to map in c64/c128" (errata 75, a later use); the
   five accepted recommendations (errata 85).
   BODY: Section 2.11.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.2.4
ORDER: 178   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 2 Bus
   TITLE: Which optional virtio features the pdp64 uses
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (virtio features)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: virtio lets each side agree optional features by bit. Three concern the machine and
   not one device. VIRTIO_RING_F_EVENT_IDX (bit 29) replaces the two on-off flags with an index
   at which a side wants to be woken. VIRTIO_F_ACCESS_PLATFORM (bit 33) says that a device's
   addresses go through the platform's translation (the IOMMU). VIRTIO_F_ORDER_PLATFORM (bit 36)
   says that the device obeys the platform's ordering rules and not weaker ones. The exact
   feature subset of each standard device is a fourth, per-device item, set when each device is
   written.
   WHY IT MATTERS: A driver on a machine with an IOMMU that does not agree ACCESS_PLATFORM
   leaves the device on physical addresses and defeats the unit; a device that is not told to
   order its writes may let an interrupt overtake data (2.5.4). EVENT_IDX changes the
   notification test in every driver.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Require ACCESS_PLATFORM and ORDER_PLATFORM of every device and refuse the device without
      them. For: the IOMMU and the ordering rule hold for every device. Against: some emulators
      and old devices do not offer them.
   B) Require them on the physical machine, accept their absence under an emulator with a
      warning. For: runs on QEMU as is. Against: two behaviours; the secure case is not the
      tested one.
   C) Do not use EVENT_IDX: the two flags only. For: smaller driver, nothing to get wrong.
      Against: more doorbells and interrupts under load.
   D) Use EVENT_IDX when the device offers it. For: fewer notifications. Against: more code and
      a corner case at index wrap; the polled mode needs no help from it.
   E) Declare nothing beyond VERSION_1, and treat translation and ordering as properties of the
      platform, not negotiated. For: fewest bits. Against: a driver cannot tell that a device
      honours them.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A for the first two, and Option C until a
   measurement shows a need for EVENT_IDX.
   PRECEDENTS: Linux negotiates ACCESS_PLATFORM and refuses translation-less operation on a
   platform that needs it, and uses EVENT_IDX when offered (unverified: general knowledge). The
   bit numbers are in the Linux headers virtio_config.h and virtio_ring.h (verified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "virtio across the board" (errata 95).
   BODY: Section 2.7.

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY J.2.5
ORDER: 179   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 2 Bus
   TITLE: The root complex's own registers and the pdp64's additions
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (root complex registers)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: Three decided rules name a register of the root complex and give no place for it: a
   bus reset is a write to a register (2.9.4), a read of an empty configuration position is
   recorded in a sticky, write-one-to-clear bit of its status register (2.4.8, errata 104), and
   the circuitry that the pdp64 adds to PCI Express for convenience and security is mentioned
   without detail (2.3.4). A fourth, where the root complex itself sits, is J.2.1. The author
   asked that the miss be recorded in a status register and mode flags that already exist and
   not in new state, and agreed to the root complex's own bit; [...]  (Full text: Appendix J,
   J.2.5.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel cannot reset the bus, or tell a dead link from an empty slot,
   without an address; and a block of registers is the natural home for the later additions.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A register block at a fixed offset from the configuration-space base. For: one address
      (the base) is passed; nothing new to discover. Against: shares the address range of the
      functions, and an enumeration walk must skip it.
   B) A PCI Express function on bus 0 whose windows hold the registers. For: found by
      enumeration like everything else; no fixed address. Against: the function needs a driver,
      and the reset is then a write to a device that the reset itself disturbs.
   C) A block at an address in the boot-information block. For: free placement. Against: one
      more field to hand over.
   D) Bits in a system register (the numbers from 13 up are reserved). For: no address. Against:
      the register is per core, so the root complex's single flag lives in a per-core place; the
      author did not choose it.
   E) A block in the interrupt controller's page, if J.6.3 gives that unit its own range. For:
      the registers of the machine's central units together. Against: ties two decisions
      together.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A or 3: pass the base once in the
   boot-information block and place the block at a fixed offset from it.
   PRECEDENTS: The x86 root complex registers (root control, root status) are in the
   configuration space of the root port (unverified: general knowledge). The VAX adapter's
   registers (configuration and status) sit in its nexus window (verified: 4.3BSD ubareg.h).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "don't we already have a status register and mode flags, can't you use a
   bit in one of those or add a bit?"; "agree root complex bit" (errata 104).
   BODY: Sections 2.3.4, 2.4.8 and 2.9.4.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.3.1
ORDER: 180   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: The deferred native devices
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The first design of the graphics and sound hardware was a family of devices of the
   pdp64's own: a video device derived from the Commodore 64's video chip, with 48-bit pixels
   (12 bits for each of red, green, blue and alpha; errata 73); a sound device derived from the
   C64's sound chip, with voices and a general sound path of 48-bit samples (errata 74); and a
   blitter (an engine that copies and combines rectangles of pixels, in the manner of the Blit
   terminal), all three controlled through a small fixed set of registers. [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.3.1.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Each native device is one more driver for the OpenBSD port, which is exactly
   what errata 78 wanted to avoid. Nothing in the instruction set depends on them; the 48-bit
   pixel and sample matter only to software that renders or mixes for them. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Keep as a marked future part. Keep the native-device material in the deferred appendix
      (appendix I, chapter 3), with every statement and tag, rewrite the wording that assumes
      the abolished register model, and state that nothing in the standard depends on it. For:
      preserves the author's work and the 48-bit decisions at the least cost; the author said
      "planned for the future", not "dropped". Against: a body of statements that nobody
      implements; wording written for the old model becomes false and must be caveated; the old
      text may be read as current.
   B) Remove it. Delete the material and keep the decisions only in the record; the graphics
      chapter then says virtio-gpu, virtio-snd, the display output and the framebuffer. For:
      nothing dead is specified; one source of contradiction fewer. Against: loses the Blit and
      C64 design record from the manual; contradicts "planned for the future"; the 48-bit pixel
      decision loses its home.
   C) Redefine as virtio. Treat virtio-gpu and virtio-snd as the whole interface and carry the
      extras as feature bits or formats on those devices (a 48-bit pixel format, a 48-bit sample
      format), implemented in every model. For: one transport driver serves everything; a format
      is a negotiation, not a device. Against: stock hosts and drivers would reject a 48-bit
      format (virtio-gpu's 2D formats are, as far as is known, 8 bits for each channel, and
      virtio-snd's stop at 32- and 64-bit integers and floats; the lists are to be checked), so
      a fork is needed; none of the sprites, raster interrupts or voices survives.
   D) An optional PCI Express card outside the standard. Publish the video, sound and blitter
      designs as an optional card with its own driver, defined from scratch with configuration
      space and message-signalled interrupts; the standard stays virtio. For: keeps the designs
      as real hardware someone may build, in the manner of the optional C128 of errata 77;
      available at any time, since any PCI Express device may be attached. Against: a full
      register specification must finally be written; the first boards have no PCI Express
      block; drivers exist only if someone writes them.
   E) Keep the Blit model as software. Drop the three devices as hardware; keep the Blit's ideas
      (the framebuffer in the address space, the four primitives of bit-block transfer, texture,
      segment and point, half-open rectangles, layers) as a library run by a core the kernel
      assigns, drawing into a virtio-gpu 2D resource and flushing rectangles. For: the Blit
      itself had no pixel-moving hardware and did everything in software; reuses cores that
      exist; no new device (no core is dedicated to graphics, errata 133). Against: one core is
      slow at 1920 by 1080 at 48 bits (12.4 MB a frame) unless it is wide or has vector
      instructions; the C64 and sound personalities vanish.
   F) A pdp64 blit and sprite extension as its own virtio device type, or as a feature of
      virtio-gpu, whose queue carries blit commands (source, destination, strides, rectangle,
      raster operation) and C64-style sprite and raster-interrupt services, so that the idea of
      registers plus DMA becomes a command queue. For: the virtio way to carry what registers
      carried; keeps the author's blit and sprite design on the standard transport. Against: no
      stock host implements a new device, so the device model, the hardware and the driver must
      all be written, which is the "bunch of device drivers" of errata 78; a new device number
      in the shared virtio registry needs care.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A now, with Option E as the intended direction:
   the material is kept in the deferred appendix, and the live part of the chapter is the
   display output (HDMI through a transmitter chip fed a pixel stream), the framebuffer and its
   memory type, now about virtio-gpu and virtio-snd. Removal would contradict the author's
   words; Options C and 6 need a format or device that no stock host provides; Option D stays
   available later. It would change if the author says that the 48-bit pixel and sample are
   required in the first iteration (Option C or 6), that he will not build them (Option B), or
   if a measurement shows that virtio-gpu scan-out cannot reach 1080p on the first board (a
   native device for the FPGA alone, Option D).
   PRECEDENTS: The repository keeps the alternatives of errata 94 as a documented comparison and
   keeps the C128 as an optional device outside the standard (verified: errata 94, 77). The Blit
   terminal (a Motorola 68000 at 8 MHz that drew every pixel itself) (verified: chapter 3
   commentary); Plan 9's draw device and libdraw keep the same primitives in software
   (unverified: general knowledge). VGA-compatible PCI cards that expose a legacy register set
   beside a native one (unverified: general knowledge); the Amiga's fixed custom chips
   (unverified). virtio-gpu carries all its commands as structures in a control queue; whether
   its 2D set has a resource-to-resource blit is not known (unverified: to check). NVMe and xHCI
   also command through queues in memory (verified: chapter 5). OpenBSD has a driver for
   virtio-gpu (verified from a fetched directory listing); [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The Blit ("i like the blit model", "we are basing the design not only on
   the c64, but also on the blit terminal"); "all the c64 graphics and audio goodies" and "48
   bit sound as well, not just the sid"; "our 12 register nic and sound card and graphics card
   are planned for the future"; "a standard audio/video interface so there is no bullshit with
   supporting a bunch of device drivers, although as a pcie bus any device MAY be attached"; the
   C128 as "just another device on the bus; not part of the pdp64 standard"; "modern 48 bit
   audio and 48 bit pixels"; HDMI "like sata and usb, is standard kit".
   BODY: Nowhere: the matter is deferred (appendix I, chapter 3: the sections 3.8 and 3.11 moved
   from the body), and the body does not mention the devices.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.3.4
ORDER: 181   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: Glyph slots against character codes
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author decided a glyph map (3.9.1): a table of slots, each holding the picture of
   one glyph, filled by a TrueType engine (software that draws a glyph from the font's outline
   at the display's pixel size) and used by every cell that names it. What is open, the details,
   is what a cell holds, a character code that a table maps to a slot, or the slot number
   itself; the layout of a slot (width, height, pixel format, whether a slot may span several
   cells for a wide glyph); and the number of slots. [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.3.4.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The choice sets the program's work (naming slots against naming characters),
   whether the scanout needs a lookup table, and how Unicode, wide glyphs and fallback fonts are
   handled.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The cell holds the character code; a table maps code to slot. For: easy for the program,
      which never names slots; matches the chat-era cell. Against: a lookup table in the
      scanout; a policy for codes that have no slot.
   B) The cell holds the slot number. For: nearly free in hardware. Against: the program or a
      library must allocate slots and keep a map from characters to slots.
   C) Both: a cell has a flag that says which. For: ASCII by code and special glyphs by slot.
      Against: two paths in the scanout and the library.
   D) A glyph atlas, as one picture, from which the cells or a blitter copy (the glyph map seen
      as the atlas of the GPU text path of 3.7). For: the same model as the GPU's; the blitter
      can draw a glyph anywhere. Against: needs the blitter of J.3.3 for the pixel layer.
   E) No slots: the library draws glyphs into the pixel layer, as on virtio-gpu (S4). For: no
      hardware. Against: gives up the hardware text layer.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A for the program's sake, if the display is
   hardware; if it is the library of S4 the question disappears.
   PRECEDENTS: The C64's character ROM of 8 bytes a glyph, replaceable by RAM (verified: chapter
   3, unverified numbers); VGA text mode's font in the adapter's memory (unverified); the GPU
   glyph atlas of Alacritty, kitty and WezTerm (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "with glyph maps we can even have a ttf engine generate glyphs and they
   will go into their memory slots for use"; "each cell holds its actual data, not a reference
   to the data" (the chat of 2026-06-18, c64stuff.txt line 5015).
   BODY: Section 3.9.1.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.3.6
ORDER: 182   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: The 48-bit pixel against the formats of virtio-gpu
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author decided a 48-bit pixel, RGBA with 12 bits for each channel (errata 73), and
   a 48-bit sound sample (errata 74), as properties of devices that are deferred (appendix I).
   The standard video device, virtio-gpu 2D, has eight 32-bit formats with 8 bits for each
   channel (B8G8R8A8_UNORM, B8G8R8X8_UNORM, A8R8G8B8_UNORM, X8R8G8B8_UNORM, R8G8B8A8_UNORM,
   X8B8G8R8_UNORM, A8B8G8R8_UNORM, R8G8B8X8_UNORM), and virtio-snd's formats stop at 32-bit
   integers and 64-bit floats. A 48-bit pixel probably exceeds what a display connector carries
   (unverified), so the device that scans out would convert on the way out.
   WHY IT MATTERS: Software that renders at 48 bits has no standard device to show it on; the
   author's wish for 48-bit pixels and samples has no home until this is answered.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The 48-bit formats belong only to a deferred native device. The standard device is 32
      bits. For: nothing is forked. Against: the decision of errata 73 has no standard effect.
   B) A pdp64 format added to virtio-gpu (a 48-bit, or a 64-bit with 16 bits a channel, resource
      format) and likewise for virtio-snd. For: one transport; a format is a negotiation.
      Against: stock hosts and drivers reject it, so a fork is needed; the format lists are to
      be checked.
   C) Render at 48 bits in software, convert to 32 for the flush. For: no change to the device;
      programs keep the precision. Against: twice the memory and a conversion on every frame.
   D) A 64-bit-per-pixel format, 16 bits for each channel, as a power-of-two size. For: aligned;
      keeps all 12 bits. Against: not the author's 48; wastes 16 bits.
   E) The pixel width is a property of the scan-out hardware, converted at the edge to what the
      transmitter takes. For: the standard protocol stays; the hardware keeps the precision.
      Against: the program cannot see it.
   PRECEDENTS: virtio-gpu's formats, from the header (verified: virtio_gpu.h); virtio-snd's
   formats (verified: virtio_snd.h); 10- and 12-bit-per-channel deep colour over HDMI
   (unverified: general knowledge).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "modern 48 bit audio and 48 bit pixels"; "the 48 bit is a new decision of
   mine, rgba format, 12 bits per color/channel"; "i want 48 bit sound as well, not just the
   sid".
   BODY: Section 3.12.1.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.3.7
ORDER: 183   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: The virtio feature subset, hot-plug and the OpenBSD drivers
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (virtio feature subset)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: virtio-gpu has optional parts: the EDID feature (the display reports the modes it
   supports), the fence flag, the cursor queue, blob resources and the 3D features of the
   header. virtio-snd has controls such as volume and flags for shared memory, polling and
   period events. The pdp64 must say which of these every implementation provides. Whether
   OpenBSD has a virtio-snd driver is not known (errata 79: "if not, one is written"); it has
   one for virtio-gpu (a directory listing). The scope of hot-plug (a monitor or a device
   arriving while running) is also open (decisions-pending.md F23).
   WHY IT MATTERS: An application can use a feature only if every machine has it; the three
   models (QEMU, sim64, the FPGA) must agree.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The minimum: 2D, fence, display information, no cursor, no EDID. For: least to implement.
      Against: no cursor, no real mode list.
   B) Minimum plus the cursor queue and EDID. For: a hardware-like cursor; a monitor's real
      modes. Against: more to implement.
   C) All of the 2D features, none of 3D or blob. For: everything a 2D driver uses. Against: the
      blob feature for a core's surface (J.3.12) is excluded.
   D) Everything the header offers. For: no later restriction. Against: 3D and capability sets
      have no use on a core with no shader hardware.
   E) Whatever the first driver (QEMU's) needs, settled by experience. For: honest. Against: the
      architecture states no requirement.
   PRECEDENTS: QEMU's virtio-gpu.c rejects out-of-range rectangles, and has a refresh_rate field
   (verified: the QEMU sources); OpenBSD viogpu (verified: a fetched listing).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "yeah, virtio is fine"; "the simplest to program while still being
   efficient".
   BODY: Section 3.12.1.

ENTRY J.3.14
ORDER: 184   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: Windows
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author: "it should mention blit terminal was inspiration for the rio and NeWS
   windowing systems, and we'd like to bring over what we can of that simplicity also" (errata
   106). The Blit's mux, Plan 9's 8½ and rio, and NeWS are in 3.18. That the pdp64 brings over
   what it can of their simplicity is decided (3.18.1); which of their simplicities, and in what
   form, is open.
   WHY IT MATTERS: It decides what a program calls to put a window on the screen, and what the
   kernel provides.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A window is a plain buffer and a rectangle. For: drawing is writes to memory. Against: a
      buffer a window (memory); a rule for who may write whose.
   B) Drawing is writes to that buffer, with no drawing protocol in the base system. For: no
      round trips. Against: every program draws for itself; a library must exist.
   C) The server composes the buffers (a small program or the display hardware). For: one place
      for overlap, flip and tear. Against: a copy or a layer per window; trust.
   D) Downloadable server-side code, as the Blit and NeWS had, here into the core of J.3.11.
      For: new abilities without changing the host. Against: a second program to secure; the
      Blit's "two halves" problem.
   E) No toolkit in the base system. For: the size rio had. Against: every program has its own
      look.
   F) Windows as files, or as virtio queues. For: the uniformity of rio and 8½. Against: a
      naming and permission model (chapter 6) or a queue per window.
   PRECEDENTS: The Blit's mux; 8½ and rio (verified: kernel-pain-points.md 14I); NeWS as an
   extensible window server (verified as quoted from the UNIX-HATERS Handbook,
   kernel-pain-points.md 14G; that it was Sun's, in PostScript, by Gosling is unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The Blit, rio and NeWS; "that simplicity".
   BODY: Section 3.18.1.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.4.5
ORDER: 185   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: Firmware for a virtio device
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (device firmware)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The author decided (4.8.1; R256) that device initialisation happens behind the bus, a
   register write telling the device to initialise itself, and that a device may also be given
   values or a binary firmware image to load. What is open is how. Some devices contain a small
   processor and need a program, firmware, loaded into them before they work; a network or
   wireless card is the usual case. The PDP-11 had no such device. Virtio has no firmware
   operation of its own. [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.4.5.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Without a mechanism, such a device cannot be used, or each vendor invents
   one.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A firmware request type on the device's own queue. For: one queue, the mechanism the
      device already has. Against: virtio would need a new request type for each device class.
   B) A descriptor that gives the address and length of an image in memory, with a small header
      (identifier, version, length, checksum) that the device checks. For: the device pulls the
      image by DMA; the header lets it refuse a wrong image. Against: a header format to
      specify.
   C) A separate small virtio device for firmware loading that other devices consult. For: one
      mechanism for every device. Against: a second device, and a dependency between devices.
   D) A firmware window in the device's register space, written by the driver. For: needs no
      queue. Against: programmed I/O of a whole image.
   E) No loaded firmware at all: every device carries its program in its own flash. For: nothing
      to specify. Against: the device is dearer, and an update is a hardware operation.
   PRECEDENTS: Linux request_firmware, OpenBSD loadfirmware(9) (reads from /etc/firmware) and
   NetBSD firmload(9) load a blob from a file at attach time (verified: read in the trees). NVMe
   has a firmware-download command (unverified; not found in the QEMU headers read). The VAX
   console and some adapters loaded microcode from storage (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "the init protocol should mostly happen behind the bus, poke a register
   to tell the device 'initialize yourself' and possibly have another register populated with
   values or a binary firmware blob for the device to load" (R256); the INIT and READY bits of
   the Unibus device (errata 29); virtio's device_status handshake (errata 95).
   BODY: Section 4.8.1.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.4.7
ORDER: 186   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: The order of the boot media
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The ROM must choose a boot device when more than one is present: a disk or flash, an
   SD card, a serial line. Which comes first, whether a key at power-up changes it, how the
   chosen device is named in a register for the later stages, and whether the order is kept in
   non-volatile memory is not decided (F10 of decisions-pending.md leaves the order after the
   virtio-blk disk open).
   WHY IT MATTERS: A board with two disks or a removable card cannot boot predictably without
   it, and the ROM's device list and its use of non-volatile memory follow from it.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A fixed order wired into the ROM: disk or flash, then SD card, then the serial line. For:
      no state. Against: no way to boot from the card when a disk is present, short of removing
      the disk.
   B) A fixed order, changed by a key held at power-up. For: a rescue path without storage.
      Against: a key input every board must offer.
   C) An order kept in non-volatile memory. For: the user chooses. Against: the memory must
      exist, and a bad setting leaves a machine that does not boot, so a reset to defaults is
      needed.
   D) The order of the bus: the first device in enumeration order that has a valid block 0. For:
      no table at all. Against: the order depends on the wiring, not on the user's intent.
   E) One boot device per board, fixed by the board's description. For: the simplest. Against:
      no choice and no recovery medium.
   PRECEDENTS: The PC BIOS keeps a boot-device order in its setup memory (unverified). UEFI
   keeps boot variables in non-volatile memory, which its loaders read and write (verified:
   GetVariable and SetVariable calls in efiboot). The DEC boot ROM cards chose the device by a
   console command or switch (M9312 comment verified; the command unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: The PDP-11 ROM; no statement of his on the order was found.
   BODY: Section 4.4.

DECISION:
