ENTRY J.4.10
ORDER: 187   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: The monitor: commands, how it is reached, its console
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (monitor commands)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The controller's ROM includes a monitor (4.10.1, decided). Open: its command set, how
   it is reached (when nothing boots, a key at power-up, or both), its console and speed,
   whether it loads a program over the serial line, and what is kept in non-volatile memory
   (boot order, serial speed). Whether a hardware watchdog, which restarts the machine when the
   kernel hangs, belongs beside it is the assistant's proposal and is in appendix H.
   WHY IT MATTERS: The monitor is the way in when nothing boots, so its ROM size and its
   dependence on a serial port decide how a new board is brought up.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Examine, deposit, load over serial, start at an address, start from a device, reset a
      core, reset the machine. For: the PDP-11's ODT plus a loader. Against: a few hundred bytes
      of ROM (an estimate) and a working serial port.
   B) Examine, deposit and start only, the ODT set. For: the smallest. Against: no program can
      be loaded when the disk is dead.
   C) A boot menu (choose a device) only. For: simple. Against: no inspection of a dead machine.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A.
   PRECEDENTS: ODT on the PDP-11 (2.11BSD cons(4) read; the commands unverified); U-Boot and
   OpenSBI consoles (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: Errata 47: "the boot ROM and its monitor".
   BODY: Section 4.10.1.

ENTRY J.5.1
ORDER: 188   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 5 Drivers and devices
   TITLE: The console, the timer and the entropy source
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (console, timer, entropy devices)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: What is decided is in 5.7.1 and 5.7.2: the main and secure cores take no periodic
   tick, and the entropy pool is one shared pool that assists the operating system's generator.
   What is open is the form of the console, of the timer that serves a tickless machine, and of
   the way the pool is reached and fed. Every standard device of the pdp64 is a virtio device,
   found by PCI Express enumeration, and three things the machine cannot do without are still
   unassigned: [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.5.1.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The console must work before enumeration: the boot ROM, a kernel panic and a
   machine whose bus is broken all need it. The timer must work before any driver has run. A
   virtio console needs a queue that the boot code has set up; a fixed UART (universal
   asynchronous receiver-transmitter: [...]
   FURTHER: (figure omitted)
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Fixed console and timer, virtio-rng for entropy. A UART-style console and a
      counter-and-compare timer sit at fixed addresses, found by a small device tree or by the
      boot-information block; entropy comes from virtio-rng in front of a physical source. For:
      both fixed devices work without enumeration or a queue, so the boot ROM and the panic path
      stay tiny; only entropy needs a virtio driver. Against: the manual then has two device
      models, and "virtio across the board" (the author's words) may not have meant that; one
      timer shared by all cores forces a lock or per-core windows; wired interrupt lines from
      the fixed devices need the converter of J.6.3.
   B) Timer in the core, fixed UART, virtio-rng. A shared constant-rate counter and a per-core
      one-shot compare register, written with MTSR and read with RDTIME; a minimal fixed UART
      for boot and panic; entropy by virtio-rng. For: the timer cannot fire on the wrong core,
      tickless operation is one write, the secure core simply never writes its compare, and the
      kernel interface is what OpenBSD's clock-interrupt code needs (read the counter, set a
      one-shot compare, take the interrupt). Against: it adds system registers and probably
      RDTIME to the instruction set (with the side-channel switch that goes with it); the
      counter must reach many cores with a stated bound on skew; the architecture, not only the
      platform, now defines the timer; RDTIME needs an ordering rule for out-of-order cores; the
      UART is still a non-virtio device.
   C) Everything enumerated. The console is virtio-console, found by enumeration (the boot ROM
      carries a small polled virtio driver, or a console private to the ROM); entropy is
      virtio-rng; the timer is in the core as in option B. For: the strictest reading of "virtio
      across the board"; one transport driver and one enumeration walk serve console, entropy,
      disk, network, graphics and audio; OpenBSD already has drivers for both devices. Against:
      the console cannot work before the bus does, so when the queue, the IOMMU mapping or the
      root complex is wrong nothing prints, and the earliest boot is hardest to debug where
      debugging is most needed; a panic path that first needs a descriptor in uncached memory
      and a doorbell is longer than a store to a data register; a queue engine in hardware just
      for text.
   D) The absorb-and-squeeze pool as a fixed device. A write mixes into a pool of 256 or 512
      bytes and a read returns fresh randomness; one device shared by all cores, no version
      register, no firmware; console and timer as in option A or 2. For: it is the author's own
      proposal, accepted in errata 85 ("a read with a known and wanted effect is not a strange
      side effect"); it feeds the operating system's generator without replacing it; the
      hardware is a small pool and a mixer. Against: it would break if OpenBSD changed its
      randomness algorithm (the author's own remark); a read with an effect on shared state is a
      known hazard (the shared-buffer leaks of 2020 in x86 processors); the device is neither
      virtio nor standard and needs a PCI Express function or a fixed window; the author's doubt
      argues against putting more in silicon than a physical source.
   E) No entropy hardware. OpenBSD's generator is used as it is: it gathers timing from
      interrupts, disk, network and clock, and starts from a seed file written at the last
      shutdown, with a flag that says whether the seed was good; no virtio-rng; console and
      timer as in option A or 2. For: it answers the author's doubt with no hardware to trust,
      costs no gates and is the smallest design; OpenBSD's design assumes weak seeding and copes
      with it. Against: the first boot with no good seed is weak, and so is a clone of a virtual
      disk (the seed repeats); main cores are tickless by the author's rule, so fewer timer
      interrupts feed the generator; address-space randomisation spends entropy, and the address
      space is small.
   F) Virtio-console, with its emergency write for boot and panic. The console is
      virtio-console, found by enumeration; the boot ROM and the panic path use the feature
      EMERG_WRITE (feature bit 2), which lets a driver print one character by storing it in the
      emerg_wr field of the device configuration (offset 8), with no queue, no descriptor, no
      memory buffer and no IOMMU entry; the ordinary tty driver uses the queues. For: it keeps
      the console a virtio device and the panic path a store; the field is in the standard;
      OpenBSD's viocon and QEMU already carry the device. Against: the store is to a mapped
      window found by enumeration and not to a fixed address, so the boot ROM still needs the
      bus walk and the capability search before it can print; OpenBSD's viocon.c does not use
      EMERG_WRITE, so the panic path would be new kernel code; a device or emulator that does
      not offer the feature leaves no way to print.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Entropy: option E with virtio-rng as an optional device,
   no instruction and no register, and the pool of option D left for the author to revive only
   if virtio-rng proves too slow. Timer: option B, a counter and compare in the core. Console: a
   minimal fixed UART kept for the boot ROM and the panic path, with virtio-console optional for
   ordinary use. The reason is that the earliest boot and the panic path are the author's first
   priority (errors must be easy to detect), and a data register that the boot ROM can write in
   one store works before any queue exists; the timer in the core removes a device and a lock
   and matches the tickless rule; the entropy doubt is respected by keeping any physical source
   behind virtio-rng and mixing it, never using it alone. A decision that no non-virtio device
   is allowed would favour option C, at the price of a harder first boot; a need for a hardware
   key-generation source in the secure core would add a seed register of raw bits.
   PRECEDENTS: QEMU's virt machines for ARM and RISC-V place a UART at a fixed address, and on
   RISC-V a core-local timer, both described to the kernel by a device tree (a data structure
   the firmware passes to say what the machine contains), with virtio devices beside them
   (unverified). The RISC-V stimecmp register (the supervisor timer compare register, with the
   Sstc extension) is written directly where present, and by a firmware call where it is not
   (verified: OpenBSD riscv64 clock.c, lines 63 to 70); OpenBSD's whole riscv64 clock backend is
   175 lines (verified). The ARM generic timer has a shared system counter and a per-core
   compare (unverified). x86's TSC-deadline mode (a per-core one-shot compare against the
   time-stamp counter) depends on an invariant counter, and the Linux maintainers' own remark on
   it is that "the stupid hardware is not reliable" (verified: quoted in the repository). [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "forget the native 8 register devices. gone. virtio across the board."
   (chosen); for the boot console, the PDP-11's "load 512 byte block 0 then jmp 0" (considered).
   He named no console, timer or entropy system from elsewhere.
   BODY: Sections 5.7.1 and 5.7.2.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.5.2
ORDER: 189   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 5 Drivers and devices
   TITLE: The features that the pdp64 accepts
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (virtio features)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: Before any queue runs, a virtio driver reads the features the device offers and writes
   back the subset it accepts, and the device confirms it (5.4.4). The version-1 feature is
   required. Everything else is a choice that the architecture has not made: [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.5.2.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The features fix what a device may do to memory and in what order: with
   ACCESS_PLATFORM the descriptor holds a device address that the IOMMU of 2.11 translates, and
   without it the device is told to use physical addresses and the IOMMU has nothing to do.
   [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The minimum. Version 1 and the features a device class cannot work without; nothing else.
      For: the smallest driver, the least to test, the fewest paths in hardware. Against: no
      flush on the disk means a write that is not known to be on the medium; no segment limit
      means a driver that must discover failure by an error.
   B) The set that OpenBSD's drivers already ask for. vioblk asks for RO, NOTIFY_ON_EMPTY,
      SIZE_MAX, SEG_MAX and FLUSH (verified: vioblk.c), and the other class drivers ask for
      their own. For: the first operating system needs no change; the devices of QEMU already
      offer them. Against: the set is another system's habit and not a pdp64 decision; it
      changes with that system.
   C) The minimum plus the platform features. Declare ACCESS_PLATFORM always, so that every
      device address is translated, and the ordering feature, so that the order is the
      platform's. For: matches the IOMMU on by default (errata 85) and the one fence rule; a
      device that cannot work this way is refused at the handshake. Against: a device or
      emulator that does not offer the features cannot be used; the IOMMU must exist before the
      first driver runs.
   D) Option B plus the throughput features. Indirect descriptors (one descriptor pointing at a
      table of descriptors), the event index (the device and driver tell each other when to
      interrupt or notify) and the packed ring (the newer queue layout of version 1.1,
      unverified). For: fewer doorbells and interrupts, longer chains. Against: more states for
      the driver and for the hardware queue engine to model; the packed ring is a different
      queue format, so all descriptors, rings and the ordering rules of chapter 2 would have two
      forms.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option C for the platform features, and the set of
   option B for each class (they are what the first operating system needs anyway), with
   indirect descriptors left out until a measurement asks for them.
   PRECEDENTS: Linux and OpenBSD accept a subset and ignore the rest; OpenBSD's
   virtio_negotiate_features masks what the driver does not know (verified in virtio.c: the
   source read; the exact masking was not checked). ACCESS_PLATFORM is how a guest with an IOMMU
   or a secure enclave tells the device to use the platform's address translation (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "forget the native 8 register devices. gone. virtio across the board"
   (errata 95); "we will support all the virtio devices in silicon (the ones it makes sense to
   support, that is)" (R359).
   BODY: Sections 5.4.4 and 5.10.3.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.5.3
ORDER: 190   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 5 Drivers and devices
   TITLE: Firmware for a virtio device
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (device firmware)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A device that contains a small processor may need a program, firmware, loaded into it
   before it works. A PDP-11 peripheral needed none. On a PC the driver reads a file at attach
   time (OpenBSD's loadfirmware reads /etc/firmware/<name>; 19 files under sys/dev/pci call it,
   counted). Virtio has no firmware operation: a search of all 29 virtio_*.h files and of QEMU's
   hw/virtio finds none, and each device class defines its own set of requests, so a firmware
   download would be a request that no class has. The pdp64 has set one precedent: the entropy
   pool has "no updatable firmware" (errata 85).
   WHY IT MATTERS: A network card or a GPU built as a virtio function may need its program
   before the first request, and the standard gives no way to send it. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) A firmware request in each class. Each device class defines a request type that carries an
      image through a queue. For: uses the transport that exists; the image is checked by the
      device. Against: not in the standard, so every device and every class driver differs; a
      QEMU model needs it too.
   B) A firmware-loader device. A separate device type, enumerated beside the function it
      serves, that takes the image. For: one mechanism for all classes; a device that needs
      nothing is not affected. Against: a new device type outside the standard; two functions to
      pair.
   C) A window in the device's own space. The driver writes the image into a window of one of
      the function's base-address registers, as PCI devices do today. For: independent of
      virtio; any PCI Express function can use it. Against: every device defines its own window
      and command sequence; the "same driver for every device" aim of virtio is lost for those
      devices.
   D) No downloadable firmware. The pdp64's standard devices carry their own program in ROM or
      flash; a device that needs a download is not a standard device and has its own driver.
      For: no mechanism to define; matches the entropy pool's rule. Against: a device that is
      cheap because it has no flash cannot be a standard device; an update means new hardware.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option D for the standard devices, with option C left to
   the drivers of devices that are not standard.
   PRECEDENTS: PC practice: a file per device, a loader in the kernel (loadfirmware(9) in
   OpenBSD, firmload(9) in NetBSD) and a separate package of files (verified for OpenBSD:
   dev/firmload.c); Linux's request_firmware is the same idea (unverified). The PDP-11 had none
   (verified: 4.1).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: None named for firmware; the entropy pool "no updatable firmware" (errata
   85).
   BODY: Section 5.12.

DECISION: 
NOTE: 

================================================================================================
ENTRY J.5.4
ORDER: 191   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 5 Drivers and devices
   TITLE: Failure, reset and departure: what happens to the queued work
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (device failure handling)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: A virtio device can fail in three ways that matter to its driver: it reports a failed
   request through the status byte, it sets its needs-reset bit or stops answering, or it leaves
   the bus (hot-plug is decided; see 2.4). In the last two the chains in the queue were handed
   to a device that will not finish them. What is decided is that a departed device's work is
   left in memory, "so it can be read, saved, resent" (R262; 2.4.5 and 5.5.5). What is not
   worked out is the rule: when the driver resubmits the chains that are not in the used ring,
   when it fails them, and whether another core takes them over. [...]  (Full text: Appendix J,
   J.5.4.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: Every class driver has a recovery routine, and the disk driver's behaviour
   decides whether a file system sees an error or a delay. The kernel's rule decides whether a
   driver has a detach routine at all (the OpenBSD vioblk has none) and who reads a departed
   device's rings.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Fail every queued request. After a reset or a departure the driver completes each
      outstanding request with an error and starts afresh. For: simple; no request is run twice;
      it is what OpenBSD's vioblk_reset does. Against: a transient hiccup becomes an I/O error
      for the file system; the author's wish that the work be resent is not met.
   B) Resubmit the chains that are not in the used ring. The driver compares the available ring
      with the used ring, reprograms the queue and posts the unfinished chains again. For: the
      author's stated purpose; no error for a stall. Against: a request that already ran, or ran
      partly, runs twice; the driver of OpenBSD zeroes the ring memory on reinitialisation, so
      the chains would have to be copied out first.
   C) Resubmit only repeatable requests. Reads, writes of the same data, sends and buffer posts
      are resent; any other request is failed. The class driver knows which is which. For: safe
      by construction; each class decides. Against: a class driver can tell "never started" from
      "started, outcome unknown" only by the request's own status, so some requests must fail
      even when they never ran.
   D) Hand the queue to another core. The core that handles the departure event may not be the
      one that owns the queue; the unfinished work is handed over with the chains and their
      buffers. For: fits a machine of many cores and a secure core; the author's reference to a
      hand-over. Against: more kernel machinery; a rule is needed for what the old core may
      still touch.
   E) Leave it to each class driver. The kernel gives a reset and a departure event; each driver
      chooses one of the above. For: a disk and a network card have different needs (a lost
      frame is harmless, a lost write is not). Against: no common routine, as many recoveries as
      classes.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option C as the kernel's common routine, with a class
   driver free to be stricter (option E above it): the file system sees no error for a
   repeatable request, and no request runs twice.
   PRECEDENTS: OpenBSD's vioblk_reset stops the device, completes what finished and fails every
   other request; its re-initialisation zeroes the ring memory and restarts both indices at 0,
   and vioblk has { sizeof(softc), vioblk_match, vioblk_attach, NULL } as its cfattach, so it
   has no detach routine (verified: vioblk.c, virtio.c). The VAX's ubareset resets the adapter
   and calls every driver's reset routine, so that each re-issues what was pending: resending,
   driver by driver (verified: 4.3BSD vaxuba/uba.c). NVMe and virtio drivers in other systems
   requeue outstanding requests after a reset (unverified). Linux's driver model has probe and
   remove (unverified). Whether the virtio specification requires the ring indices to restart
   after a reset (unverified: the specification is not on this computer).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "so it can be read, saved, resent" (R262); the precise bus fault for a
   departed device (2.4.9, 2.9.1).
   BODY: Sections 5.5.4, 5.5.5 and 5.11.2.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.5.7
ORDER: 192   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 5 Drivers and devices
   TITLE: USB: the controller interface and the class drivers
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (USB controller interface)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: USB is a standard device of the pdp64 (statement 5.8.3: the author, 2026-08-29 and
   2026-11-03, "modern usb keyboard, mouse, and nvme hard drive", "pcie and nvme and usb"): a
   host controller that is a PCI Express function, with the operating system's USB stack
   enumerating what is plugged in. Two details are not worked out. The first is the controller
   interface, the register and memory layout that the driver programs. The one that PCI Express
   USB controllers have is xHCI (extensible host controller interface): [...]  (Full text:
   Appendix J, J.5.7.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The controller interface decides the one USB driver that the kernel carries
   and what the hardware and the emulator model must provide; the class drivers decide whether
   the machine has a keyboard and a mouse at first boot.
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) xHCI only. One controller interface, a small compliance-tested subset of it. For: what
      real USB 3 controllers on PCI Express present; one driver. Against: a large and complex
      interface (many registers, rings and contexts); no older USB 1 or 2 controller works.
   B) xHCI plus a simpler controller interface for the first board. For: a smaller first driver.
      Against: two drivers; a second model for the emulator.
   C) A pdp64 controller that is a virtio device. For: "virtio across the board". Against: the
      virtio device types read here list no USB host controller (unverified: not searched for);
      a bridge to real USB hardware is still needed.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A: xHCI only, as the repository's earlier lean
   (decisions-pending F9) said.
   PRECEDENTS: NVMe and xHCI both command through queues in memory (unverified: general
   knowledge). OpenBSD drives PCI Express USB controllers through its xhci driver (unverified
   here).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: USB enumerated over PCI Express as the model of a bus attached to a bus
   ("just as usb is unuerated over a pcie bus", R292); "modern usb keyboard, mouse, and nvme
   hard drive. usb hard drive too" (R20). He named no controller interface.
   BODY: Section 5.8.3.

ENTRY J.7.2
ORDER: 193   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 7 Multiple processors
   TITLE: Coherence beyond four cores
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (coherence protocol, not an instruction)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The first device has four cores that snoop (every cache watches the others' writes)
   with three states, modified, shared and invalid (7.6.1), and the architecture supports at
   least four cores, with sixteen or more as the preferred ceiling (7.6.3, errata 92). The
   protocol that serves more than four coherent cores has not been chosen. The architecture
   promises that memory is coherent and sequentially consistent (7.5.1) and says nothing of the
   protocol. The statement as first written: the protocol for more than four coherent cores is
   open; [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.7.2.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The number of general cores sets the size of the cache controller, the FPGA
   that can hold the design (about 16,000 look-up tables for a core before coherence, by a paper
   estimate at plus or minus 40 percent; [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) Keep three-state snooping for two to four cores and say so. For: smallest; fits the first
      device. Against: breaks the target of sixteen or more cores; snooping traffic grows with
      the cores.
   B) MESI within clusters of up to four cores, a snoop filter at a shared second-level cache,
      and a directory between clusters. MESI adds an exclusive state, so a write to a clean line
      that nobody else holds needs no bus transaction. For: four cores are one cluster and
      nothing is wasted; the ceiling is reached by adding the inter-cluster layer. Against: two
      protocols; the directory is where protocol deadlocks hide.
   C) A flat directory. One home place per line and a bit-vector of sharers. For: simplest to
      reason about; scales to 64. Against: two hops for the common case, which is more than a
      four-core machine needs.
   D) No coherence between clusters; explicit cache operations. For: cheapest to scale. Against:
      every missing flush is a race that reproduces once a week; it weakens sequential
      consistency to "within a cluster".
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option B as the direction, built in two steps: one
   cluster of four cores first, with the shared cache designed so a directory can be added; the
   architecture text says "coherent, sequentially consistent, line size 64 bytes" and nothing of
   the protocol, so more cores are an implementation and not a change.
   PRECEDENTS: PDP-11/70 caches were kept coherent by write-through and invalidation on DMA
   writes (unverified: from memory). Bus-snooping multiprocessors (Sequent, Sun Enterprise)
   stopped near 16 to 64 processors (unverified); directory machines (Stanford DASH, SGI Origin)
   scale by messaging the sharers (unverified); x86 uses snoop filters inside a die and
   directories across sockets; ARM's coherent interconnects use filters or directories
   (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "4 cores minimum, 16 or more preferred"; "for initial device 4 cores is
   enough hopefully that fits on the fpga".
   BODY: Section 7.6.4.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.7.5
ORDER: 194   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 7 Multiple processors
   TITLE: The reservation granule and how software learns its size
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (the reservation granule: a feature register)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: LL reserves a granule: a naturally aligned piece of memory of a size the
   implementation chooses, not smaller than 8 bytes (errata 45; 7.4.1). The text leaves the size
   to the implementation and says that a feature register is to report it (errata 45 lists
   "granule discovery in the feature registers" as still to write). Nothing says which register,
   nor what software may assume before it reads it. [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.7.5.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The kernel lays out its lock words and per-CPU structures with the granule in
   mind (padding a hot lock to a granule costs memory and saves failed SCs). The compiler's
   LL/SC loops and the C library's allocator need a safe assumption before any feature register
   is read. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The granule is the cache line, fixed by the architecture. One number for every
      implementation (the line size is itself open, J.7.2). For: software needs no discovery;
      one rule for locks and for padding. Against: an implementation with another line size
      cannot conform; it fixes the line size as a side effect.
   B) An implementation choice, reported in a feature register, with a stated minimum of 8
      bytes. This is the text as it stands. For: the implementation is free. Against: portable
      code must read the register or assume 8 bytes, the worst case for padding.
   C) An implementation choice between 8 bytes and a stated maximum, for example 128 bytes,
      reported in a register. For: software can pad to the maximum with no register read; the
      implementation keeps a range. Against: a maximum is another number to commit to.
   D) A reservation on the exact word, held in a monitor at the memory controller. For: no false
      sharing, whatever the lines are. Against: a monitor with an address comparator for every
      core, and a rule for cached lines that move between cores while a reservation is held.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option C, with the maximum equal to the line size once
   J.7.2 fixes it.
   PRECEDENTS: RISC-V lets the implementation choose the size of the reservation set and
   requires no minimum beyond the word (unverified: from memory of the specification). Arm
   exposes the exclusives reservation granule size in a system register and gives a range of 8
   bytes to 2 KB (unverified). x86's lock prefix works on a cache line and has no granule to
   report (unverified).
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: None named; the spec is the author's (errata 45: "a naturally aligned
   granule of the implementation's choice (not smaller than 8 bytes)").
   BODY: Section 7.4.6.

DECISION:

ENTRY J.8.1
ORDER: 195   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 8 Vector work and the accelerator
   TITLE: The operations and the number formats of the engine
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (a device's operations, not processor opcodes)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: The engine is a standard part of the architecture (8.4.1), so the architecture has to
   say what it does: its operations, its number formats, and how software learns what a given
   engine implements (8.4.2). The processor has binary64, binary32 and binary16 as scalar types
   (8.6.1) and nothing else; the engine is where bfloat16, 8-bit floating point, integers of 8
   and 4 bits and one-bit weights would live, if they live anywhere. Three families of work make
   different demands. [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.8.1.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: The operation set decides what an array-language interpreter can hand to the
   engine and what stays on the processor, and so whether the engine repays its setup. The
   formats decide whether a model published in one of them runs. [...]
   FURTHER: The number formats. The sizes are for one matrix of 4096 by 4096 elements
   (16,777,216 elements), computed.
   FURTHER: | Format | Bits | What it is | Largest finite value | Bytes for the matrix |
   |---|---|---|---|---| | binary64 | 64 | IEEE double | about 1.8e308 | 134,217,728 | |
   binary32 | 32 | IEEE single | about 3.4e38 | 67,108,864 | | binary16 | 16 | IEEE half: 5
   exponent bits, 10 fraction bits | 65,504 | 33,554,432 | | bfloat16 | 16 | 8 exponent bits, 7
   fraction bits: [...]
   FURTHER: The largest values of the two 8-bit formats follow from their definitions: E5M2
   follows the IEEE rules and tops out at 1.75 x 2 to the 15 = 57,344; E4M3 as the
   machine-learning formats define it tops out at 1.75 x 2 to the 8 = 448 (the definitions are
   unverified here). Two more families have no entry above. [...]
   FURTHER: Accumulate widths. A dot product adds many products, so the sum needs more bits than
   the factors. Two 8-bit signed integers multiply to at most 128 x 128 = 16,384 (2 to the 14);
   a sum of n such products fits a signed 32-bit integer for any n up to 131,071 (131,072 x
   16,384 is exactly 2 to the 31), so an 8-bit dot product accumulates in 32 bits without
   overflow over any realistic layer. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options:
   A) The array-language set: element-wise, reduce, scan, index. About 50 operations in the
      assistant's count of the primitives of APL and K: 16 binary element-wise (add, subtract,
      multiply, divide, remainder, minimum, maximum, and, or, exclusive-or, the shifts, power),
      12 unary (negate, absolute value, not, square root, reciprocal, floor, convert), 6
      comparisons, 7 reductions and 7 scans (sum, product, minimum, maximum, and, or,
      exclusive-or), gather, scatter, compress, index generation and select; over integers of 8
      to 64 bits, binary16, binary32, binary64 and bit arrays. Effect: the processor is
      unchanged; the engine is streaming units behind the ring engine; the driver builds one
      kind of request. For: it is what the author named (APL and K, R277), it is small, and each
      operation maps to one descriptor (8.5.3). Against: no matrix operation, so inference gets
      only its element-wise parts; by the figures above a single pass is memory-bound, so the
      benefit is fusing a short expression into one descriptor, which this option does not
      specify.
   B) BLAS levels 1 to 3 over the floating-point formats. About a dozen routines of levels 1 and
      2 (axpy, dot, scal, gemv, ger, ...) and gemm (and its relatives syrk and trsm) at
      binary16, binary32 and binary64, accumulating in the wider format. Effect: processor
      unchanged; the engine needs floating-point multiply-accumulate units, binary64 ones being
      the largest; the driver passes matrix shapes, strides and scalars (alpha, beta). For: the
      software of numerical computing is written against the BLAS (LAPACK is built on it,
      unverified), so it ports; its numerical behaviour is well understood. Against: no integer,
      bit or index operations (nothing for APL's where), no convolution, softmax or
      normalisation; levels 1 and 2 are memory-bound like option A.
   C) The machine-learning operator set, whole-tensor, at the level of TOSA. The MLIR dialect
      that implements TOSA (the Tensor Operator Set Architecture) defines 71 operations (read,
      in the LLVM sources): 10 tensor operators (matrix multiply, three convolutions, depthwise
      convolution, two pooling operators, two Fourier transforms, argmax), 4 activations, 17
      element-wise binary operators, 13 unary, 4 selection and comparison, 6 reductions, 7
      layout operators (concatenate, pad, reshape, reverse, slice, tile, transpose), gather and
      scatter, resize, cast and rescale, and five helpers (constant, identity, custom, if,
      while). The dialect describes the set as meant "to enable a variety of implementations
      running on a diverse range of processors, with the results at the TOSA level consistent
      across those implementations", and defines profiles (supported subsets) and integer types
      down to 4 bits. ONNX's operator set is larger, about two hundred operators (unverified).
      Effect: processor unchanged; the engine implements tensor operators with shape, stride,
      dilation and padding attributes; the descriptor carries those. For: a ready-made, reviewed
      operator set that frameworks already lower to, with a conformance idea (profiles).
      Against: 71 operators each with shape rules is a large specification to verify, and a
      small engine cannot do convolution and while; the control operators (if, while) are beyond
      a streaming unit.
   D) One matrix multiply-accumulate on tiles, plus a few element-wise helpers. This is the
      tensor-core family. NVIDIA's matrix operations take fragments in binary16, bfloat16, TF32,
      4-bit integers and single bits (with xor.popc and and.popc, the binary dot product that
      XNORPOP is), and the 8-bit floating-point and MX element types (read in LLVM's NVVM
      intrinsics). Intel AMX has 8 tile registers of 1 KiB (read: 8,192 bits each in LLVM, so 8
      KiB in all) and instructions for 8-bit integer (four sign combinations), bfloat16 and
      binary16 products (29 definitions in X86InstrAMX.td, pseudo-instructions included). ARM's
      SME has outer-product accumulates, including a bitwise one (BMOPA) and BFMOPA for bfloat16
      (read). SiFive's Xsfvqmaccdod and Xsfmm are 8-bit matrix products for RISC-V (read).
      Google's TPU is a 256 by 256 array of 8-bit multiply-accumulate units with a command set
      of about a dozen instructions, and Apple has a matrix coprocessor (AMX) and a neural
      engine (ANE) (unverified). Effect: processor unchanged; the engine is a
      multiply-accumulate array; the tile shape is part of the operation. For: it covers nearly
      all the arithmetic of inference and training. Against: nothing for the array languages;
      element-wise work needs another unit; the tile geometry must be fixed or reported, and a
      matrix unit starved by memory does not help batch-one inference (J.8.4 computes this).
   E) A narrow binary or ternary kernel: sign-flip accumulate and population-count dot product.
      The five steps of a LittleBit layer (the LB64 document: scale the activations, accumulate
      or subtract them by weight bit, scale, accumulate again, scale) on one-bit weights and
      16-bit activations. Effect: processor unchanged; the engine is adders and sign flips with
      no multipliers; the driver builds a chain of descriptors per layer. For: the author's
      referent (LittleBit-2); 160 times less weight traffic than binary16 for a 4096 by 4096
      layer (33,554,432 bytes against about 209,715, computed). Against: a standard part that
      serves one family of models is stranded if the field moves, as it has moved before
      (ternary models, unverified); it does nothing for the array languages.
   F) A small mandatory core and named optional sets, found by asking. The core is option A plus
      gemm, at the integer formats, binary16, binary32 and binary64; optional sets are named in
      a capability block that the program reads: further formats (bfloat16, FP8, 4-bit integers,
      binary, MX), further operators (convolution, softmax, normalisation). Effect: processor
      unchanged; every engine implements the core, which a small system may implement in
      firmware (J.8.4, option E); the driver reads the capability block with the virtio feature
      words (5.4.4) and the device-specific configuration. For: standard (the core is always
      present, 8.4.2) and still extensible; the sets can follow the field. Against: a library
      must handle engines of different capability; conformance tests grow with the sets.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option F, with the core of options A and 2 (the
   element-wise, reduce, scan and index set, and gemm, with 8-bit integers accumulating in 32
   bits) and the field's moving formats as named sets. It makes the engine standard without
   freezing a format list that has changed every few years, and it lets the smallest system
   implement the core in software.
   PRECEDENTS: Verified in sources on this machine. LLVM's NVVM intrinsics name the formats f16,
   bf16, tf32, s4, u4, b1, e4m3, e5m2, e2m1, e2m3, e3m2 and ue8m0. LLVM's x86 target defines AMX
   tiles (8 of 8,192 bits) and the VPDPBUSD dot products; its AArch64 target defines SDOT and
   UDOT (92 definitions with their variants), BFMLALB, BMOPA and BFMOPA. The QEMU decoder for
   the RISC-V vector extension has the bfloat16 forms (vfwmaccbf16, vfncvtbf16, vfwcvtbf16) and
   ordered and unordered floating-point sum reductions (vfredosum, vfredusum: the order of a
   floating-point sum changes the result, so the architecture names both). LLVM's RISC-V target
   has Zvqdotq (quad widening dot product), Xsfvqmaccdod and Xsfmm. LLVM's Hexagon target
   defines the HVX vector unit: 32 registers of 64 or 128 bytes and 992 definitions named V6_.
   MLIR defines TOSA (71 operations, profiles). [...]
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "mmap device arm style route" (R277); APL and K "easier to support and
   more efficient" (R277); "just optimize inference whether in cpu or gpu whatever makes most
   sense" (R139); "the simd/avax stuff" (R136); the LittleBit-2 encoding (R136); and, 2026-11-10
   (errata 131): "I want all the best options with rationale and precedents for each of those
   items. even if it doesn't involve specific opcodes 'in the cpu' still it must be built in as
   a standard part of the architecture, just as the fpu mmu and iommu are."
   BODY: Statements 8.4.1, 8.4.2, 8.5.5 and 8.6.2.

ENTRY J.8.2
ORDER: 196   KIND: open item   CHAPTER: 8 Vector work and the accelerator
   TITLE: The descriptor of the engine and the virtio descriptor
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (device descriptor)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: Chapter 5 puts every standard device on virtio queues (5.4.2): a descriptor is 16
   bytes (a 64-bit address, a 32-bit length, flags, and a next index), and descriptors chain
   with NEXT. The engine needs a request that names an operation, several arrays with lengths
   and strides, formats and a place for the status. Two sizes follow by computation (an
   illustration, not a layout). An element-wise request fits 64 bytes: operation (2), format
   (2), flags (4), length (8), three addresses (24), completion address (8), three strides (12),
   cookie (4). A matrix request fits 128: [...]  (Full text: Appendix J, J.8.2.)
   WHY IT MATTERS: If the engine does not fit the virtio queue it needs a ring engine, a driver
   skeleton and an IOMMU story of its own, which is the cost that errata 95 removed. A chain,
   one operation following another with no processor between, is how a layer of a network or an
   array expression is expressed. [...]
OPTIONS:
  Options For every option the processor is unchanged (FENCE and a doorbell, 8.4.6), apart from
     the instruction named in option C:
   A) The virtio form: the command header is the first buffer, the operands are the next buffers
      of the chain, the status is the last. This is the shape of virtio-blk and virtio-gpu. An
      element-wise request is five 16-byte descriptors (header, two inputs, one output, status)
      plus a header block of, say, 32 bytes: about 112 bytes of fetches against 64 for a fixed
      descriptor (computed); a virtio indirect descriptor (VRING_DESC_F_INDIRECT, read) puts the
      five in one ring slot. Device and driver: the engine is a virtio device with one queue
      engine shared with the disk; the driver is the generic transport skeleton plus a request
      builder. For: nothing new in the transport; the IOMMU validates every operand because each
      is in a descriptor; the IN_ORDER feature (35, read) lets a sequence of requests complete
      in order. Against: five fetches for one operation; "the next operation uses this one's
      result" is two requests and a buffer in RAM between them.
   B) One descriptor points at a device-defined work block that holds the operands and can
      chain. This was the previous leaning of this entry. The ring carries one 16-byte
      descriptor to a block of 64 or 128 bytes with a next field, as the DSA batch does. Device
      and driver: one ring, a block format in the engine's specification; the driver fills
      blocks. For: one fetch for the block, any size, a chain in the block. Against: the operand
      addresses are inside the block, outside the descriptors the transport and its tools
      understand; the IOMMU rule has to be written for the block's contents; the status of a
      chain is the block's business.
   C) A fixed 64-byte descriptor in a ring of the engine's own. This is the NVMe, io_uring and
      DSA form. Device and driver: a second ring structure beside virtio; its own driver
      skeleton and its own IOMMU hooks; optionally one processor instruction that stores 64
      bytes to the doorbell in one atomic write (x86 has ENQCMD and MOVDIR64B, which LLVM
      defines; their exact semantics unverified). For: one fetch per operation, a size that
      suits one cache line, the simplest engine. Against: it leaves virtio (errata 95 said every
      device is virtio); a 128-byte matrix request needs two entries; a second contract to keep
      correct.
   D) A 16-byte head followed by extension descriptors in the same chain. The head says which
      operation and how many descriptors follow; some of those carry values (operation, format,
      stride) in place of an address, marked by a flag. Device and driver: the engine is
      virtio-like; a new descriptor kind is a change to what virtio means by a descriptor. For:
      all in 16-byte units on the existing ring, with the IOMMU applying to the pointer
      descriptors. Against: the engine must walk the chain to find its parameters; a "value
      descriptor" is not virtio.
   E) Commands that name objects held by the device: handles. This is the virtio-gpu pattern:
      create a resource, attach its backing memory (once), then send small commands that name
      resources by number; the SMMU command queue is the same idea with 16-byte commands. Engine
      commands would be ALLOC, ATTACH, EXEC(op, handle, handle, handle) and FREE; results stay
      on the device between operations. Device and driver: the engine has memory it manages
      (allocation, lifetime, exhaustion); the driver tracks handles. For: weights are attached
      once and each layer is a small command; the IOMMU is consulted at ATTACH, not at every
      operation; no uncached readback between layers (8.4). Against: the device carries an
      allocator, which is operating-system work inside the device; on an engine that uses only
      host RAM the handles add a layer for nothing.
   F) Registers, as the RK11 had. A few device registers hold the operation and the addresses,
      one request at a time. Device and driver: the native register model that errata 95
      removed. For: the simplest hardware. Against: one operation at a time, an uncached store
      for each field, no queue and no chain. It is listed as the ancestor and not as a
      candidate.
   Leaning (assistant, not a decision): Option A, refined: the engine is a virtio device whose
   requests have a command header in the first buffer, as virtio-blk and virtio-gpu do, and that
   header may itself hold a short chain of operations (option B's list) so that fused
   expressions are one request; handles (option E) only if the engine has memory of its own. No
   change to virtio is needed.
   PRECEDENTS: Verified: the table above. Virtio's own use of the first buffer for a
   device-defined header (blk, net, gpu) and its indirect descriptors are in the Linux headers.
   The Linux headers define no virtio device type for compute: the identifiers in virtio_ids.h
   run from VIRTIO_ID_NET (1) to VIRTIO_ID_SPI (45), with a GPU (16) whose SUBMIT_3D command
   carries an opaque command stream in a buffer, and none for a neural-network or vector engine;
   the engine would need an identifier of its own (the allocation procedure is unverified).
   Unverified: the PDP-11 attached array processors took library calls and device registers.
   AUTHOR'S REFERENTS: "virtio across the board" (errata 95); "descriptors" (R277); the PDP-11
   RK11 and the DEUNA ring as the ancestors in chapter 5.
   BODY: Statements 8.4.2 and 8.4.7.

DECISION:

ENTRY H.1.1.5
ORDER: 197   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 1 Introduction
   TITLE: 1.1.5 A thread between the two lineages....
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 1.1.5 A thread between the two lineages. The pdp64 joins a DEC
   machine's instruction set to a Commodore machine's devices, and the two companies are linked
   by at least one person: Ira Velinsky, the industrial designer credited with modifying the
   Commodore 64's case, had earlier worked at Digital Equipment Corporation (he is credited
   there with the Rainbow 100 of 1981), and a Commodore history credits his DEC work with
   drawing the attention of Commodore's management. The author predicted such a link before
   finding it. The hypothesis he draws from it is that personnel passed between the two
   companies and that the similarities between the C64 and the PDP-11 are too many to be chance
   ; the source itself claims only the person, and no influence of DEC designs on Commodore's,
   and the similarities are not yet listed with sources (the record is in
   docs/c64-devices-from-chat.md, section 8).
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

DECISION:

   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)

   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 3.8.4 The blitter, the Blit's bit-block-transfer operation as a
   device, copies and combines rectangles of pixels with raster operations: registers for the
   source and destination base addresses, the strides, the width and height, a raster operation
   (the sixteen binary operations of source against destination and the common ternary ones), a

   done. The device chat chose a three-operand draw instead (decisions-pending.md E3). The
   register list is the assistant's sketch from the ISA chat; the author has not discussed it.

ENTRY H.3.9
ORDER: 201   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: 3.9 The terminal as the author describes it
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: The terminal and its glyph map are decided in principle (3.9.1 in
   the body). What follows is the assistant's elaboration of the cell and the slot. What the
   author's earlier terminal chat adds (2026-06-18, docs/c64-devices-from-chat.md section 6; a
   separate project, not a pdp64 decision): a cell holds a 32-bit foreground colour, a 32-bit
   background colour, a UTF-8 character and about eight bits of attributes (bold, italic,
   underline, blink); "the number of cells take up so little memory, the 64 bits devoted to
   color is trivial"; "each cell holds its actual data, not a reference to the data"; sprites
   are a separate plane that composites over the cells; existing tty programs must run through a
   shim and native programs use the terminal fully, "peek and poke is the internal interface".
   That design fits the cell array of the figure below and differs from the author's new words
   in one place: the chat-era cell holds the character code itself, while the glyph map of the
   new words implies a slot (3.14, pair 3, candidate (a) against (b)). (figure omitted) As an
   arithmetic example, with the chat-era cell (about 13 bytes: 4 + 4 + 4 for a character + 1 for
   attributes) and a 1920 by 1080 screen of 8 by 16 pixel cells, there are 240 columns and 67
   rows, 16,080 cells and about 209 KB of cells; with slots of 16 by 32 coverage bytes (512
   bytes) and 4,096 slots, the glyph map is 2 MB. Both are small against a 12.4 MB frame at 48
   bits (3.11.2), which is the author's observation of 2026-06-18: [... full text in Appendix H]
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.

      filled another way.

ENTRY H.3.10
ORDER: 202   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: 3.10 A peek/poke program for the terminal
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

ENTRY H.3.18
ORDER: 207   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: 3.18 A c64 mode: a small computer on the die that acts as the terminal
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: The c64 mode is the author's (3.16.1 and 3.16.2 in the body). This
   section is the assistant's explanation of how it could work, for the two readers, and states
   the questions it leaves open. What the Blit did. The Blit (3.3) was a terminal with a
   computer of its own, and the host sent it programs instead of escape codes to interpret. The
   records in docs/kernel-pain-points.md (section 14I, from the Blit papers) say that the
   terminal received downloaded code over its serial line, that mpxterm, the terminal's program,
   "is 1627 lines of C" plus "204 lines of assembler" and multiplexed several host programs over
   the one line, and that the authors held that "an intelligent terminal is not an idiot savant,
   it is one that can be educated". A terminal with its own small computer is therefore a
   display, keyboard and mouse driven by a processor on the terminal's side of the cable,
   running a program the host loaded; the host sends it messages and the program draws. How the
   download worked (the protocol, the program format) is not in the records and is unverified.
   The VT52 and VT100 were the opposite: a fixed circuit obeying a fixed set of codes (3.2). A
   small core with the C64's chips is a terminal on a chip. Take a small computer, give it the
   C64 family's circuits (the VIC, the video chip; the SID, the sound chip), screen memory,
   colour RAM, glyph memory (3.9) and a window towards the host, and let the host load its
   program. The program is the terminal's firmware: [... full text in Appendix H]

   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.

ENTRY H.3.s-the-compositor-question
ORDER: 208   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: The compositor question
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: The author asked, 2026-11-10: "not sure what that would look like.
   is there virtio-compositor?" What a compositor is, at more length. A compositor (defined in
   3.16.2) combines the pictures of several windows or sources into the one picture sent to the
   display, which is then scanned out as in 3.15. In a desktop window system each program draws
   into its own picture (its surface) and the compositor places the surfaces, one over another,
   into the frame the scanout reads. The PC's windowing systems did this in software (3.5); in
   the layered sketches of 3.21 the scanout hardware does it, as the C64's chip did with
   sprites. The PDP-11 has no antecedent; its terminal was a single stream. Why "takes over" is
   the simple case. If the c128 owns the output outright when enabled, there is nothing to
   compose: the machine has one scanout and one audio path, and the only question is who drives
   them. The multiplexer is a single enable bit (path (a) below). The compositor case differs in
   one way: the core is one client among others, so its picture and sound must be handed over in
   a form that the host's compositor, or the display controller's layers, can place. (figure
   omitted) What exists, checked. No virtio device is called a compositor: no entry of
   virtio_ids.h on this machine names one. The display-related types are GPU (16), input (18),
   sound (25), video encoder and decoder (30, 31), DMA buffer (37) and audio policy (39). [...
   full text in Appendix H]
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

ENTRY H.3.21
ORDER: 211   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: 3.21 A design still being worked out: what a mashup of GPU, video memory, C64 and Blit
   could look like
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: The author, 2026-11-10: "conclude the graphics chapter: we're still
   figuring it out, what would a mashup of gpu, vram, c64 and blit look like." Nothing in this
   section is decided. Every statement is tagged or (S); the sketches are shapes of ideas put
   side by side so that the questions at the end can be answered, and the repository recommends
   none. The chain behind them: the PDP-11 terminal was a character stream (3.1), the Blit and
   the C64 put memory-mapped bitmaps, text, sprites and sound behind a processor or a few chips
   (3.3, 3.4), the PC split them (3.5), and the GPU turned drawing into queued commands and
   shaders (3.6); the sketches mix those inheritances. Two terms first. VRAM (video memory) is
   memory that belongs to the display device: it sits on the device, behind a window on the bus,
   and the scanout reads it without crossing the bus; the processor reaches it through the
   window (slowly: every access is a bus transaction). The alternative is system memory that the
   device reads by DMA as a bus master, as the video device of 3.11.2 does; the framebuffer is
   then ordinary memory and the scanout competes with the processor for it. A GPU card has VRAM;
   the first board of the pdp64 has none to speak of (the block RAM is 5.5 megabits and a frame
   is 8.3 MB, 3.11.2), so on it every layer lives in system memory, and "VRAM" in the sketches
   below means "memory the device owns the use of", whichever kind it is. Compositing is
   combining several pictures into the one that is shown; [... full text in Appendix H]

   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.

ENTRY H.3.21.1
ORDER: 212   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 3 Graphics and the C64 devices
   TITLE: 3.21.1 Three layers (a text-cell layer, a pixel layer and a sprite layer) are...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 3.21.1 Three layers (a text-cell layer, a pixel layer and a sprite
   layer) are plain arrays in video memory that the processor pokes; the display hardware
   composes them as it reads them out, one pixel at a time, and there is no command queue. What
   the programmer sees. Three arrays, each at a fixed place, each with a small set of registers
   (base, size, stride, enable). The text array is the cell array of 3.9, with its glyph slots
   in another array; the pixel array is a framebuffer; the sprite layer is a table of entries
   (x, y, shape, colour, enable). A program pokes whichever it wants, in any order. What is in
   video memory: the cells, the glyph slots, the pixels, the sprite table and the sprite shapes.
   At scanout: for each pixel the hardware asks the layers from the top down (sprites, then
   text, then pixels), looks the cell up to its glyph, takes the glyph's coverage and the
   colours, and gives the first layer that is not transparent; the sprite layer needs to know
   only a few entries per row. The composing is done in a line buffer (a small memory that holds
   one row), as 3.11.2 already provides for. Costs: a compositor of the order of the 1,500
   lookup tables the repository estimates for the blitter, plus a line buffer per layer (an
   estimate of mine, unmeasured); scanout traffic is the pixel layer's 498 MB/s at 1920 by 1080,
   4 bytes, 60 Hz (746 MB/s at 6 bytes), plus about 13 MB/s for the cells and the glyph reads
   (arithmetic from the examples of 3.9). Gives up: [... full text in Appendix H]

   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.

   seen as one picture (3.7): a glyph is drawn from it by the blitter into the pixel layer when
   a program wants a glyph on the pixel layer at an arbitrary position, and the text layer's
   cells point at it directly. In video memory: sketch 1's contents and the atlas. At scanout:
   as sketch 1. The blitter runs beside it, in the blanking time or contending for the memory.
   Costs: the blitter and its arbitration with scanout; the repository's own estimate is about
   1,500 lookup tables and a line buffer, "nearly all address arithmetic" (3.8 commentary), and
   a bus-master port on video memory. Gives up: the same as sketch 1, except that drawing,
   scrolling and window moves in the pixel layer no longer cost the processor the pixels.
   Properties kept: T, C, G, S as sketch 1; B yes (bit-blit with the raster operations of 3.8.7,
   and windows as layers or as rectangles in the pixel layer); U no. Split: healed as sketch 1,
   and the pixel layer gains the Blit's operations, so that text on it (a window of a program
   that draws its own glyphs) is cheap. A game: [... full text in Appendix H]

   Blit conveniences done in software by a library (cells, glyphs, sprites and cursor, blits,
   flips), and no special hardware. What the programmer sees. A library that offers the
   peek/poke look: a cell array in memory, put_char, sprites as library objects, and a present
   call that does the transfer and the flush for the damaged rectangles. In video memory: the
   host's resources; on the pdp64 the program's memory is the backing (3.14.3). At scanout: the
   host reads its resource when it likes; the library cannot flip at a blank because virtio-gpu
   has none (3.12); tearing is the host's concern and, on an emulator, usually invisible. Costs:
   nothing in gates; the processor draws everything and a flush copies; bus traffic is the
   damage rectangles. The glyph slots are a library table, filled from the TrueType engine on
   the processor. Gives up: everything hardware: the hardware cursor is the cursor queue only,
   there are no sprites except the cursor, no blits, no layers; the C64's "the chip draws it
   while you do other things" is the library's loop. Properties kept: T only through the library
   (the program pokes memory the library owns, and the library presents it); C only to 32 bits
   (3.14.4); G yes, in software; S no (the cursor excepted); B in software; U no. Split: healed
   in the library's picture, but each screen is still one pixel framebuffer. A game: [... full
   text in Appendix H]

   hidden from it. With virtio: the open tension of 3.16, question 6.

ENTRY H.4.3.1b
ORDER: 216   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: 4.3.1b The ROM brings up the machine, loads the first block, and offers a...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 4.3.1b The ROM brings up the machine, loads the first block, and
   offers a console monitor when nothing boots. It has no resident services and nothing is
   called after boot. (The first sentence of 4.3.1 is in the body, decided by errata 47.)
   Commentary . Trade-offs. The pdp64 returns to the PDP-11 split and drops job 3: every device
   speaks virtio through one transport (chapter 2), so the loader and the kernel share one
   driver design and the loader needs no firmware to read a disk. The cost is a driver for the
   boot disk in the second stage, small because a virtio device is a handful of structures.
   Benefits. There is no firmware API to specify, no compatibility layer, no resident management
   code and no closed blob at the bottom of the trust chain (x86 firmware also keeps resident
   system-management code that the operating system cannot see, unverified; arm64 and RISC-V
   servers need trusted firmware and usually UEFI beside it, unverified, though OpenBSD's arm64
   and riscv64 ports do boot through efiboot, read); the failure cases reduce to one, the first
   block did not load, and the monitor is the recovery. Impact. Kernel: no firmware services to
   call or to fix. Compiler: builds the ROM, loader and tools. Hardware: a boot ROM of a few
   hundred bytes to a few kilobytes, with a driver per boot device (not specified more closely).
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

ENTRY H.4.4.4
ORDER: 219   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: 4.4.4 If a key is held at power-up, the monitor runs on the serial console....
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (a monitor key at power-up)

   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 4.4.4 If a key is held at power-up, the monitor runs on the serial
   console. (That the monitor runs when nothing boots is 4.10.1 in the body.) Commentary .
   Trade-offs. A boot ROM with a driver per boot device is more code in the controller than a
   "jump to a fixed flash address" scheme and much less than firmware; the first block must fit
   in one sector. Benefits. The first stage is the same on every board, and the first kernel
   code makes no guesses about the reset state, which earlier machines left to the console
   (inference: the VAX console and the PC's BIOS each defined their own). Impact. Compiler: the
   first block is 446 bytes of assembler. Hardware: the ROM reads one block and starts one core.

ENTRY H.4.5
ORDER: 220   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: 4.5 The disk: a GPT around the BSD label
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: The first stage lives in the protective MBR. Block 0 of a GPT disk
   is the protective MBR, 512 bytes: 446 bytes of code, the table (one entry, type 0xEE), then
   0xAA55 (the bytes 0x55, 0xAA: the 0x55AA of 4.4.1). The first stage has 446 bytes. It cannot
   understand a GPT or a file system in that space and does not try: it carries a block list,
   three numbers per piece of the second stage (first block, number of blocks, load address),
   written into its code area by the installboot tool at installation; it reads those blocks and
   jumps to the first. The BSDs already do this. OpenBSD 4.6's installboot(8) page for the sparc
   says the first-stage program "loads the second stage program from a set of filesystem block
   numbers that have been hard-coded into it by the installboot program"; on the PC installboot
   patches biosboot with the location of /boot, and biosboot(8) says to re-run it whenever
   boot(8) is changed, "as its inode may change" (read). Because the boot-code bytes are the
   only place the pdp64 keeps block-list data, a damaged GPT cannot stop the machine reaching
   the second stage, which can repair it. (a code example is omitted here; see the manual) The
   pseudocode hides a question the repository does not answer: how the first stage reads blocks.
   4.3.1 says the ROM offers no services after boot, and a virtio-blk read needs a queue, three
   descriptors, a doorbell and a poll (4.4). Whether that fits in 446 bytes beside the list is
   not established. [... full text in Appendix H]

ENTRY H.4.6
ORDER: 222   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: 4.6 The second stage and the hand-off
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: What the second stage is for (the fuller sketch; the general form
   is in the body). The first stage put a bigger program in memory. The second stage does what
   the PDP-11's boot did, with more to read: it finds the disk, reads the partition table, reads
   the label, finds the root file system, finds the kernel by name in it, loads the kernel and
   enters it. Because it is a C program loaded from disk it can be as large as it needs and be
   replaced by copying a file. It has its own drivers (the virtio-blk reader, now complete) and
   no firmware to call. As a sketch: (a code example is omitted here; see the manual) The
   pdp64's hand-off . A kernel that is to run on any pdp64 has to be told: how much memory there
   is and where (ranges, since memory is not always one block), how many cores, where the bus's
   root complex is (so that it can enumerate, walk the bus and list what is attached, 2.4),
   which device it was loaded from, where the console is, and the command line. The block is
   addressed by r1. The pdp64's hand-off is small because the bus describes its own devices: the
   block names memory, cores, the root complex, the boot device, the console and the screen if
   one was set up, and the kernel finds everything else by enumeration. (figure omitted)
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

ENTRY H.4.6.3
ORDER: 225   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: 4.6.3 The block's layout is defined once, in a grammar from which the headers...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 4.6.3 The block's layout is defined once, in a grammar from which
   the headers are generated, and carries a checksum and a version. Commentary . Trade-offs. A
   fixed-layout block with a checksum has two failure cases (bad checksum, unknown version),
   against the parser a device tree or ACPI needs. OpenBSD's arm64 and riscv64 ports already
   parse a device tree and attach drivers from it, which could save kernel code if the port
   reuses many of their drivers (4.13). Benefits. Because the bus describes its own devices, the
   block shrinks to memory, cores and the bus controller's address, and the generated header
   cannot disagree with the loader. Impact. Kernel: reads a structure. Compiler: generates the
   header from one grammar. Hardware: none.

ENTRY H.4.8
ORDER: 226   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: 4.8 Starting the devices
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: The INIT bit (the sentences moved). Statement 4.8.1 keeps INIT and
   READY. At boot the ROM runs the device start for the boot disk (4.4), the second stage again
   for the devices it uses, and each kernel driver at attach. Firmware for a device. Some
   devices contain a small processor and need a program, firmware, loaded into them before they
   work; a network or wireless card is the usual case, a graphics device another. The PDP-11 had
   no such device and no way to give it one. On a PC the driver loads the blob from a file at
   attach time (Linux's request_firmware, declared in include/linux/firmware.h; OpenBSD's
   loadfirmware(9), which reads the file from /etc/firmware; NetBSD's firmload(9): all read),
   and the files come in a separate package. The pdp64 proposal is that such a device is given
   its firmware through a descriptor operation (a request on the device's queue, or a descriptor
   giving the address and length of an image in memory, with a small header the device checks:
   identifier, version, length, checksum) once the root file system is available, and that a
   device the boot depends on needs none: the boot disk, the console and the interrupt
   controller work from their built-in logic alone, so that no device waits for firmware that is
   on the disk it controls. (a code example is omitted here; see the manual)
OPTIONS:

      assistant's, not yet the author's.

   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)

   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 4.8.1b A device that wants firmware is given it through a
   descriptor operation once the root file system is available, and a device the boot depends on
   needs no firmware. (That a device is initialised by a register write behind the bus, and may
   be given values or a firmware image, is 4.8.1 in the body.) How the statement reads under
   virtio. 4.8.1 is the PDP-11 form, one INIT bit and one READY bit (errata 29); under virtio
   (errata 95) it is the device_status handshake above. How a virtio device is handed firmware
   is not specified: virtio has no firmware operation of its own, so a request type or a
   separate device would have to be defined (4.13). Commentary. Chain. PDP-11: the CSR's INIT
   bit, nothing more. PC: PCI Express devices initialised through configuration space and
   per-device driver code, firmware blobs loaded from files. VAX: the console and some adapters
   loaded microcode from storage (unverified). pdp64: the virtio handshake, firmware by
   descriptor. Trade-offs. A reset bit and a status register make the simplest device, with one
   failure case, a timeout. A firmware window lets a device be cheap (no flash) and makes an
   update a file replacement, at the cost of a header format and a device that rejects a wrong
   image safely; boot-critical devices are excluded because a device whose firmware is on a disk
   it must itself read cannot start. NVMe has a firmware-download command that needs a working
   queue first (unverified; not found in the QEMU headers read). Impact. Kernel: [... full text
   in Appendix H]

ENTRY H.4.9
ORDER: 228   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: 4.9 Why UEFI is not used
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: Statements 4.9.1 and 4.9.2 stay in the chapter. The details of a
   shim are proposed. What a shim is, and what it is not. The author asked, "not sure why
   firmware and coff are needed for uefi, with virtio native that shuld be minimal, no?" and
   then, "yes, state the shim's real scope." Virtio removes most of the driver mass that a full
   firmware carries (it ships drivers for every disk, network card and screen it might meet; the
   pdp64 has one device interface), and the shim needs only the drivers the second stage has.
   Two things stay, and they are the shim's real scope: 1. PE/COFF. The UEFI specification
   requires a UEFI application to be a PE32+ image (unverified: the specification is not here);
   that is a property of the loader programs, not of the firmware. To run an existing loader
   such as OpenBSD's efiboot it must be built as a PE/COFF image for the pdp64, so the toolchain
   has to produce one, and a machine-type code (a 16-bit number in the image's header that names
   the processor; OpenBSD's arm64 loader uses IMAGE_FILE_MACHINE_ARM64 0xaa64, read) would have
   to be assigned by the owners of the specification (unverified). It need not be a linker
   feature: OpenBSD's arm64 loader is linked as an ordinary ELF file and wrapped as PE32+ by a
   header written by hand in assembler (start.S, 176 lines) plus a self-relocator (self_reloc.c,
   124 lines), with objcopy doing the rest (read, counted). The loader is itself a program that
   must be compiled for the pdp64. 2. A small services layer. [... full text in Appendix H]
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap

ENTRY H.4.10
ORDER: 229   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 4 Boot
   TITLE: 4.10 Recovery
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: What to do when nothing boots. A PDP-11 with a dead disk was not a
   dead machine: ODT let you examine memory and key a bootstrap in (4.1), and V7 shipped
   /boot.bu, a backup of the second stage. The pdp64 keeps the idea and gives it a place. The
   ROM that reads block 0 (4.4) also contains a monitor, a small program that runs on the serial
   console when no boot device answers or when a key is held at power-up and offers a few
   commands: examine and deposit memory and registers, load a program over the serial line,
   start at an address or from a named device, reset a core, reset the machine. It needs a
   working serial port and nothing else, so it also brings up a new board before any boot device
   works. (a code example is omitted here; see the manual) The command letters and the prompt
   are an illustration. A watchdog is a hardware timer that counts down and restarts the machine
   unless software writes to it ("kicks" it) in time; a hung kernel stops kicking and is
   restarted. The PDP-11 had none. PC chipsets carry watchdog timers (QEMU models the ICH9
   chipset's TCO timer in hw/acpi/ich9_tco.c and the Intel 6300ESB watchdog in
   hw/watchdog/wdt_i6300esb.c: read); whether PC firmware arms one is unverified. It needs about
   ten lines in the kernel. decisions-pending.md P7 asks for a bus-controller reset register and
   a watchdog in place of firmware calls, and the monitor's reset command writes that register.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).
