ENTRY H.5.13.2
ORDER: 236   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 5 Drivers and devices
   TITLE: 5.13.2 Until devices have an address-translation unit, a user-space driver can...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 5.13.2 Until devices have an address-translation unit, a user-space
   driver can instruct its device to write anywhere in memory, so it is as trusted as the
   device. In user space . Microkernels and exokernels run drivers as ordinary programs where
   the hardware lets one device be mapped to one process (unverified); the PDP-11's Unibus gave
   every device a fixed address and no protection. Windows of whole pages make that possible
   here (5.13.1), and the IOMMU is what would make it safe: until it exists, a user-space driver
   is as trusted as its device (5.13.2). Linux VFIO and UIO and the DPDK libraries are the same
   idea and rely on an I/O translation unit (unverified). Trade-offs. A driver in user space can
   be tested, restarted and replaced without taking the kernel with it; its cost is a crossing
   for each request and the trust above. The whole-page rule also costs address space and no
   memory, and lets the kernel give one device to one protection domain.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

ENTRY H.8.2.3
ORDER: 241   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 8 Vector work and the accelerator
   TITLE: 8.2.3 A scalable design (ARM SVE, RISC-V V) avoids the fixed-width problem of...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 8.2.3 A scalable design (ARM SVE, RISC-V V) avoids the fixed-width
   problem of 8.3.3, and is the most complex to specify and to implement; it is the only
   in-processor form that would suit this project's range, from a small FPGA core to a possible
   large one. (It is option 3 of J.8.5.) A scalable vector design is a vector machine (8.2) in
   which the register width is not part of the architecture: the hardware has registers of
   whatever length it can build, and a program asks it how many elements it can handle at a time
   and loops until the array is done (strip mining, with the length chosen by the machine and
   not by the programmer). The same binary runs on a small and a large implementation, which a
   fixed-width SIMD set cannot do. The cost is the largest specification in the architecture and
   a compiler model that must handle a length it does not know.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

DECISION: 
NOTE:

================================================================================================
ENTRY H.8.4.3
ORDER: 246   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 8 Vector work and the accelerator
   TITLE: 8.4.3 The interpreter stays on the processor and sends a primitive to the...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 8.4.3 The interpreter stays on the processor and sends a primitive
   to the device when the array is long enough to repay the setup; short arrays run on the
   processor. Commentary . Trade-offs. Offloading a primitive has a fixed cost, so the benefit
   is for arrays above a size threshold that has not been measured; reductions and scans carry a
   dependence along the array that the device must support directly; sorting and gather are
   limited by memory bandwidth. Benefits. The same device that serves the array languages serves
   the element-wise work of inference and the lane-wise work of graphics. Impact. Hardware: a
   streaming engine with a small set of element-wise, reduce, scan and gather units behind the
   ring engine, sized to the memory bandwidth. (The operation set of the engine is the open item
   J.8.1.)
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

DECISION: 
NOTE:

ENTRY H.8.6.3
ORDER: 249   KIND: proposal   CHAPTER: 8 Vector work and the accelerator
   TITLE: 8.6.3 The buffers are uncached, as for every device, so the processor's reads...
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (not instruction semantics)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PROPOSAL AND EXPLANATION: 8.6.3 The buffers are uncached, as for every device, so the
   processor's reads of results are slow; a large result is left in the device's memory or read
   back by the next descriptor. Commentary . This is the same driver skeleton as for a disk
   (chapter 5), with an operation field that means "add these arrays". Trade-offs. The slow
   uncached readback is the cost of the explicit hand-off; an interpreter that needs many small
   results back on the processor should leave them in device memory. Impact. Kernel: one driver
   skeleton. Compiler: none. Hardware: one ring engine shared with every device.
OPTIONS:
   A) Accept as proposed. For: it settles the statement. Against: the proposal is the
      assistant's, not yet the author's.
   B) Reject it. For: removes an unwanted statement. Against: the manual then needs the gap
      filled another way.
   C) Accept with changes (type the changes in NOTE).

ENTRY W.1
ORDER: 302   KIND: standing decision   CHAPTER: Standing decisions
   TITLE: Which first FPGA board
   AFFECTS OPCODES: no (board choice)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ISSUE: No board is named in the author's words. The manual proposes the DE10-Nano first and
   the Zybo Z7-20 or the Kria KV260 when timing limits; on paper estimates four cores with the
   floating-point unit fit only a Kria-class board, and no core has been synthesised. (The other
   choices of the FPGA plan are V.N7.)
OPTIONS:
   A) DE10-Nano (Cyclone V SE, hard Cortex-A9, HDMI, MiSTer ecosystem). For: cheap, popular, a
      hard processor for the harness. Against: smallest logic budget; may not hold the float
      unit with more than one core.
   B) Zybo Z7-20 (Zynq 7020). For: moderate size, hard Cortex-A9. Against: no hard PCI Express
      root complex.
   C) Kria KV260 (Zynq UltraScale+). For: largest budget of the three, has a hard root complex.
      Against: dearest.
   D) ULX3S (open toolchain). For: open tools. Against: loses the hard-core harness.

DECISION:
